PHY121

The Question Book

General Physics II · every question, solved and explained · 13 August 2026

All 253 questions from the lecture slides, the reference sheets and both real tests. Grouped by topic, and within each topic ordered easy first, so the definitions come before the calculations that assume them.

Every question carries the source it came from. Where the same question was set twice it appears once and says where else it was asked. The answer is ticked, and every other option carries a line saying why it fails.

1Charge, Coulomb and the Electric Field

Speed tip. Convert to SI before anything else. mg to kg, µC to C, cm to m. Then ask: one charge or two? One gives a field, two gives a force.

1.1easyReference sheet R.3

Step 1 of the seven-step method is to read the question twice and underline what is asked for. Why does it come first?

  • ✓Because many marks are lost answering a question that was not setCorrect, and it is the reason the step exists.
  • ✗Because it saves time laterWrong. It costs time and buys accuracy.
  • ✗Because the formula cannot be chosen until the units are knownWrong. That describes steps 3 and 4.
  • ✗Because examiners award marks for underliningWrong. The underlining is for you, not the marker.
  • ✗Because it is the only step that does not need a calculatorWrong. That is not a reason for its order.

Answering the wrong question scores zero however perfect the arithmetic. Test 1 question 13 is the standing example: it gives two charges and asks for the field produced by the NEGATIVE one, and using the other charge produces an answer that is on the option list, so the mistake is invisible unless you read carefully.

1.2easyReference sheet R.3

Step 2 is to list what you know with symbols and units, and to write the unknown with a {{1}} mark.

Answer:

A question mark. Writing the unknown down as its own line, with a question mark, is what makes step 4 possible: you cannot choose the formula containing what you know and what you want until both are on the page in front of you.

1.3easyReference sheet R.4

What distinguishes a conductor from an insulator?

  • ✓In a conductor charge moves freely; in an insulator charge is bound and cannot move freelyCorrect. Mobility, not quantity.
  • ✗A conductor has charge and an insulator has noneWrong. Both are full of charge; an insulator's simply cannot travel.
  • ✗A conductor is always metal and an insulator is always plasticWrong. Those are examples, not the definition.
  • ✗A conductor stores charge and an insulator releases itWrong. That describes a capacitor, and inaccurately.
  • ✗An insulator has no electronsWrong. Every atom has electrons, bound ones included.

Whether the charge can MOVE. Both have plenty of charge in them; the difference is mobility. It is why a conductor has no interior field, the free charges rearranging until it cancels, and why an insulator can instead polarise, its bound charges shifting slightly to form dipoles without ever leaving home.

1.4easyReference sheet R.1

What is the value of the Coulomb constant k?

  • ✓8.99 x 109 N m2 C-2Correct. k = 8.99 x 109 N m2 C-2, taken as 9 x 109 in practice.
  • ✗8.85 x 10-12 N m2 C-2Wrong. That is ε₀, the permittivity of free space, in F/m.
  • ✗9.11 x 10-31 N m2 C-2Wrong. That is the electron mass in kilograms.
  • ✗1.602 x 10-19 N m2 C-2Wrong. That is the elementary charge in coulombs.
  • ✗4 π x 10-7 N m2 C-2Wrong. That is μ₀, the permeability of free space, in H/m.

k = 8.99 x 109 N m2 C-2, usually rounded to 9 x 109 in a calculation. It is the constant in Coulomb’s law and in E = kQ/r2, and it is related to the permittivity of free space by k = 1/(4 π ε₀).

1.5easyReference sheet R.1

The Coulomb constant k has the value 8.99 × 10 to the power {{1}} N m² per C².

Answer:

The exponent is positive 9. A common slip is to borrow the negative exponent of ε₀; k is a large number precisely because the electric force is strong.

1.6easyReference sheet R.1

What is the value of the permittivity of free space, ε₀?

  • ✓8.85 x 10-12 F m-1Correct. ε₀ = 8.85 x 10-12 F m-1.
  • ✗8.99 x 109 F m-1Wrong. That is the Coulomb constant k.
  • ✗4 π x 10-7 F m-1Wrong. That is μ₀, the magnetic counterpart.
  • ✗1.602 x 10-19 F m-1Wrong. That is the elementary charge.
  • ✗1.67 x 10-27 F m-1Wrong. That is the proton mass.

ε₀ = 8.85 x 10-12 F m-1. It appears in the parallel plate formula C = ε r ε₀ A/d and in Gauss’s law, and it is tied to k by k = 1/(4 π ε₀).

1.7easyReference sheet R.1

What is the value of the elementary charge e?

  • ✓1.602 x 10-19 CCorrect. e = 1.602 x 10-19 C.
  • ✗1.602 x 1019 CWrong sign on the exponent. A charge that size is impossible for one electron.
  • ✗9.11 x 10-31 CWrong. That is the electron MASS in kilograms, not its charge.
  • ✗1.67 x 10-27 CWrong. That is the proton mass in kilograms.
  • ✗8.85 x 10-12 CWrong. That is ε₀.

e = 1.602 x 10-19 C. Every charge is a whole multiple of it, which is what q = ne means. Its reciprocal is about 6.24 x 1018, which is why a system of roughly 6.2 x 1018 electrons carries close to one coulomb.

1.8easyReference sheet R.1

Which statement is the starting point of all electrostatics?

  • ✓Like charges repel and unlike charges attractCorrect. Opposites attract; the same signs push apart.
  • ✗Like charges attract and unlike charges repelWrong, and exactly reversed.
  • ✗All charges attract, regardless of signWrong. Gravity always attracts; the electric force does not.
  • ✗All charges repel, regardless of signWrong, for the same reason.
  • ✗Charges neither attract nor repel unless they are movingWrong. Electrostatics is the study of charges AT REST exerting forces on each other.

Like repels, unlike attracts. Everything electrostatic starts here, and the sign of the Coulomb force follows from it: a positive product of charges means repulsion, a negative product means attraction.

1.9easyReference sheet R.2

Two electrons placed near each other will attract.

Answer: false

Background, so this is not just a formula

Charge comes in two kinds, and the rule for how they behave is the first thing to fix in your head because everything else leans on it: LIKE charges repel, UNLIKE charges attract. Gravity has no equivalent, since mass only ever attracts, which is why electric forces can hold matter together at all. An atom exists because the negative electrons are pulled towards the positive nucleus while pushing each other apart.

Find: Whether two electrons placed near each other attract or repel

The working, line by line
  1. Identify the sign of each: an electron is NEGATIVE.
  2. Both are negative, so the two charges are LIKE.
  3. Like charges repel, so the statement is FALSE. They repel.
  4. Coulomb's law agrees: the product of two negatives is positive, and a positive force in the convention means repulsion.
Check it

Ask what would happen if it were otherwise. If electrons attracted each other, every electron cloud in every atom would collapse in on itself and matter would not have volume. The fact that you cannot push your hand through a table is the repulsion between electrons, felt at everyday scale.

They repel. Both carry negative charge, so they are like charges, and like charges repel.

1.10easyThe real test 1, question 7

A charge of +1.5 x 10-9 C is placed in a uniform electric field of magnitude 2500 N/C. What is the magnitude of the force acting on the charge?

  • ✗4.0 x 10-6 NWrong. This would need a charge of 1.6 x 10-9 C.
  • ✗1.0 x 10-6 NWrong. This drops the 1.5 and uses the charge as 4 x 10-10 C.
  • ✓3.75 x 10-6 NCorrect. F = qE = 1.5 x 10-9 x 2500 = 3.75 x 10-6 N.
  • ✗2.0 x 10-6 NWrong. This is the answer for a charge of 8 x 10-10 C.
  • ✗3.0 x 10-6 NWrong. This rounds 3.75 down to 3, which the options do not intend.
Background, so this is not just a formula

The electric field is defined as the force felt per unit charge, so it is force divided by charge. Turn that definition around and it becomes a way of finding the force: put a charge in a known field and the force is simply the field times the charge. The field belongs to the region and exists whether or not anything is there to feel it; the force only appears when a charge is placed in it.

Given
  • q = +1.5 × 10⁻⁹ C
  • E = 2500 N C⁻¹ (uniform, so the same everywhere)

Find: The magnitude of the force F on the charge, in newtons

Formula

F = qE

Why this one. ONE charge is named, not two, and a field is given rather than a second charge and a distance. That count decides it: two charges and a separation would need Coulomb's law, but one charge sitting in a ready-made field needs only this. Uniform means E does not change from place to place, so no distance is needed at all.

What each symbol means
  • F = the electric force, in newtons
  • q = the charge placed in the field, in coulombs
  • E = the field strength, in N C⁻¹, which is the same unit as V m⁻¹
The working, line by line
  1. F = q × E
  2. F = 1.5 × 10⁻⁹ × 2500
  3. Write 2500 in standard form: 2.5 × 10³
  4. Front numbers: 1.5 × 2.5 = 3.75
  5. Powers of ten: 10⁻⁹ × 10³ = 10⁻⁶
  6. F = 3.75 × 10⁻⁶ N
Check it

No distance appears anywhere in the question, which is the giveaway that no inverse square is involved. If you found yourself squaring something, you reached for Coulomb's law by reflex. The answer is a few micronewtons, which is right for a nanocoulomb sitting in an ordinary laboratory field.

F = qE, the definition of the electric field as force per unit charge rearranged. F = 1.5 x 10-9 x 2500 = 3.75 x 10-6 N. Nothing else is needed: the field is given, so Coulomb's law does not come into it.

1.11easyReference sheet R.1

Charge is quantised as q = ne, where n must be a {{1}} number.

Answer:

A whole number. Charge comes in coins, not in liquid: you can have 3 electrons or 4, never three and a half. That is why the charge of any object is always an exact multiple of 1.602 x 10-19 C.

1.12easyReference sheet R.3

Step 1 of the method is to read the question twice and underline what is asked for.

Answer: true

True, and the reason is blunt: many marks are lost answering a question that was not set. Test 1 question 13 asks for the field produced by the NEGATIVE charge, and using the other one gives an answer that appears on the option list, so the error is invisible without careful reading.

1.13easyReference sheet R.4

In a conductor charge moves freely, while in an insulator charge is {{1}} and cannot move freely.

Answer:

Bound. Both materials are full of charge; the difference is whether it can travel. That single word explains why a conductor has no interior field, its free charges rearranging until everything cancels, and why an insulator instead polarises, its bound charges shifting slightly into dipoles without leaving home.

1.14easyLecture slides, module 1, slide 60

The S.I. unit of electric field is:

  • ✓NC-1Correct. Force per unit charge.
  • ✗Vm-2Wrong. The other valid form is Vm-1, volts per metre, not per metre squared.
  • ✗CmWrong. Coulomb metre is the unit of DIPOLE MOMENT, p = qd.
  • ✗VmWrong. Volt metre inverts the correct volts per metre.
  • ✗NCWrong. Newton coulomb multiplies where the definition divides.

Read it off the definition. E = F/q, a force in newtons divided by a charge in coulombs, giving newtons per coulomb, NC-1. The slide also derives it the other way, from E = minus dV/dx, a voltage divided by a distance, giving volts per metre, Vm-1. The two are the same unit, which is why a field may be quoted in either N/C or V/m.

1.15easyLecture slides, module 1, slide 68

The magnitude of the electric intensity is represented by E = 3i + 4j, where i and j are unit vectors along the X and Y axes respectively. The Z component of the electric field is:

  • ✓0 Vm-1Correct. No k term means no z component.
  • ✗5 Vm-1Wrong. 5 is the MAGNITUDE of this vector, from the square root of 9 + 16, not its z component.
  • ✗7 Vm-1Wrong. 7 is 3 + 4, added as though the components were plain numbers.
  • ✗1 Vm-1Wrong. Nothing here gives 1.
  • ✗3 Vm-1Wrong. 3 is the x component.

There is no k term in the expression, and k is the unit vector along z. A component that is not written is zero. The whole field lies in the x-y plane. The question looks like it wants a calculation and does not: it is checking that you can read a vector.

1.16mediumReference sheet R.3

Step 3 says convert everything to SI immediately, before anything else is done.

Answer: true

True, and it is one of the two steps that save the most marks. cm to metres, mg to kilograms, μF to farads, all before a single number enters a formula. In this course 5 cm left unconverted costs a factor of 100 once it is squared, and both the wrong answers it produces appear on the option lists.

1.17mediumReference sheet R.3

Step 4 is to choose the formula that contains what you know and what you want. What does the method say to do if no single formula does?

  • ✓You need an intermediate stepCorrect, and it is the standard shape of the harder questions on this paper.
  • ✗Guess the closest optionWrong. The method exists to avoid guessing.
  • ✗The question cannot be answeredWrong. It usually just needs two steps rather than one.
  • ✗Use any formula with the right unitsWrong. Matching units is a check, not a way to choose a formula.
  • ✗Go back and convert againWrong. Converting is step 3 and is already done.

An intermediate step: find some quantity that links the two. Test 2 question 4 is exactly this. No formula takes you from a terminal p.d. straight to the emf, so you compute the current first, I = V/R, and that current is the bridge into E = I(R + r).

1.18mediumReference sheet R.3

Step 5 is to rearrange in {{1}} first, and substitute numbers only at the end.

Answer:

In symbols. Rearranging a formula that already has numbers in it is where sign and reciprocal errors breed. Getting E = mg/q on the page before any digit appears means the only thing left to do is arithmetic.

1.19mediumReference sheet R.3

Step 6 is to compute, keeping track of the powers of ten {{1}} from the front numbers.

Answer:

Separately, in two columns. Multiply the front numbers, then add the exponents. It is what turns a frightening expression like 9.1 x 10-31 × 4 x 106 into two easy operations, and it is why the exponent stops slipping.

1.20mediumReference sheet R.3

Step 7 is to check. Select the three checks the method names.

  • ✓Is the size believable?Correct, and it kills the positive-exponent capacitance in Test 1 question 6 immediately.
  • ✓Does it have the right unit?Correct, and it catches a force answered where a field was asked.
  • ✓Does the sanity check apply, such as parallel being less than the smallest?Correct, and the sanity checks are free.
  • ✗Did you use a calculator?Wrong. Not one of the checks, and a calculator will happily give a wrong answer.
  • ✗Is the answer one of the offered options?Wrong, and dangerous: the examiner deliberately puts the common wrong answers on the list.

Size, unit, sanity check. Together they catch most of what goes wrong: a capacitance of 4.77 farads is not believable, an answer in newtons when the question wanted volts is not the right unit, and a parallel resistance larger than its smallest member is impossible. Steps 3 and 7 are the two that save the most marks and the two most often skipped.

1.21mediumReference sheet R.2

A field points away from a positive charge and {{1}} a negative one.

Answer:

Towards. The memory hook is the fan and the vacuum: a positive charge is a fan blowing outward, a negative charge is a vacuum sucking inward, and the field is which way the air moves. Stand between them and both send you the same way, so their fields ADD. Two fans fight, so they cancel.

1.22mediumReference sheet R.1

The permittivity of free space is written ε₀ and is measured in farads per {{1}}.

Answer:

Farads per metre. Reading the unit is itself a check: a permittivity multiplied by an area over a distance gives a capacitance in farads, which is exactly the parallel plate formula.

1.23mediumReference sheet R.1

The elementary charge is 1.602 × 10 to the power minus {{1}} coulombs.

Answer:

Minus 19. Keep it apart from the electron mass at 10 to the − 31 kg and the proton mass at 10 to the − 27 kg: one is a charge, the others are masses.

1.24mediumReference sheet R.1

The hook for q = ne is that charge comes in coins, not in liquid. What does that mean in practice?

  • ✓A charge is always a whole multiple of e, never a fraction of an electronCorrect. n is a whole number, so a fraction of an electron is not available.
  • ✗Charge can take any value at allWrong, and this is what quantisation denies.
  • ✗Charge can be created and destroyed freelyWrong. That contradicts conservation of charge, a separate property.
  • ✗Charge depends on the speed of the objectWrong. Charge is invariant: it does not depend on speed or reference frame.
  • ✗Charge is a vector quantityWrong. Charge is a scalar; the + and − signs indicate type, not direction.

Quantisation: charge exists only in whole multiples of the elementary charge, so q = ne with n a whole number. You can have 3e or 4e but never 3.5e, exactly as you can have three coins or four but not three and a half.

1.25mediumReference sheet R.2

In q = ne, the letter n must be a {{1}} number.

Answer:

A whole number. That is the whole content of quantisation, and it is why the answer to a charge-from-electrons question is the electron count multiplied by e.

1.26mediumReference sheet R.1

Coulomb’s law is an inverse square law. If the distance between two charges is TRIPLED, what happens to the force?

  • ✓It falls to one ninthCorrect. Three squared is nine, so the force drops to a ninth.
  • ✗It falls to one thirdWrong, and it is the trap: falling to a third is what the POTENTIAL does, because V goes as 1/r rather than 1/r2.
  • ✗It falls to one sixthWrong. No inverse power gives a sixth for a tripling.
  • ✗It becomes three times largerWrong direction. Moving charges apart weakens the force.
  • ✗It is unchangedWrong. Distance is in the formula, so it certainly changes.

F = k q1 q2 / r2, so the force depends on the SQUARE of the distance. Tripling r multiplies r2 by 9, so the force falls to one ninth. The same reasoning applies to the electric field E = kQ/r2, but NOT to the potential V = kQ/r, which would fall only to one third.

1.27mediumReference sheet R.2

Tripling the distance in Coulomb’s law drops the force to one {{1}}.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

Coulomb's law is an INVERSE SQUARE law, and the square is the part that catches people. Move a charge further away and the force does not fall in proportion to the distance; it falls in proportion to the distance squared. The reason is geometric: the influence spreads out over the surface of a sphere, and a sphere's area grows as r², so the same influence is spread over more area and thinned by that same factor.

Find: The word that completes: tripling the distance drops the force to one ___

Formula

F =kQ₁Q₂r²

Why this one. The whole question lives in the r² on the bottom. Because the distance is squared, tripling it multiplies the bottom by 3² = 9, so the force is divided by 9, not by 3.

What each symbol means
  • F = the force, in newtons
  • r = the separation, and it is SQUARED
  • k = the Coulomb constant, unchanged by the move
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is NINTH.
  2. Original force: F ∝ 1 ÷ r²
  3. Triple the distance: the new bottom is (3r)² = 9r²
  4. So the new force is F ÷ 9, one ninth of the original.
  5. Why not third: that is what would happen if the law were 1 ÷ r rather than 1 ÷ r².
  6. Why not sixth or twelfth: neither 6 nor 12 is a square, and an inverse square law can only ever divide by a square number.
Check it

Test the same move on POTENTIAL, which has a plain r rather than r². Tripling the distance would drop the potential to one third, not one ninth. That contrast is worth holding, because examiners set the two questions side by side to see whether you noticed which quantity has the square.

One ninth, because the distance is squared. Compare the potential, which would fall only to one third.

1.28mediumReference sheet R.1

What does the principle of superposition tell you to do, and what does it apply to?

  • ✓Compute each contribution one at a time and then add them, which works for forces, fields and potentialsCorrect, and the fact that it covers all three is what makes it so useful.
  • ✗Compute all contributions at once, which works only for forcesWrong on both counts: the method is one at a time, and it is not restricted to forces.
  • ✗Add the charges first and then apply one formula, which works only for fieldsWrong. Adding the charges first only works in special symmetric cases and is not the principle.
  • ✗Take only the largest contribution and ignore the restWrong. Every contribution counts.
  • ✗Multiply the individual contributions togetherWrong. Contributions add, they do not multiply.

One at a time, then add. Superposition is what lets a many-charge problem be broken into a series of two-charge problems, and it holds for forces, for fields and for potentials alike. Remember that forces and fields are VECTORS, so they add as vectors, while potentials are scalars and add as plain numbers.

1.29mediumReference sheet R.2

Superposition applies to electric forces and fields but not to electric potentials.

Answer: false

It applies to all three. Potentials superpose too, and being scalars they are the easiest of the three to add, since no directions are involved.

1.30mediumThe real test 1, question 3

Calculate the electric field in which a charged oil drops of mass 8 mg and charged +9 μC will be held stationary.

  • ✗5.3 Vm-1Wrong. No route to 5.3 from these numbers.
  • ✗4.35 Vm-1Wrong. This is roughly half the correct value, which is what you get from using 4 mg or double the charge.
  • ✓8.89 Vm-1Correct. E = mg/q = (8 x 10-6 x 10) / (9 x 10-6) = 8.89 Vm-1.
  • ✗7.26 Vm-1Wrong. No route to 7.26 from these numbers.
  • ✗6.62 Vm-1Wrong. No route to 6.62 from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Anything that hangs still is not free of forces; it has forces that cancel. A charged oil drop in an electric field has two: gravity pulling it down, and the electric force pushing it up. When they are equal in size and opposite in direction, the drop hangs. This is the whole of Millikan's famous experiment, which is how the charge on the electron was first measured.

Given
  • m = 8 mg = 8 × 10⁻⁶ kg (milligram, so ÷ 1000 twice)
  • q = +9 µC = 9 × 10⁻⁶ C
  • g = 10 m s⁻² (this examiner uses 10, not 9.8)

Find: The electric field E that holds it stationary, in V m⁻¹

Formula

qE = mg, so E = mg / q

Why this one. The words HELD STATIONARY are the whole instruction. Stationary means the net force is zero, so the upward electric force qE must exactly equal the downward weight mg. Setting them equal and rearranging for E is the entire question. Suspended, in equilibrium and does not fall all mean the same thing.

What each symbol means
  • q = the charge on the drop, in coulombs
  • E = the electric field strength, in V m⁻¹ (the same unit as N C⁻¹)
  • m = the mass of the drop, in kilograms
  • g = the acceleration due to gravity, in m s⁻²
The working, line by line
  1. m = 8 mg = 8 × 10⁻⁶ kg
  2. q = 9 µC = 9 × 10⁻⁶ C
  3. mg = 8 × 10⁻⁶ × 10 = 8 × 10⁻⁵ N
  4. E = mg / q = (8 × 10⁻⁵) / (9 × 10⁻⁶)
  5. The powers of ten: 10⁻⁵ ÷ 10⁻⁶ = 10¹
  6. E = (8 / 9) × 10 = 0.889 × 10 = 8.89 V m⁻¹
Check it

Both quantities were × 10⁻⁶ before g was applied, so the prefixes cancel and only the 10 from g survives. That is why the answer is a clean single-digit number rather than something tiny.

Stationary means the electric force balances the weight, so qE = mg and E = mg/q. Given m = 8 mg = 8 x 10-6 kg and q = 9 x 10-6 C, E = (8 x 10-6 x 10) / (9 x 10-6) = 80 x 10-6 / 9 x 10-6 = 8.89 Vm-1. Note the examiner is using g = 10 ms-2; with g = 9.8 the answer would be 8.71, which is not offered.

1.31mediumThe real test 1, question 10

What is the charge of a system that has 6.2 x 1018 electrons?

  • ✗1.99 CWrong, and about double: this comes from using 3.2 x 10-19 for the electronic charge.
  • ✗0.89 CWrong. A near miss that does not follow from 6.2 x 1018 electrons.
  • ✓0.99 CCorrect. q = ne = 6.2 x 1018 x 1.602 x 10-19 = 0.99 C.
  • ✗0.79 CWrong. A near miss that does not follow from 6.2 x 1018 electrons.
  • ✗1.07 CWrong. This would need about 6.7 x 1018 electrons.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Charge is not continuous; it comes in lumps. Every lump is the charge on one electron, e = 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C, and no smaller amount has ever been found free. That is called QUANTISATION of charge. So the total charge of anything is just how many lumps it has, times the size of one lump.

Given
  • n = 6.2 × 10¹⁸ electrons
  • e = 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C (the elementary charge)

Find: The total charge Q, in coulombs

Formula

Q = n e

Why this one. The question gives a COUNT of electrons and asks for a charge, so the two are linked by the size of one electron's charge. Nothing else in the question is needed, and no distances or fields appear.

What each symbol means
  • Q = the total charge, in coulombs
  • n = the number of elementary charges, a pure count with no unit
  • e = the elementary charge, 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹ C
The working, line by line
  1. Q = n × e
  2. Q = 6.2 × 10¹⁸ × 1.602 × 10⁻¹⁹
  3. Front numbers: 6.2 × 1.602 = 9.93
  4. Powers of ten: 10¹⁸ × 10⁻¹⁹ = 10⁻¹
  5. Q = 9.93 × 10⁻¹ = 0.993 ≈ 0.99 C
Check it

1 ÷ e is about 6.24 × 10¹⁸, so 6.2 × 10¹⁸ electrons is very nearly exactly one coulomb. An answer just under 1 C is what you should expect, and anything far from 1 means a slipped power of ten.

Charge is quantised: q = ne, where e = 1.602 x 10-19 C. So q = 6.2 x 1018 x 1.602 x 10-19 = 0.993 C, which rounds to 0.99 C. The number of electrons is close to 1/e, which is why the answer sits just under 1 coulomb.

1.32mediumThe real test 1, question 13

A charge of 8 x 10-8 C is 0.40 m from a charge of -2.0 x 10-8 C. Determine the electric field produced by the negative charge at the location of the positive charge.

  • ✗1114.07 N/CWrong. A near miss designed to catch a rounding slip in the division.
  • ✓1125.75 N/CCorrect. E = kQ/r2 using only the negative charge: 9 x 109 x 2 x 10-8 / 0.16 = 1125 N/C.
  • ✗1523.23 N/CWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✗3124.76 N/CWrong. This is close to four times the answer, which is what using the 8 x 10-8 C charge instead would give.
  • ✗2314.56 N/CWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A field is created by a charge and exists in the space around it whether or not anything is there to feel it. So when a question asks for the field PRODUCED BY one charge AT the position of another, the second charge is only a place marker. It tells you where to stand and nothing more. Its own size never enters, because a charge does not produce a field on itself.

Given
  • The field-producing charge: Q = 2.0 × 10⁻⁸ C, negative
  • r = 0.40 m
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²
  • The 8 × 10⁻⁸ C charge is only the LOCATION, and is not used

Find: The electric field E at the position of the positive charge, in N C⁻¹

Formula

E =kQr²

Why this one. Only the charge that PRODUCES the field goes into the formula. Reading the question decides which one that is: it says the field produced by the negative charge, so Q is 2.0 × 10⁻⁸ C. Had it asked for the FORCE between them, both charges would have been needed and Coulomb's law would apply instead. Field means one charge, force means two.

What each symbol means
  • E = the field strength at that point, in N C⁻¹
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²
  • Q = the charge producing the field, in coulombs
  • r = the distance from the producing charge to the point, and it is SQUARED
The working, line by line
  1. Square the distance first: r² = 0.40² = 0.16 m²
  2. Top: kQ = 9 × 10⁹ × 2.0 × 10⁻⁸
  3. Front numbers: 9 × 2 = 18
  4. Powers of ten: 10⁹ × 10⁻⁸ = 10¹
  5. Top = 18 × 10¹ = 180
  6. E = 180 ÷ 0.16
  7. E ≈ 1125 N C⁻¹, and the direction is TOWARDS the negative charge
Check it

Two habits protect this mark. First, if you find yourself using both charges, re-read the question, because it says produced BY the negative charge. Second, r² = 0.16 and not 0.4, which is the slip that would give 450 and appears nowhere in the options. The direction is towards the negative charge, since field lines always end on negative charge.

Read the question carefully: it asks for the field produced by the NEGATIVE charge, so only that charge and the separation are used. The 8 x 10-8 C charge is where you are measuring, not what is producing. E = kQ/r2 = 9 x 109 x 2 x 10-8 / (0.40)2 = 180 / 0.16 = 1125 N/C, which the examiner gives as 1125.75.

1.33mediumReference sheet R.1

Coulomb's law is an inverse square law, so tripling the distance between two charges drops the force to a ninth.

Answer: true

True. F =k q1 q2r², and three squared is nine. It is the standard sanity check on any force answer: doubling the distance quarters the force, halving it quadruples the force. The same square appears in the FIELD but not in the potential.

1.34mediumReference sheet R.1

The electric field E = F/q may be quoted in newtons per coulomb or, equivalently, in volts per {{1}}.

Answer:

Volts per metre. The two units are the same thing seen from two definitions: E = F/q gives N/C, and E = V/d gives V/m. Test 1 uses Vm-1 while Test 2 uses N/C, so both must be recognised on sight.

1.35mediumReference sheet R.3

Step 5 says to substitute the numbers first and then rearrange the formula.

Answer: false

The other way round. Rearrange in SYMBOLS first, and substitute numbers only at the end. Rearranging an expression that already contains numbers is where reciprocal and sign errors breed: get E = mg/q on the page before a single digit appears, and all that remains is arithmetic.

1.36mediumReference sheet R.3

Step 7 asks three things: is the size believable, is the {{1}} right, and does the sanity check apply?

Answer:

The unit. Together with size and the sanity checks it catches most of what goes wrong: a capacitance of 4.77 farads is not believable, an answer in newtons where volts were wanted has the wrong unit, and a parallel resistance above its smallest member is impossible. Steps 3 and 7 save the most marks and are skipped the most often.

1.37mediumReference sheet R.2

Using the fan and vacuum picture, a positive charge is a fan blowing outward and a negative charge is a vacuum sucking inward. What happens midway between one of each?

  • ✓Both send you the same way, so the two fields ADDCorrect, and it is why the answer doubles rather than vanishing.
  • ✗They push you in opposite directions, so the fields cancelWrong. That is what two charges of the SAME sign would do.
  • ✗Neither has any effect at the midpointWrong. Both act at the midpoint; they simply act the same way.
  • ✗The fan wins because it is positiveWrong. Equal magnitudes at equal distances give equal contributions.
  • ✗The field points at right angles to the line joining themWrong. Both contributions lie along the line joining the charges.

The fan blows you away from itself, to the right, and the vacuum pulls you towards itself, also to the right. Same direction, so they add, which is why Test 1 question 8 comes to 2.16 x 107 rather than zero. Two FANS would fight and cancel. Opposite signs add, same signs cancel, and most people guess that backwards.

1.38mediumLecture slides, module 1, slide 57

What force would a 1.4 x 10-5 C negative charge experience at 1 m distance from a 1 C positive charge, and would the force be directed toward or away from the 1 C charge?

  • ✓1.26 x 105 N, directed toward the 1 C chargeCorrect. 1.26 x 105 N, and attraction because the signs are opposite.
  • ✗1.26 x 105 N, directed away from the 1 C chargeWrong on direction only. Opposite charges attract, so the force points toward, not away.
  • ✗1.4 x 10-5 N, directed toward the 1 C chargeWrong. This is the charge itself copied out, with no force calculated.
  • ✗9 x 109 N, directed away from the 1 C chargeWrong. That is Coulomb's constant, not a force, and the direction is wrong too.
  • ✗1.26 x 10-5 N, directed toward the 1 C chargeWrong by ten orders of magnitude: the exponent should be positive, since 9 x 109 × 1.4 x 10-5 is 1.26 x 105.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Coulomb's law works in exactly the same shape as gravity, but with one extra freedom: charges can push as well as pull. Like signs repel, unlike signs attract, and the size of the force does not care which is which. So the routine is always two separate jobs: get the size from the formula, then get the direction from the signs.

Given
  • Q₁ = 1 C, positive
  • Q₂ = 1.4 × 10⁻⁵ C, negative
  • r = 1 m
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²

Find: The size of the force on the negative charge, and its direction

Formula

F =kQ₁Q₂r²

Why this one. Two charges and a separation are named, which is the signature of Coulomb's law rather than F = qE. The signs are deliberately left out of the arithmetic: putting a minus into the formula gives a negative force, which is meaningless as a magnitude. Handle the sign in words instead.

What each symbol means
  • F = the electrostatic force, in newtons
  • k = the Coulomb constant, 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²
  • Q₁, Q₂ = the sizes of the two charges, in coulombs
  • r = their separation, in metres, and it is SQUARED
The working, line by line
  1. F = (9 × 10⁹ × 1 × 1.4 × 10⁻⁵) ÷ 1²
  2. r² = 1² = 1, so the bottom does nothing here
  3. Front numbers: 9 × 1.4 = 12.6
  4. Powers of ten: 10⁹ × 10⁻⁵ = 10⁴
  5. F = 12.6 × 10⁴ = 1.26 × 10⁵ N
  6. Direction: one charge is positive and the other negative, so they ATTRACT
  7. The force on the negative charge points TOWARDS the 1 C charge
Check it

Four of the five options carry a plausible-looking number, so the direction is doing half the work of this question. Read the signs before you read the numbers: unlike signs means towards, like signs means away. Note also how large 1.26 × 10⁵ N is, about the weight of a lorry, which is why a whole coulomb of charge is never found sitting still in one place.

Coulomb's law needs BOTH charges, since a force acts between two objects. F = k q1 q2 over r² = (9 x 109 x 1 x 1.4 x 10-5) / 1² = 1.26 x 105 N. Then the direction, decided by the signs and not by the arithmetic: the charges are opposite, so they attract, and the force on the negative charge points TOWARD the 1 C positive charge. Note how large the answer is. A whole coulomb is an enormous charge, which is why real problems use μC.

1.39mediumLecture slides, module 1, slide 64

If F be the electrostatic force between two charges in air, then the force between the charges when air is replaced by a medium of dielectric constant K is:

  • ✓F/KCorrect. The dielectric screens the charges from each other, so the force falls, and it falls by exactly K because ε sits on the bottom of Coulomb's law.
  • ✗F/√KWrong. The direction is right, the force does fall, but nothing here takes a square root. K divides the force once, not by √K.
  • ✗√K FWrong twice: it makes the force BIGGER, and it uses a square root that appears nowhere in Coulomb's law.
  • ✗KFWrong, and it is the trap. K multiplying the force is what happens to CAPACITANCE, where ε sits on top. Here it sits underneath, so K divides instead.
  • ✗F, unchangedWrong. Something physical changed in the gap: the molecules polarise and screen the charges, so the force cannot be unaffected.

Picture it first, then the formula. Two charges sit in air, a certain distance apart, pulling on each other with force F. Now slide a slab of glass or plastic into the gap between them. Nothing else changes: same charges, same distance. The slab is made of molecules, and each molecule has a positive end and a negative end. Put them near a charge and they all TURN, lining up with their opposite ends facing it. That is called POLARISATION, and the effect is a thin layer of opposite charge sitting between the two originals, partly hiding each one from the other. Each charge now sees less of its partner than it did through air. So the force goes DOWN. That alone rules out three of the five options. By how much? By exactly K, the dielectric constant of the slab. F becomes F/K. Glass has K around 5, so the force drops to about a fifth. The formula agrees, and here is the one line that makes it obvious. In F =q₁q₂4πεr², the permittivity ε sits UNDERNEATH. Adding the slab multiplies ε by K. Multiplying the bottom of a fraction by K divides the whole thing by K. So F becomes F/K. The contrast worth carrying: in capacitance, C = εA/d, that same ε sits ON TOP, so the same slab MULTIPLIES the capacitance by K instead. Same material, same K, opposite effect, purely because of which side of the fraction ε is on.

1.40mediumLecture slides, module 1, slide 66

The magnitude of the electric intensity represented by E = 3i + 4j - 2k, where i, j and k are unit vectors along the x, y and z axes respectively, is:

  • ✓5.4 Vm-1Correct. The square root of 29.
  • ✗5 Vm-1Wrong, and it is the trap: 5 is the answer if you drop the k component and use only 3 and 4, the familiar 3-4-5 triangle.
  • ✗9 Vm-1Wrong. 9 is 3², one term of the sum rather than the result.
  • ✗81 Vm-1Wrong. 81 is 9², squaring something that was already a square.
  • ✗29 Vm-1Wrong. 29 is the sum of the squares, before taking the square root.

The magnitude of a vector is the square root of the sum of the squares of its components. E = square root of (3² + 4² + minus 2²) = square root of (9 + 16 + 4) = square root of 29 = 5.39, which rounds to 5.4 Vm-1. Note that − 2² is PLUS 4: squaring destroys the sign, which is why a magnitude can never come out negative.

1.41hardReference sheet R.1

The magnitude bars in E = k|Q|/r² mean you drop the sign of the charge, because the formula returns the SIZE of the field while the sign fixes its direction instead.

Answer: true

True. Feed a negative Q into the arithmetic and you get a negative field strength, which means nothing: a field has a direction, not a negative size. So use the magnitude to get the number, then use the sign separately to decide the direction, away from a positive charge and towards a negative one.

1.42hardThe real test 1, question 8

Two charges Q1 = +3.0 x 10-6 C and Q2 = -3.0x10-6 C, are placed 0.10 m apart in air. Find the net electric field at the midpoint between the charges.

  • ✗1.99 x107 N/CWrong. No route to 1.99 from these numbers.
  • ✗1.08 x 107 N/CWrong. This is the field from ONE charge only, and it is the trap: the two contributions add rather than cancel because the charges are opposite in sign.
  • ✓2.16 x 107 N/CCorrect. Two equal contributions of 1.08 x 107 N/C pointing the same way, so 2.16 x 107 N/C.
  • ✗3.0 x 107 N/CWrong. This would need a separation smaller than the one given.
  • ✗1.07 x 107 N/CWrong, and it is a near miss on the single-charge value, which is itself only half the answer.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Two equal and opposite charges a short distance apart make a dipole, the arrangement at the heart of every polar molecule. The field lines leave the positive charge and land on the negative one, so between the two charges they all point the same way, from plus to minus. That is why the two fields at the midpoint REINFORCE instead of cancelling, which is the exact opposite of what most people guess on first reading.

Given
  • Q₁ = +3.0 × 10⁻⁶ C
  • Q₂ = −3.0 × 10⁻⁶ C
  • separation = 0.10 m, so the midpoint is at r = 0.05 m from EACH charge
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²

Find: The net electric field at the midpoint, in N C⁻¹

Formula

E =kQr², then add the two as vectors

Why this one. This is a FIELD question, so r is squared, and there are two sources, so the two fields must be combined by direction rather than simply added as numbers. Here the directions happen to coincide, so the combination reduces to doubling. Getting the direction right before the arithmetic is what makes this a hard question rather than an easy one.

What each symbol means
  • E = the field due to one charge, in N C⁻¹
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²
  • Q = the size of that charge, sign handled by the direction argument
  • r = the distance from that charge to the point, and it is SQUARED
  • r = 0.05 m here, HALF the separation, not the whole 0.10 m
The working, line by line
  1. Distance from each charge to the midpoint: r = 0.10 ÷ 2 = 0.05 m
  2. Square it once, for both: r² = 0.05² = 0.0025 m²
  3. Field from the positive charge, pointing away from it, so towards Q₂:
  4. E₁ = (9 × 10⁹ × 3.0 × 10⁻⁶) ÷ 0.0025
  5. Top: 9 × 3 = 27, and 10⁹ × 10⁻⁶ = 10³, so top = 27 × 10³ = 2.7 × 10⁴
  6. E₁ = (2.7 × 10⁴) ÷ 0.0025 = 1.08 × 10⁷ N C⁻¹
  7. Field from the negative charge, pointing TOWARDS it, which is the same direction:
  8. E₂ = 1.08 × 10⁷ N C⁻¹, identical in size because the charges are equal
  9. Same direction, so ADD: E = 1.08 × 10⁷ + 1.08 × 10⁷
  10. E = 2.16 × 10⁷ N C⁻¹, directed from the positive charge towards the negative
Check it

The two traps are both in the option list. 1.08 × 10⁷ is what you get by finding one field and forgetting to add the second, and using the full 0.10 m instead of the half would divide the answer by four. Remember the rule: between UNLIKE charges the fields add, between LIKE charges they cancel and the midpoint field is zero.

The midpoint is 0.05 m from each charge. Each contributes E = kQ/r2 = 9 x 109 x 3 x 10-6 / (0.05)2 = 27000 / 0.0025 = 1.08 x 107 N/C. The field points AWAY from the positive charge and TOWARDS the negative one, so at the midpoint both contributions point the same way and they add: 2 x 1.08 x 107 = 2.16 x 107 N/C.

1.43hardThe real test 1, question 9

Calculate the magnitude of the electric field intensity represented by

  • ✗10.2 Vm-1Cannot be judged. The figure the stem depends on is missing from the captured paper.
  • ✗12 Vm-1Cannot be judged. The figure the stem depends on is missing from the captured paper.
  • ✓10 Vm-1This is the option selected in the sitting, and the examiner marked it WRONG. It is recorded here only to keep the paper complete.
  • ✗9 Vm-1Cannot be judged. The figure the stem depends on is missing from the captured paper.
  • ✗8 Vm-1Cannot be judged. The figure the stem depends on is missing from the captured paper.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A field has a direction as well as a size, so it is a VECTOR. Writing it as 3i + 4j − 2k is just saying how far it reaches along three perpendicular directions at once: i is along x, j along y, k along z. Asking for the magnitude is asking how long that arrow is overall, which is Pythagoras extended into three dimensions.

Given
  • E = 3i + 4j − 2k, where i, j and k are the unit vectors along x, y and z

Find: The magnitude |E|, a single number with no direction

Formula

|E| = √(x² + y² + z²)

Why this one. The components are at right angles to each other, so they combine by Pythagoras rather than by simple addition. In two dimensions 3 and 4 give 5; the third component just adds one more square under the root.

What each symbol means
  • |E| = the magnitude, always positive
  • x, y, z = the components along i, j and k
The working, line by line
  1. Square each component: 3² = 9, 4² = 16, (−2)² = 4
  2. Add them: 9 + 16 + 4 = 29
  3. Take the square root: |E| = √29
  4. |E| = 5.39
Check it

A magnitude is always LARGER than the biggest single component and SMALLER than their sum. Here 4 < 5.39 < 9. If your answer fails that, you added instead of squaring. Note the minus vanished when squared, which is why a magnitude can never be negative.

QUOTED AS PRINTED, AND INCOMPLETE. The stem ends at "represented by" and the figure it refers to is not in the captured paper, so the question cannot be worked from what survives. This is also the one question on Test 1 marked 0 out of 1: the option selected in the sitting was 10 Vm-1, and the examiner marked it wrong, so the true key is not recoverable from the capture either. The answer is set to the selected option only so the paper stays answerable end to end; treat this question as evidence that a figure was lost, not as a fact to learn. Everything the question tests, E = V/d and reading a field from a diagram, is drilled properly elsewhere in this topic.

1.44hardReference sheet R.1

Why does the field formula take the MAGNITUDE of the charge, written E = k|Q|/r²?

  • ✓Because the formula returns the size of the field, while the sign is used separately to fix its directionCorrect. Size from the magnitude, direction from the sign, in two separate steps.
  • ✗Because charges are always positiveWrong. Charges are routinely negative, and Test 1 question 13 uses one.
  • ✗Because the field of a negative charge is zeroWrong. A negative charge has a perfectly ordinary field; it simply points inward.
  • ✗Because the bars mean the same as bracketsWrong. Bars mean absolute value, brackets merely group.
  • ✗Because r² is always positiveWrong. That is true but has nothing to do with why the bars are on the Q.

A field has a direction, not a negative size. Feeding a negative Q into the arithmetic would give a negative field strength, which means nothing. So the magnitude gives the number, and the sign is spent afterwards on the direction: away from a positive charge, towards a negative one.

1.45hardLecture slides, module 1, slide 58

Point charge A has a charge of -1.0 nC and point charge B has a charge of 4.0 nC. They are separated by 1.0 cm. What are the magnitude and direction of the electric forces on charges A and B?

  • ✓3.6 x 10-4 N on each, attractive, equal and oppositeCorrect, and the equal-and-opposite part is the physics being tested.
  • ✗3.6 x 10-4 N on A but 1.4 x 10-3 N on B, because B has more chargeWrong, and it is the trap. A bigger charge does not feel a bigger force: q1 and q2 appear as a product, so both charges see the same number.
  • ✗3.6 x 10-6 N on each, attractiveWrong by a factor of 100, which is what leaving the separation as 1.0 cm rather than 0.010 m does.
  • ✗3.6 x 10-4 N on each, repulsiveWrong on direction. One charge is negative and the other positive, so they attract.
  • ✗3.6 x 10-2 N on each, attractiveWrong by a factor of 100 the other way.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Newton's third law does not stop being true for electric forces. Whatever A does to B, B does back to A with equal size and opposite direction, no matter how unequal the two charges are. This surprises people, because a 4 nC charge feels like it ought to push harder than a 1 nC one. It does not, and the formula shows why: both charges appear in the SAME product, so there is only one force size to be had.

Given
  • Q_A = 1.0 nC = 1.0 × 10⁻⁹ C, negative
  • Q_B = 4.0 nC = 4.0 × 10⁻⁹ C, positive
  • r = 1.0 cm = 0.01 m
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²

Find: The size and direction of the force on A, and the force on B

Formula

F =kQ₁Q₂r²

Why this one. Two charges and a separation again, so Coulomb's law. The important reading is that the formula produces ONE number, not one per charge. That single number is the force each feels, which is Newton's third law falling out of the algebra rather than being quoted separately.

What each symbol means
  • F = the force each charge feels, in newtons
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²
  • Q₁, Q₂ = the two charges, here 1.0 nC and 4.0 nC, in coulombs
  • r = their separation, in metres, SQUARED
  • 1 nC = 10⁻⁹ C, and 1 cm = 0.01 m
The working, line by line
  1. Convert first: 1.0 nC = 1.0 × 10⁻⁹ C, 4.0 nC = 4.0 × 10⁻⁹ C, 1.0 cm = 0.01 m
  2. Square the distance: r² = 0.01² = 1 × 10⁻⁴ m²
  3. Top: 9 × 10⁹ × 1.0 × 10⁻⁹ × 4.0 × 10⁻⁹
  4. Front numbers: 9 × 1 × 4 = 36
  5. Powers of ten: 10⁹ × 10⁻⁹ × 10⁻⁹ = 10⁻⁹
  6. Top = 36 × 10⁻⁹ = 3.6 × 10⁻⁸
  7. F = (3.6 × 10⁻⁸) ÷ (1 × 10⁻⁴) = 3.6 × 10⁻⁴ N
  8. Direction: one negative, one positive, so ATTRACTIVE, each pulled towards the other
Check it

The wrong option that says B feels more because it has more charge is the exact misconception this question exists to catch. Squash it with the formula: Q_A and Q_B are multiplied together, so swapping which charge you call which changes nothing. Watch the centimetre too, since 1 cm squared is 10⁻⁴ and not 10⁻², and that slip moves the answer by two powers of ten straight into another option.

Convert first: 1.0 cm = 0.010 m. Then F = k q1 q2 over r² = (9 x 109 x 1.0 x 10-9 x 4.0 x 10-9) / (0.010)² = 3.6 x 10-8 / 1.0 x 10-4 = 3.6 x 10-4 N. The signs are opposite so the force is attractive, and by Newton's third law the two charges feel forces of EQUAL size in opposite directions, however unequal the charges themselves are.

1.46hardLecture slides, module 1, slide 62

The force on an electron of charge q placed at a point in an electric field where the electric intensity is E, is given by F =

  • ✓qE opposite to the direction of the fieldCorrect. The size is qE and the electron's negative charge reverses the direction.
  • ✗qE in the direction of the fieldWrong on direction, and it is the trap: this is the right answer for a POSITIVE charge, not an electron.
  • ✗E/q in the direction of the fieldWrong on both. E/q inverts the formula, and the direction is wrong for an electron.
  • ✗E/q opposite to the direction of the fieldWrong size. The direction is right but E/q is upside down.
  • ✗qE at right angles to the fieldWrong. A right-angle force is the MAGNETIC case, F = qvB, not the electric one.

Two separate things to get right. The SIZE comes from F = qE, the definition of the field as force per unit charge rearranged. The DIRECTION comes from the sign of the charge, and the question says ELECTRON, which is negative. The field points the way a POSITIVE charge would be pushed, so a negative charge is pushed the opposite way. Hence qE, opposite to the field.

1.47hardLecture slides, module 1, slide 69

The electric field in which a charged oil drop of mass 5 mg and charge +10 μC be held stationary is:

  • ✓5 Vm-1 upwardsCorrect. 5 Vm-1, and upwards because a positive charge must be pushed up against its weight.
  • ✗5 Vm-1 downwardsWrong direction. A downward field would push a positive charge down, adding to the weight rather than balancing it.
  • ✗5000 Vm-1 upwardsWrong by a factor of 1000, which is what forgetting to convert mg to kilograms does.
  • ✗5000 Vm-1 downwardsWrong on both the size and the direction.
  • ✗50 Vm-1 upwardsWrong by a factor of ten in the exponent arithmetic.

Stationary means the upward electric force balances the downward weight: qE = mg, so E = mg/q. Convert first, because both units are prefixed: 5 mg = 5 x 10-6 kg and 10 μC = 10 x 10-6 C. Then E = (5 x 10-6 x 10) / (10 x 10-6) = 5 x 10-5 / 1 x 10-5 = 5 Vm-1. The direction must be UPWARDS, because the charge is positive and the force on a positive charge follows the field, and that force has to point up to hold the weight. This is the same question as Test 1 question 3 with different numbers.

1.48hardLecture slides, module 1, slide 55

A charge S of +8 μC feels a force of 320 N upwards from a charge P, and 120 N to the left from a charge R, the two being at right angles. What is the net force on S?

  • ✓341.76 N at 69.44° to the 120 N forceCorrect. Pythagoras for the size, then the arctangent for the angle.
  • ✗440 N at 45°Wrong. 440 N is 320 + 120, adding the sizes as if they pointed the same way. They are at right angles.
  • ✗200 N at 69.44°Wrong. 200 N is 320 − 120, subtracting as if they opposed.
  • ✗341.76 N at 20.56° to the 120 N forceWrong angle. 20.56° is the angle to the OTHER force, since the two angles add to 90.
  • ✗116800 N at 69.44°Wrong. 116800 is the sum of the squares, before taking the square root.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Forces add as arrows, not as numbers. If two forces pull in different directions you cannot simply total them, because part of each is spent pulling across the other. When the two happen to be at RIGHT ANGLES the arithmetic is at its kindest: neither has any component along the other, so the resultant is just the diagonal of the rectangle they make, which is Pythagoras.

Given
  • F₁ = 320 N, directed upwards (from charge P)
  • F₂ = 120 N, directed to the left (from charge R)
  • The angle between them is 90°
  • The +8 µC on S is scene-setting and is NOT used

Find: The resultant force on S: its size, and the angle it makes

Formula

F = √(F₁² + F₂²), tan θ = F₁ / F₂

Why this one. Two perpendicular forces are being combined, and perpendicular means Pythagoras. The angle comes from the tangent, which is opposite over adjacent, so which force you put on top decides which angle you get. The question asks for the angle to the 120 N force, so the 320 N is the opposite side and goes on top.

What each symbol means
  • F = the resultant, the single force that would do the same job as both
  • F₁ = 320 N, the upward force
  • F₂ = 120 N, the leftward force
  • θ = the angle measured from the 120 N force round to the resultant
The working, line by line
  1. Square each force: 320² = 102400, 120² = 14400
  2. Add: 102400 + 14400 = 116800
  3. Square root: F = √116800 = 341.76 N
  4. Now the angle: tan θ = 320 ÷ 120 = 2.667
  5. θ = tan⁻¹(2.667) = 69.44°, measured from the 120 N force
Check it

116800 is sitting in the option list as an answer, which is what you get by stopping before the square root, so finish the job. And sanity-check the angle by eye: the 320 N force is much the bigger of the two, so the resultant must lean towards it, meaning an angle well over 45° from the 120 N. 69.44° leans the right way; the option offering 20.56° is the same angle measured from the wrong force.

Forces add as VECTORS, not as numbers. At right angles that means Pythagoras for the size: F net = square root of (320 squared + 120 squared) = square root of (102400 + 14400) = square root of 116800 = 341.76 N. Then the angle by trigonometry: tan θ = 320 over 120 = 2.67, so θ = 69.44°, measured from the 120 N force. That is superposition: work out each force on its own, then combine.

2Electric Potential and Energy

Speed tip. Potential has a plain r, field has r squared. Four times further out makes the potential a quarter and the field a sixteenth.

2.1easyReference sheet R.4

An equipotential is a surface on which the potential is the same everywhere. Which of these follows?

  • ✓No work is done moving a charge along it, and field lines cross it at right anglesCorrect on both counts.
  • ✗Work is done moving a charge along it, proportional to the distanceWrong. No potential difference means no work, however far you go.
  • ✗Field lines run parallel to itWrong, and exactly backwards: they are perpendicular.
  • ✗Two equipotential surfaces may intersectWrong. Intersecting would give one point two potentials.
  • ✗The field is zero everywhere on itWrong. The surface of a charged sphere is an equipotential and the field there is certainly not zero.

W = qV, and along an equipotential there is no CHANGE in V, so no work is done. Field lines are always perpendicular to equipotentials, because any component of field along the surface would mean a potential difference along it, which would contradict it being equipotential. And they never intersect, since a point cannot hold two different potentials at once.

2.2easyReference sheet R.1

The work done moving a charge through a potential difference is W = qV, charge times voltage.

Answer: true

True, and it is simply the definition of potential difference turned around: a volt IS a joule per coulomb, so multiplying coulombs by volts returns joules. It is the reason no work is done along an equipotential, where V does not change at all.

2.3easyReference sheet R.4

An equipotential is a surface on which the potential is the same everywhere, so moving a charge along it does {{1}} work.

Answer:

No work at all. W = qV depends on the CHANGE in potential, and along an equipotential there is none. Field lines always cross such a surface at right angles, because any component along it would create a potential difference and contradict the definition.

2.4mediumReference sheet R.1

Electric potential is V =kQr. Unlike the field, the r here is to the power {{1}}.

Answer:

One. V = kQ/r has a plain r, while E = kQ/r². Move three times further away and the FIELD falls to a ninth but the POTENTIAL only to a third. Test 1 question 4 depends on this: 5 cm to 20 cm is four times the distance, and the potential falls from 720 kV to 180 kV, exactly a quarter.

2.5mediumReference sheet R.1

The electric potential energy of two charges is:

  • ✓U = kQq/r, which also equals qVCorrect, and the qV form is the one that makes W = qV obvious.
  • ✗U = kQq/r²Wrong. r² belongs to the FORCE and the FIELD, not to energy or potential.
  • ✗U = kQ/rWrong. That is the potential V, which is energy per unit charge, not the energy itself.
  • ✗U = qEWrong. qE is a FORCE in newtons, not an energy in joules.
  • ✗U = kQq rWrong. r divides here, it does not multiply.

U = kQq/r, in joules, and since V = kQ/r it can be written more simply as U = qV: the energy is the charge times the potential it sits in. Note the plain r, matching the potential rather than the field.

2.6mediumReference sheet R.1

A charge of 3 C moves through a potential difference of 12 V. Calculate the work done.

  • ✓36 JCorrect. 3 coulombs each carried through 12 volts.
  • ✗4 JWrong, and upside down: 12 divided by 3.
  • ✗0.25 JWrong. 3 divided by 12.
  • ✗15 JWrong. This ADDS the two numbers.
  • ✗9 JWrong. No route to 9 from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Potential difference is not really about volts as a substance; it is a statement about ENERGY PER COULOMB. Saying a battery is 12 V is saying that each coulomb passing through it gains 12 joules. So the work done on any amount of charge is just how many coulombs there are, times how many joules each one gets.

Given
  • Q = 3 C
  • V = 12 V

Find: The work done W, in joules

Formula

W = QV

Why this one. This comes straight from the definition of the volt: one volt is one joule per coulomb. Multiply the volts by the coulombs and the coulombs cancel, leaving joules. Nothing else is needed, and in particular no distance or field, because a potential difference already has the distance built into it.

What each symbol means
  • W = the work done, or the energy transferred, in joules
  • Q = the charge moved, in coulombs
  • V = the potential difference it moves through, in volts
The working, line by line
  1. W = Q × V
  2. W = 3 × 12
  3. W = 36 J
Check it

Check by units, which is faster than re-doing the arithmetic. Coulombs × (joules per coulomb) = joules, so the units work out and the multiplication was the right operation. Had you divided you would have got 0.25, which is sitting in the option list waiting for exactly that mistake.

W = qV = 3 x 12 = 36 J. That is the definition of potential difference rearranged: a volt is a joule per coulomb, so multiplying coulombs by volts gives joules. This is a class question from the Module 1 slides on potential.

2.7mediumReference sheet R.1

Between parallel plates the field is uniform, and E = V over {{1}}, where that symbol is the separation of the plates.

Answer:

E = V/d. More generally E = minus dV/dr, the field being the negative gradient of the potential, but for the uniform field between plates it simplifies to voltage divided by separation. That is also why volts per metre is a valid unit for a field.

2.8mediumReference sheet R.2

The electric field is a vector, but the electric potential is a {{1}}.

Answer:

A scalar. It has magnitude and sign but no direction, which is why potentials from several charges are added as ordinary signed numbers.

2.9mediumThe real test 1, question 1

Find the work done on a charge of +6.0 μC placed in vacuum as it moves through a distance of 50 cm.

  • ✗2 x 10-6 JWrong. This is q x d with the distance left as 1/3 m rather than 0.50 m.
  • ✗1.08 x 10-6 JWrong. No route to this value from a 6.0 μC charge over 0.50 m.
  • ✗0.5 x 10-6 JWrong, and it is the trap: this is the DISTANCE in metres multiplied by 10-6, not the work.
  • ✗0.65 x 10-6 JWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✓3 x 10-6 JCorrect. W = qEd = 6.0 x 10-6 x 1 x 0.50 = 3 x 10-6 J.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Coulomb's law is the electric version of a rule you already know. Two masses pull on each other with a force that dies off as the square of the distance; two charges do exactly the same, except that charges can also PUSH. The reason for the square is geometric: the influence of a point charge spreads over the surface of a sphere, and a sphere's area is 4πr², so anything spreading out evenly gets diluted by r² as it goes.

Given
  • Q₁ = the first charge, in coulombs (C)
  • Q₂ = the second charge, in coulombs (C)
  • r = the separation between them, in metres (m)

Find: The force F between them, in newtons (N)

Formula

F = kQ₁Q₂ / r²

Why this one. Two charges are named and a distance is given, so this is a FORCE question, not a field question. One charge and a distance would give a field instead. That single count, one charge or two, chooses the formula every time.

What each symbol means
  • F = the electrostatic force, in newtons
  • k = the Coulomb constant, 8.99 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻², often rounded to 9 × 10⁹
  • Q₁, Q₂ = the two charges, in coulombs
  • r = their separation in metres, and it is SQUARED
The working, line by line
  1. Convert both charges to coulombs. µC means × 10⁻⁶, nC means × 10⁻⁹.
  2. Convert the distance to metres. cm means ÷ 100, mm means ÷ 1000.
  3. Square r first, on its own, before dividing.
  4. Multiply k × Q₁ × Q₂ on top.
  5. Divide top by bottom.
  6. Give the direction: like charges repel, unlike attract.
Check it

Double the distance and the force must fall to a QUARTER, not a half. If your answer halves, you forgot to square r.

Work done moving a charge along a field is W = qEd. Converting first: q = 6.0 x 10-6 C and d = 50 cm = 0.50 m. With the unit field the examiner intends, E = 1 Vm-1, W = 6.0 x 10-6 x 1 x 0.50 = 3 x 10-6 J. The stem does not print a field strength, so the marked answer only follows if E is taken as 1 Vm-1; the arithmetic that earns the mark is q x d.

2.10mediumThe real test 1, question 4

What is the absolute potential at each of the following distances from a charge of +4 μC in air: r = 5 cm and r = 20 cm?

  • ✗750 KV & 180 kVWrong on the first value. The second is right, so this catches anyone who computes only one and matches on it.
  • ✗150 KV & 75 KVWrong. This pair falls by half, not by a quarter, so it cannot come from a 1/r law over a fourfold change in distance.
  • ✓720 KV & 180 KVCorrect. V = kQ/r gives 720 kV at 5 cm and 180 kV at 20 cm.
  • ✗250 KV & 50 KVWrong. This pair falls to a fifth and neither value follows from kQ = 36000.
  • ✗500 KV & 150 KVWrong. Neither value follows from kQ/r with these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Potential answers a different question from field. Field asks how hard a charge placed here would be pushed; potential asks how much energy it took to bring it here from far away. Because energy is a scalar there is no direction to worry about, only a sign. The key structural difference to hold on to: potential has a plain r underneath, field has r SQUARED. That one difference decides most questions in this topic.

Given
  • Q = +4 µC = 4 × 10⁻⁶ C
  • r₁ = 5 cm = 0.05 m
  • r₂ = 20 cm = 0.20 m
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²

Find: The absolute potential V at each of the two distances, in volts

Formula

V =kQr

Why this one. The question asks for POTENTIAL, so r appears to the first power. Absolute potential means measured against infinity, where the potential is taken as zero, which is exactly what this formula gives, so no difference between two points is being asked for. Only one charge is named, so nothing has to be added vectorially.

What each symbol means
  • V = the absolute potential at that point, in volts
  • k = the Coulomb constant, 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²
  • Q = the charge creating the potential, in coulombs
  • r = the distance from the charge, in metres, NOT squared
The working, line by line
  1. kQ is the same for both parts, so compute it once:
  2. kQ = 9 × 10⁹ × 4 × 10⁻⁶ = 36 × 10³ = 3.6 × 10⁴
  3. At r₁: V₁ = (3.6 × 10⁴) ÷ 0.05
  4. V₁ = 720 000 V = 720 kV
  5. At r₂: V₂ = (3.6 × 10⁴) ÷ 0.20
  6. V₂ = 180 000 V = 180 kV
Check it

The second distance is FOUR times the first, and potential goes as 1/r, so the second potential must be exactly a QUARTER of the first. 720 ÷ 4 = 180, which it is. Had the question asked for the field instead, the same four times further would have made it SIXTEEN times weaker, because of the square. Computing kQ once and dividing twice also saves half a minute under exam pressure.

Absolute potential is V = kQ/r, which falls as 1/r, not as 1/r2. With kQ = 9 x 109 x 4 x 10-6 = 36000: at r = 0.05 m, V = 36000/0.05 = 720000 V = 720 kV; at r = 0.20 m, V = 36000/0.20 = 180000 V = 180 kV. The distance quadruples, so the potential falls to a quarter, which is the check to run on the pair.

2.11mediumReference sheet R.1

You move from 5 cm to 20 cm away from a point charge, four times further out. What happens to the POTENTIAL?

  • ✓It falls to a quarter, because V = kQ/r has a plain rCorrect, and the printed answers on the paper confirm the quarter.
  • ✗It falls to a sixteenth, because of the inverse squareWrong. A sixteenth is what the FIELD would do, not the potential.
  • ✗It is unchanged, because potential is a scalarWrong. Being a scalar means it has no direction, not that it ignores distance.
  • ✗It rises four timesWrong direction: moving away lowers the potential.
  • ✗It falls to a halfWrong. A half would need r to appear as a square root.

A quarter. The potential carries a plain r, so four times the distance means a quarter of the value. Only the FIELD is squared. Test 1 question 4 confirms it: 720 kV at 5 cm becomes 180 kV at 20 cm, exactly a quarter. Had you squared it you would have looked for a sixteenth and found no such option.

2.12mediumReference sheet R.1

The potential energy of a charge q sitting at a potential V is U = qV, measured in {{1}}.

Answer:

Joules. Coulombs times volts gives joules, because a volt is defined as a joule per coulomb. That is also why W = qV computes a work in joules from a charge and a voltage.

2.13mediumReference sheet R.1

A uniform field exists between two parallel plates 2 mm apart with 60 V across them. What is the field strength?

  • ✓30000 V/mCorrect. 60 divided by 0.002.
  • ✗30 V/mWrong by a factor of 1000, from leaving the gap in mm.
  • ✗120 V/mWrong. This multiplies rather than divides.
  • ✗0.033 V/mWrong, and upside down: 0.002 divided by 60.
  • ✗3000 V/mWrong by a factor of ten in the conversion.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Between two parallel plates the field is UNIFORM, which means it has the same strength everywhere between them, unlike the field of a point charge which weakens with distance. That uniformity is what makes the plate formula so simple: the voltage is shared out evenly across the gap, so the field is just volts per metre. The unit V m⁻¹ is telling you the formula.

Given
  • V = 60 V across the plates
  • d = 2 mm = 2 × 10⁻³ m

Find: The field strength E between the plates, in V m⁻¹

Formula

E =Vd

Why this one. Parallel plates with a stated voltage and separation is the signature of this formula. There is no inverse square here at all, because the field does not spread out: the plates are flat and the lines run straight across, so nothing gets diluted with distance.

What each symbol means
  • E = the uniform field strength, in V m⁻¹, the same as N C⁻¹
  • V = the potential difference across the plates, in volts
  • d = the plate separation, in METRES, which is where the mark is lost
The working, line by line
  1. Convert the gap: 2 mm = 2 × 10⁻³ m
  2. E = V ÷ d
  3. E = 60 ÷ (2 × 10⁻³)
  4. Front numbers: 60 ÷ 2 = 30
  5. Dividing by 10⁻³ is multiplying by 10³: E = 30 × 10³
  6. E = 30000 V m⁻¹
Check it

The option 30 V m⁻¹ is what you get by leaving the gap in millimetres, and it is the commonest answer given by people who did the physics correctly. Dividing by a SMALL number must give a BIG answer, so if your result looks similar in size to the 60 you started with, the conversion did not happen.

Convert first: 2 mm = 0.002 m. Then E = V/d = 60 / 0.002 = 30000 V/m. Note the size: a modest 60 V across a very small gap gives an enormous field, which is exactly why capacitors are built with plates close together and why they eventually break down.

2.14hardReference sheet R.1

What is the key difference between E = kQ/r2 and V = kQ/r?

  • ✓The field goes as one over r² and is a vector, while the potential goes as one over r and is a scalarCorrect on both the power of r and the vector or scalar nature.
  • ✗The field goes as one over r and is a scalar, while the potential goes as one over r² and is a vectorWrong, and it swaps the two entirely.
  • ✗Both go as one over r², but only the potential is a vectorWrong. The potential goes as 1/r, and it is a scalar not a vector.
  • ✗Both go as one over r, and both are vectorsWrong. The field goes as 1/r².
  • ✗They are the same quantity written two waysWrong. They are different quantities with different units, N/C against volts.

Field is squared, potential is not. The field is a vector, so contributions add with direction; the potential is a scalar, so contributions add as plain signed numbers. That difference is why a potential question is usually the easier of the two.

3Electric Flux and Gauss

Speed tip. For flux, the angle is measured from the NORMAL, not the surface. Face on means the angle is 0 and the cosine is 1, so the flux is just EA.

3.1easyReference sheet R.4

A Gaussian surface is an {{1}} closed surface, chosen to make Gauss's law easy.

Answer:

Imaginary. It is a mathematical construction, not a physical object, and you choose its shape to match the symmetry of the problem: a sphere around a point charge, a cylinder around a line charge. The Amperian loop in magnetism is the same idea.

3.2easyThe real test 1, question 5

A uniform electric field E = 200 N/C passes through a surface of area 2 m2 at 0°. Find Flux.

  • ✗450 400 N/m2/CWrong, and the printed option runs two numbers together. Neither follows from 200 x 2.
  • ✗300 N/m2/CWrong. This would need an area of 1.5 m2.
  • ✓400 N/m2/CCorrect. Φ = EA cos 0 = 200 x 2 x 1 = 400.
  • ✗120 N/m2/CWrong. This is 200 x 2 x 0.3, which is no cosine of any angle offered.
  • ✗100 N/m2/CWrong. This halves the field instead of doubling it by the area.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Flux is a counting idea, not a force idea. Picture the field as a set of parallel arrows and the surface as a hoop held in their path. Flux asks: how many arrows go THROUGH the hoop? Hold the hoop face on and you catch the most. Tilt it and you catch fewer. Turn it edge on and the arrows slide past without crossing it at all, so the flux is zero.

Given
  • E = 200 N C⁻¹
  • A = 2 m²
  • θ = 0° (the angle from the NORMAL to the surface)

Find: The electric flux Φ, in N m² C⁻¹

Formula

Φ = EA cos θ

Why this one. A uniform field and a flat area is the simplest flux case, so the full Gauss machinery is not needed. The cosine is there to take only the part of the field that is perpendicular to the surface, since only that part actually passes through.

What each symbol means
  • Φ = the electric flux, in N m² C⁻¹
  • E = the field strength, in N C⁻¹
  • A = the area of the surface, in m²
  • θ = the angle between the field and the NORMAL to the surface, not the surface itself
The working, line by line
  1. θ = 0°, so cos θ = cos 0 = 1
  2. Φ = E × A × cos θ
  3. Φ = 200 × 2 × 1
  4. Φ = 400 N m² C⁻¹
Check it

At 0° the surface faces the field squarely, which is the maximum possible flux, so the answer must simply be E × A. If the question had said 60°, cos 60 = 0.5 and the flux would halve to 200.

Flux is the field times the area times the cosine of the angle between the field and the surface NORMAL: Φ = EA cos(θ). At 0° the field is straight through, cos 0 = 1, so Φ = 200 x 2 x 1 = 400. The angle being zero is what makes this a one-line question; at 90° the flux would be zero.

3.3easyReference sheet R.4

A Gaussian surface is a real physical shell that must be built around the charge before Gauss's law can be applied.

Answer: false

It is IMAGINARY, a mathematical surface you draw on the diagram and choose to suit the symmetry: a sphere around a point charge, a cylinder around a line. Nothing is constructed. The Amperian loop in magnetism is the same idea, and both are chosen purely to make the algebra come out.

3.4mediumReference sheet R.1

Electric flux is Φ = EA cos θ, where θ is measured from the {{1}} to the surface.

Answer:

From the normal, the line sticking straight out of the surface. Facing the field squarely gives θ = 0 and cos 0 = 1, the maximum. Edge on gives θ = 90 and cos 90 = 0, nothing through. Measuring from the surface instead inverts every answer.

3.5mediumReference sheet R.1

A closed spherical surface encloses a charge of 3 μC. A separate 10 μC charge sits just outside it. What is the total electric flux through the surface?

  • ✓3.39 x 105 N m2/CCorrect. Only the enclosed 3 μC counts.
  • ✗1.47 x 106 N m2/CWrong. This adds the outside charge, which contributes nothing.
  • ✗1.13 x 106 N m2/CWrong. This uses the 10 μC instead of the 3.
  • ✗Zero, because the charges have opposite effectsWrong. There is enclosed charge, so there is net flux.
  • ✗2.66 x 10-17 N m2/CWrong. This multiplies by ε₀ instead of dividing.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Gauss's law makes a claim that sounds too good to be true: the total flux out of a closed surface depends only on the charge INSIDE it, and not at all on charges outside, nor on where inside the charge sits, nor on the shape of the surface. The reason is that a field line from an outside charge that enters the surface must also leave it, so it contributes once as negative flux and once as positive and cancels itself out.

Given
  • Charge enclosed: Q = 3 µC = 3 × 10⁻⁶ C
  • A separate 10 µC charge sits OUTSIDE the surface
  • ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹

Find: The total electric flux Φ through the closed surface, in N m² C⁻¹

Formula

Φ =Qε₀

Why this one. The surface is CLOSED, which is the word that summons Gauss's law rather than Φ = EA cos θ. The outside charge is a deliberate distractor: it certainly changes the field at every point on the surface, but it adds nothing to the TOTAL flux, because whatever enters also leaves.

What each symbol means
  • Φ = the total flux through the closed surface, in N m² C⁻¹
  • Q = the ENCLOSED charge only, in coulombs
  • ε₀ = the permittivity of free space, 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹
The working, line by line
  1. Enclosed charge only: Q = 3 µC = 3 × 10⁻⁶ C
  2. The 10 µC is outside, so it contributes zero net flux and is discarded
  3. Φ = (3 × 10⁻⁶) ÷ (8.85 × 10⁻¹²)
  4. Front numbers: 3 ÷ 8.85 = 0.339
  5. Powers of ten: 10⁻⁶ ÷ 10⁻¹² = 10⁶
  6. Φ = 0.339 × 10⁶ = 3.39 × 10⁵ N m² C⁻¹
Check it

Two traps are in the options. Adding both charges gives 1.47 × 10⁶, and the zero option tempts anyone who thinks the outside charge cancels the inside one, which it does not: it cancels only itself. Ask one question of any Gauss problem before anything else, which is what is INSIDE, and the rest is one division.

Gauss's law counts ENCLOSED charge only: Φ = Q enclosed over ε₀ = 3 x 10-6 / 8.85 x 10-12 = 3.39 x 105 N m² per C. The 10 μC outside contributes exactly nothing, because every field line it sends into the surface comes back out again.

3.6mediumReference sheet R.2

Asked twice. Also set as: Reference sheet R.2.

The electric field inside a hollow conductor is zero, provided no charge is enclosed within that region.

Answer: true

True, and it is the Faraday cage. Charge on a conductor sits on the outer surface, so a Gaussian surface drawn inside encloses no charge, and by Gauss's law the flux, and therefore the field, is zero. It is why a car protects you from lightning and why sensitive electronics are wrapped in metal.

3.7mediumReference sheet R.2

In Gauss’s law the flux out equals the charge in divided by {{1}} nought.

Answer:

ε₀, the permittivity of free space. μ₀ is its magnetic counterpart, σ is a surface charge density and λ a line charge density.

3.8mediumReference sheet R.1

What is the electric field inside a conductor in electrostatic equilibrium, and what is the everyday name for the consequence?

  • ✓Zero, and the consequence is the Faraday cageCorrect on both.
  • ✗Zero, and the consequence is the Gaussian surfaceWrong on the name. A Gaussian surface is the imaginary surface you draw to apply the law, not a consequence of it.
  • ✗Equal to kQ/r², and the consequence is the Faraday cageWrong. The field inside is zero, not an inverse square.
  • ✗Equal to σε₀, and the consequence is polarisationWrong.σε₀ is a sheet field, and polarisation happens in insulators.
  • ✗Undefined, because a conductor has no interiorWrong. The interior is exactly where the field is zero.

E = 0 inside a conductor. Any charge on it moves to the surface, so a Gaussian surface drawn inside encloses no charge, and no enclosed charge means no field. The practical consequence is the Faraday cage: the inside of a conducting shell is shielded from outside fields.

3.9mediumReference sheet R.2

Asked twice. Also set as: Reference sheet R.2.

The electric field inside a hollow conductor is zero provided no charge is enclosed within that region.

Answer: true

Background, so this is not just a formula

Put a charge inside a hollow metal box and the free electrons in the metal shuffle around until the field they create exactly cancels the field inside the metal itself. That is why a car or an aeroplane protects you from lightning, and it is called a Faraday cage. But the cancellation applies to the region with NO enclosed charge; put a charge in the hollow and there is certainly a field in there.

Find: Whether the statement is true, and what the qualification is doing

Formula

Φ =Qε₀

Why this one. Gauss's law is what proves it. Draw an imaginary closed surface inside the cavity. If it encloses no charge then the total flux through it is zero, and by symmetry that forces the field itself to be zero everywhere on it. The moment a charge is enclosed, the right-hand side is no longer zero and neither is the field.

What each symbol means
  • Φ = the total flux through the imaginary closed surface
  • Q = the charge ENCLOSED by that surface, which is the whole point
  • ε₀ = the permittivity of free space
The working, line by line
  1. The statement is TRUE, and the qualification is load-bearing.
  2. Draw an imaginary closed surface inside the cavity.
  3. No charge is enclosed, so Q = 0 and the flux is zero.
  4. By the symmetry of the situation, zero flux everywhere means zero field.
  5. Remove the qualification and it breaks: enclose a charge and there is a field.
Check it

Sort out which zero you are talking about, since three different regions get confused. Inside the metal itself the field is always zero in electrostatics, whatever is around. Inside an EMPTY cavity the field is zero. Inside a cavity CONTAINING a charge the field is not zero. The word enclosed is what separates the three cases.

True, and the qualification matters: it is the absence of ENCLOSED charge that forces the field to zero, by Gauss’s law.
