3.10mediumThe real test 1, question 14

A conducting sphere of radius 0.25 m carries a total charge of 6 × 10-6 C. Calculate the electric field at a point 0.50 m from the center of the sphere.

  • ✗2.92 x 105 NC-1Wrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✓2.16 x 105 NC-1Correct. E = kQ/r2 = 9 x 109 x 6 x 10-6 / 0.25 = 2.16 x 105 NC-1.
  • ✗3.53 x 105 NC-1Wrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✗5 x 105 NC-1Wrong. This drops the 0.25 m2 denominator to a bare division and loses the factor entirely.
  • ✗1.47 x 105 NC-1Wrong. This is close to what you get measuring 0.50 m from the SURFACE, that is r = 0.75 m, which the question does not ask for.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Gauss's law delivers one enormously useful result for spheres: from OUTSIDE, a uniformly charged sphere behaves exactly as though all its charge were squeezed into a point at its centre. So its radius is irrelevant to the field outside it. Inside a conducting sphere the field is zero instead, because the charges rearrange until they cancel any field within the metal, which is why a car protects you in a thunderstorm.

Given
  • Sphere radius R = 0.25 m
  • Q = 6 × 10⁻⁶ C
  • The point of interest is r = 0.50 m from the CENTRE
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²

Find: The electric field E at that point, in N C⁻¹

Formula

E =kQr²

Why this one. The first job is to check where the point is. r = 0.50 m is greater than the radius 0.25 m, so the point lies OUTSIDE the sphere, and outside a sphere the point-charge formula applies without modification. Had the point been inside the conductor, the answer would have been zero instead, whatever the charge.

What each symbol means
  • E = the field at the point, in N C⁻¹
  • k = 9 × 10⁹ N m² C⁻²
  • Q = the total charge on the sphere, in coulombs
  • r = the distance from the CENTRE of the sphere, not from its surface
The working, line by line
  1. Check first: is r > R? 0.50 > 0.25, yes, so the point is outside
  2. Square the distance: r² = 0.50² = 0.25 m²
  3. Top: kQ = 9 × 10⁹ × 6 × 10⁻⁶
  4. Front numbers: 9 × 6 = 54
  5. Powers of ten: 10⁹ × 10⁻⁶ = 10³
  6. Top = 54 × 10³ = 5.4 × 10⁴
  7. E = (5.4 × 10⁴) ÷ 0.25
  8. E = 2.16 × 10⁵ N C⁻¹
Check it

The trap is measuring from the surface instead of the centre. Using 0.50 − 0.25 = 0.25 m would give four times the answer, 8.64 × 10⁵, which is not offered. Always start a sphere question by asking whether the point is inside or outside, and always measure r from the centre. Note also that 0.25 appears twice in this question meaning two different things, the radius and r², so label your lines.

Outside a charged conducting sphere the field is the same as if all the charge sat at the centre, so E = kQ/r2 with r measured from the CENTRE, not from the surface. E = 9 x 109 x 6 x 10-6 / (0.50)2 = 54000 / 0.25 = 2.16 x 105 NC-1. The 0.25 m radius is there to tell you the point is outside the sphere, and for no other reason.

3.11mediumReference sheet R.1

A field of 200 N/C crosses an area of 2 square metres at 60° to the normal. What is the flux?

  • ✓200 N m2/CCorrect. Half of the 400 you would get face on.
  • ✗400 N m2/CWrong. 400 is the answer at 0°, with the surface square to the field.
  • ✗346 N m2/CWrong. 346 uses SIN 60, not cos, which is the angle-from-the-surface error.
  • ✗0 N m2/CWrong. Zero needs 90°, with the field skimming along the surface.
  • ✗100 N m2/CWrong. This halves it twice over.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Flux counts how much of a field actually passes through a surface. Hold a hoop square-on to the field and you catch everything; tilt it and you catch less; turn it edge-on and nothing goes through at all. The cosine does that job, but only if the angle is measured the way physics defines it, which is from the NORMAL, the line sticking straight out of the surface, not from the surface itself.

Given
  • E = 200 N C⁻¹
  • A = 2 m²
  • θ = 60° to the normal

Find: The electric flux Φ, in N m² C⁻¹

Formula

Φ = EA cos θ

Why this one. The surface is flat and open rather than closed, and the field is uniform, so this is the direct flux formula and not Gauss's law. Cosine rather than sine because at 0°, square-on, the flux must be at its maximum, and cos 0 = 1 while sin 0 = 0.

What each symbol means
  • Φ = the electric flux, in N m² C⁻¹
  • E = the field strength, in N C⁻¹
  • A = the area, in m²
  • θ = the angle from the NORMAL to the surface
The working, line by line
  1. cos 60° = 0.5 (worth knowing without a calculator)
  2. Φ = E × A × cos θ
  3. Φ = 200 × 2 × 0.5
  4. 200 × 2 = 400
  5. Φ = 400 × 0.5 = 200 N m² C⁻¹
Check it

The option 400 is the flux you would get square-on, so it must be the maximum and cannot be right for a tilted surface: a tilt can only reduce the flux, never increase it. The option 346 is what sin 60° would have given, which is the standard punishment for using the wrong trigonometric function.

Φ = EA cos θ = 200 x 2 x cos 60 = 200 x 2 x 0.5 = 200 N m² per C. Test 1 question 5 is this same setup at 0°, where cos 0 = 1 and the answer is the full 400. Tilting to 60° halves it, because cos 60 is exactly 0.5.

3.12mediumReference sheet R.1

A charge sitting outside a closed surface contributes nothing to the net flux through it.

Answer: true

True. Every field line an external charge sends into the surface comes out again somewhere else, so the ins and outs cancel exactly. Gauss's law counts only ENCLOSED charge, which is what makes it useful: you can ignore everything outside the surface you drew.

3.13mediumReference sheet R.1

The field of an infinite line charge is E = λ over 2 π ε₀ r, which falls as 1 over r to the power {{1}}.

Answer:

One. A line spreads its field over a cylinder whose area grows in proportion to r, so the field falls as 1/r, more slowly than a point charge's 1/r². The magnetic field of a straight wire falls the same way and for the same geometric reason.

3.14mediumReference sheet R.1

Why is the electric field zero inside a hollow conductor?

  • ✓Because no charge is enclosed, so by Gauss's law there is no flux and no fieldCorrect, and it is a consequence of Gauss's law rather than a separate rule.
  • ✗Because the metal absorbs the fieldWrong. Nothing is absorbed; the charges rearrange until the interior field cancels.
  • ✗Because conductors carry no charge at allWrong. A charged conductor carries plenty; it just keeps it on the surface.
  • ✗Because the field is cancelled by the earthWrong. It works whether or not the conductor is earthed.
  • ✗Because air inside is an insulatorWrong. The effect is the same with a vacuum inside.

Charge on a conductor sits on its OUTER surface, so any Gaussian surface drawn inside encloses nothing, and Gauss's law then forces the field to zero. That is the Faraday cage: it is why a car protects you from lightning and why sensitive electronics are wrapped in metal.

3.15hardReference sheet R.1

Match how the field falls with distance for a point charge, an infinite line charge, and an infinite plane sheet.

  • ✓Point 1/r², line 1/r, sheet independent of distanceCorrect, and the reason is the geometry of how the field spreads.
  • ✗Point 1/r, line 1/r², sheet independent of distanceWrong, and it swaps the point and line cases.
  • ✗All three fall as 1/r²Wrong. Only the point charge falls as an inverse square.
  • ✗Point 1/r², line independent of distance, sheet 1/rWrong on the line and the sheet.
  • ✗All three are independent of distanceWrong. Only the infinite sheet has that property.

The pattern follows the shape. A point spreads its field over a sphere whose area grows as r², so E = kQ/r². A line spreads over a cylinder whose area grows as r, so E = λ over 2 π ε₀ r, falling as 1/r. A plane does not spread at all, so E = σ over 2 ε₀ is the SAME however far away you stand, which is startling but correct for an infinite sheet.

3.16hardReference sheet R.1

A charge sits OUTSIDE a closed surface. What is its contribution to the net flux through that surface?

  • ✓Nothing, because only enclosed charge contributesCorrect. Every line an outside charge sends in comes back out, so the net is zero.
  • ✗The same as if it were insideWrong. If that were so, the law could not be written in terms of enclosed charge alone.
  • ✗Half as much as if it were insideWrong. The contribution is zero, not a fraction.
  • ✗It makes the net flux negativeWrong. It contributes nothing at all, of either sign.
  • ✗It doubles the net fluxWrong, for the same reason.

Flux out equals charge in over ε₀. An external charge sends just as many field lines into the surface as out of it, so its net contribution is exactly zero. That is why Gauss’s law says Q ENCLOSED and nothing else.

3.17hardReference sheet R.1

The field of an infinite charged plane sheet, E =σ2 ε₀, is the same however far from the sheet you stand.

Answer: true

True, and it is genuinely surprising. There is no r in the formula at all. The reason is geometric: a point spreads its field over a growing sphere and a line over a growing cylinder, but an infinite plane does not spread it at all, so moving away gains you nothing. It is what makes the uniform field between capacitor plates possible.

4Capacitance, Dielectrics and Energy

Speed tip. Capacitors are the opposite of resistors: parallel adds, series is the reciprocal. Energy has V squared in it.

4.1easyReference sheet R.1

For a parallel plate capacitor, C = εᵣε₀ Ad, where A is the plate area and d is the {{1}} between the plates.

Answer:

The separation, that is the gap. Closer plates mean a bigger capacitance, since d is in the denominator, which is why real capacitors use very thin insulating layers. Note that d is the gap, not the thickness of the metal.

4.2easyReference sheet R.4

Capacitance is the charge stored per volt applied, and its unit is the farad, which is one coulomb per {{1}}.

Answer:

One coulomb per volt, straight from C = Q/V. The farad is a huge unit in practice, which is why real capacitors are quoted in μF, nF and pF.

4.3easyReference sheet R.4

An electric dipole is:

  • ✓Two equal and opposite charges separated by a small distanceCorrect. Two equal and opposite charges a small distance apart, with the dipole moment p = qd pointing from the negative charge to the positive one.
  • ✗A single charge on a conductorWrong. A dipole needs two charges.
  • ✗Two equal charges of the same signWrong. They must be OPPOSITE, or the net effect cancels differently.
  • ✗A conductor with no net chargeWrong. A neutral conductor is not a dipole, though it can be polarised into one.
  • ✗An insulator between capacitor platesWrong. That is a dielectric, though dielectrics work BY forming dipoles.

Two equal and opposite charges a small distance apart, with dipole moment p = qd pointing from the negative to the positive. The polarised atom is the everyday example: an applied field pulls the electron cloud one way and the nucleus the other, creating exactly this arrangement, which is what polarisation in an insulator means.

4.4easyReference sheet R.1

Inserting a dielectric between capacitor plates always increases the capacitance.

Answer: true

True. C = εᵣ times C₀, and the relative permittivity εᵣ is always greater than 1 for a real material, being exactly 1 only for vacuum. The mechanism is polarisation: the dielectric's induced dipoles partly cancel the field between the plates, so the same charge sits at a lower voltage, and C = Q/V rises. Note it works the OPPOSITE way for the Coulomb force, which a dielectric divides by the same factor.

4.5easyReference sheet R.1

Capacitance is C =QV, the charge stored per {{1}} applied.

Answer:

Per volt. It is a ratio, and crucially a FIXED one: raise the voltage and you push in proportionally more charge, so Q/V does not move. Capacitance is set by geometry, never by what you store in it.

4.6easyReference sheet R.4

Capacitance is defined as the charge stored per volt applied, and is measured in farads.

Answer: true

True, and the farad is a coulomb per volt. It is an enormous unit in practice: the capacitors in this course run from pF to μF, and a 1 F capacitor would be a remarkable object. Seeing an answer of 4.77 F should therefore fail the step 7 believability check instantly.

4.7easyReference sheet R.4

A dipole is two equal and {{1}} charges separated by a small distance.

Answer:

Opposite. A polarised atom is the everyday case: an applied field pulls the electron cloud one way and the nucleus the other, producing exactly this arrangement. Its dipole moment is p = qd, pointing from the negative charge to the positive one.

4.8easyReference sheet R.4

One farad is equal to:

  • ✓One coulomb per voltCorrect. A coulomb per volt, read straight off the definition C = Q/V.
  • ✗One volt per coulombWrong, and inverted.
  • ✗One coulomb per secondWrong. That is the ampere.
  • ✗One joule per coulombWrong. That is the volt.
  • ✗One volt per ampWrong. That is the ohm.

A coulomb per volt, read straight off C = Q/V. The other four options are all real units of other things, which is why they make good distractors: a coulomb per second is the AMPERE, a joule per coulomb is the VOLT, and a volt per amp is the OHM.

4.9easyLecture slides, module 2, slide 11

The dipole moment is p = qd, where q is a charge in coulombs and d a separation in metres. Its unit is therefore the coulomb {{1}}.

Answer:

Coulomb metre, written C m. The unit of any product is the product of the units, so a charge in coulombs times a distance in metres gives coulomb metres. This is the class drill the slide sets, and it is worth doing rather than skipping: reading a unit off a formula is the fastest check that you have the formula the right way up.

4.10mediumReference sheet R.1

The torque on an electric dipole in a uniform field is τ = pE sin θ. At what angle is the torque zero?

  • ✓Zero degrees, with the dipole aligned along the fieldCorrect. Aligned means sin θ = 0, so no torque.
  • ✗Ninety degrees, with the dipole across the fieldWrong. 90° is where the torque is at its MAXIMUM.
  • ✗Forty-five degreesWrong. sin 45 is about 0.71, so the torque is substantial.
  • ✗The torque is never zero in a fieldWrong. It is zero at alignment, which is why dipoles line up.
  • ✗One hundred and eighty degrees onlyWrong. sin 180 is also zero, but so is sin 0, and 0 is the stable one.

sin 0 = 0, so the torque vanishes when the dipole lies along the field. That is the position the field twists it INTO: a dipole placed at an angle rotates until it is aligned, and then stops. The torque is greatest at 90°, where sin is 1, because that is when the field has the most turning to do.

4.11mediumReference sheet R.1

The energy of a dipole in a field is U = minus pE cos θ, and it is at a minimum when the dipole is aligned with the field.

Answer: true

True. At θ = 0, cos 0 = 1, so U = minus pE, the most negative value available and therefore the minimum. Systems settle into their lowest energy, which is exactly why a dipole turns to align itself. The torque formula and the energy formula are telling the same story from two directions.

4.12mediumReference sheet R.2

Asked twice. Also set as: Reference sheet R.1.

How do capacitors combine, compared with resistors?

  • ✓Backwards: capacitors ADD in parallel, while resistors add in seriesCorrect, and remembering one and flipping is the reliable way to keep them apart.
  • ✗The same way as resistors in both casesWrong. They behave oppositely, which is the whole difficulty.
  • ✗Capacitors add in series, and resistors add in parallel tooWrong on both halves.
  • ✗Capacitors always add, whichever way they are connectedWrong. In series they combine reciprocally.
  • ✗Capacitors never simply addWrong. In parallel they add straightforwardly.

Capacitors ADD in parallel: C = C1 + C2. In series they combine reciprocally, giving less than the smallest. Resistors are the exact opposite: they add in series and combine reciprocally in parallel. The hook is to learn ONE of them properly and flip it for the other. Physically, putting capacitors side by side is just making one bigger plate, so the capacitances add.

4.13mediumReference sheet R.1

Capacitors in series combine as 1 over C equivalent = 1 over C1 + 1 over C2, which always gives a total LESS than the {{1}} capacitor in the combination.

Answer:

Less than the smallest. That is a free check on every series answer: work it out, and if your total is not below the smallest capacitor you have forgotten to flip the reciprocal back. Test 1 question 2 uses it, where 1 and 5 μF in series give 0.833, below the 1.

4.14mediumReference sheet R.1

Which of these is NOT an equivalent expression for the energy stored in a capacitor?

  • ✓W = QVCorrect as the odd one out. QV has no half, so it is double the true energy.
  • ✗W = one half CV squaredWrong choice: this IS a valid form, the one used most often.
  • ✗W = one half QVWrong choice: this is valid, and follows from C = Q/V.
  • ✗W =Q²2CWrong choice: this is valid, and is the form to use when you know Q rather than V.
  • ✗All four are equivalentWrong. QV is not among the valid forms.

The three valid forms all carry a factor of one half: W = one half CV squared = one half QV =Q²2C. QV without the half is wrong, and the half is there for a physical reason: the voltage climbed from zero to V as the capacitor charged, so the average voltage the charge crossed was V/2, not V. Forgetting it doubles the answer, and Test 1 question 15 offers exactly that wrong value.

4.15mediumReference sheet R.4

Match each quantity to what it describes.

Permittivity, symbol epsilonHow readily a material permits an ELECTRIC field
Permeability, symbol muHow readily a material permits a MAGNETIC field
PolarisationInduced dipoles forming in an insulator under an applied field

The two names are deliberately parallel, and so are their roles: ε₀ appears in every electrostatic formula and μ₀ in every magnetic one. Their product is what gives the speed of light. Polarisation is the mechanism by which a dielectric raises capacitance.

4.16mediumReference sheet R.2

Capacitance is charge per volt, and it is fixed by the capacitor’s {{1}}.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

Capacitance is charge per volt, and the striking thing is that this ratio is FIXED for a given capacitor. Put more charge on and the voltage rises in exact proportion, so the ratio does not budge. That means capacitance cannot depend on how much charge or voltage you happen to be using; it depends only on how the thing is built, which means its shape, its size, and what is between the plates.

Find: The word that completes: capacitance is fixed by the capacitor's ___

Formula

C =ε₀ εr Ad

Why this one. Every quantity on the right-hand side is a fact about the construction: the plate area, the gap, and the material filling it. Neither Q nor V appears anywhere, which is the algebraic statement of the answer.

What each symbol means
  • A = the plate area, part of the geometry
  • d = the plate separation, part of the geometry
  • εr = the dielectric constant of the filling, a property of the material
  • ε₀ = a constant of nature, not of this capacitor
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is GEOMETRY.
  2. Why not charge: doubling the charge doubles the voltage too, so C = Q ÷ V is unchanged.
  3. Why not voltage: the same argument the other way round.
  4. Why not temperature: real dielectrics do drift a little with temperature, but that is a second-order imperfection, not what sets the value.
Check it

Look at the parallel plate formula and ask which symbols could change without rebuilding the capacitor. A and d cannot, εr cannot, ε₀ certainly cannot. Nothing in it can vary with use, which is precisely why capacitance is quoted as a fixed number on the component itself.

Geometry. For a parallel plate that means the area and the separation, plus the dielectric between the plates.

4.17mediumReference sheet R.2

The half in one half CV squared is there because the voltage rose from zero as the capacitor was charged.

Answer: true

True. The charge that arrives first is moved across almost no potential difference, so the average voltage over the whole charging process is half the final value.

4.18mediumReference sheet R.1

Asked twice. Also set as: Reference sheet R.2.

How do capacitors combine compared with resistors?

  • ✓Capacitors ADD in parallel, which is backwards from resistors, which add in seriesCorrect, and this is the pair most often confused under time pressure.
  • ✗Capacitors add in series, exactly like resistorsWrong. Series capacitors combine by reciprocals, giving less than the smallest.
  • ✗Both add in parallelWrong. Resistors in parallel take reciprocals.
  • ✗Both use reciprocals in both arrangementsWrong. Each uses the reciprocal rule in one arrangement only.
  • ✗Capacitors cannot be combined at allWrong. Combining them is routine, and Test 1 question 2 does exactly that.

Capacitors add in parallel and take reciprocals in series; resistors do the opposite. Remember ONE of them and flip it for the other. The physical reason is that putting capacitors side by side effectively enlarges the plate area, and a larger area means more capacitance.

4.19mediumReference sheet R.2

Capacitors add when they are in {{1}}, which is backwards from resistors.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

Resistors and capacitors combine by opposite rules, and knowing WHY stops you having to memorise two of everything. Put resistors in series and the current must fight through both, so resistance adds. Put capacitors in parallel and you have effectively built one capacitor with more plate area, so capacitance adds. Series capacitors behave like one capacitor with a wider gap, which makes them worse, hence the reciprocals.

Find: The word that completes: capacitors add when they are in ___

The working, line by line
  1. The answer is PARALLEL.
  2. Parallel capacitors share the same two terminals, so it is as if the plates were made bigger, and bigger area means more capacitance: C = C₁ + C₂.
  3. Series capacitors sit one behind the other, like a single capacitor with a wider gap, so the total is SMALLER than either: 1/C = 1/C₁ + 1/C₂.
  4. Resistors do the reverse, adding in series.
  5. Why not opposition or phase: those belong to a.c. circuits and reactance, not to combining capacitance.
Check it

One sentence carries both rules: capacitors are backwards from resistors. So work out the resistor case, which most people find natural, and then flip it. And you always have an independent guard on the answer, since a series combination must come out SMALLER than the smallest item in it, while a parallel one must come out larger than the largest.

Parallel. Resistors add in series, so learning one rule and flipping it for the other is safer than trying to hold both.

4.20mediumThe real test 1, question 11

A metal sphere mounted on an insulating rod carries a charge of 4.5 nC when its potential is 180 V higher than its surroundings. What is the capacitance of the capacitor formed by the sphere and its surroundings?

  • ✓25 pFCorrect. C = Q/V = 4.5 x 10-9 / 180 = 2.5 x 10-11 F = 25 pF.
  • ✗12 pFWrong, and about half: this comes from doubling the potential difference.
  • ✗8.1 pFWrong. No route to this value from 4.5 nC and 180 V.
  • ✗30 pFWrong. This would need 5.4 nC at the same potential.
  • ✗6.3 pFWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Capacitance is not about how much charge something is holding at this moment; it is about how much charge it holds PER VOLT. A big capacitance means you can pile on a lot of charge without the voltage climbing much. Even an isolated metal sphere has a capacitance, because raising its charge raises its potential above its surroundings, and the ratio of the two is fixed by its size and shape alone.

Given
  • Q = 4.5 nC = 4.5 × 10⁻⁹ C
  • V = 180 V (the potential above the surroundings)

Find: The capacitance C, in picofarads (pF)

Formula

C =QV

Why this one. This is the definition of capacitance itself, so it needs no derivation and no geometry. The question gives one charge and one voltage and asks for capacitance, which is exactly the ratio. The sphere and the insulating rod are scene-setting: they explain WHY there is a capacitance to speak of, but they add no numbers to the calculation.

What each symbol means
  • C = the capacitance, in farads (F)
  • Q = the charge stored, in coulombs
  • V = the potential difference that charge produces, in volts
  • 1 pF = 10⁻¹² F, and 1 nF = 10⁻⁹ F
The working, line by line
  1. Convert the charge: 4.5 nC = 4.5 × 10⁻⁹ C
  2. C = Q ÷ V
  3. C = (4.5 × 10⁻⁹) ÷ 180
  4. Front numbers: 4.5 ÷ 180 = 0.025
  5. C = 0.025 × 10⁻⁹ F
  6. Move to picofarads: 0.025 × 10⁻⁹ = 25 × 10⁻¹²
  7. C = 25 pF
Check it

Every option is in picofarads, so the conversion from 10⁻⁹ to 10⁻¹² is compulsory and is where the mark is won. The rule: to go from nano to pico, multiply by 1000. And 25 pF is a believable capacitance for a small isolated sphere, being about what a short length of coaxial cable has.

Capacitance is charge per unit potential difference: C = Q/V. The geometry is a distraction, because the sphere and its surroundings are simply being treated as a capacitor. C = 4.5 x 10-9 / 180 = 2.5 x 10-11 F = 25 pF. Remember 1 pF = 10-12 F.

4.21mediumThe real test 1, question 15

What is the capacitance of a capacitor having a stored energy of 2.25 mJ after being charged with a 30-volt source?

  • ✗10 μFWrong, and it is the classic slip: forgetting the 1/2 and computing U/V2 doubled, or equivalently dropping the factor of two.
  • ✓5 μFCorrect. C = 2U/V2 = 4.5 x 10-3 / 900 = 5 μF.
  • ✗25 μFWrong. No route to this value from 2.25 mJ at 30 V.
  • ✗13 μFWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✗2.5 μFWrong, and exactly half the answer: this comes from using U/V2 without the factor of two.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Charging a capacitor is not free. The first charge crosses easily, but each new charge has to be pushed against the ones already sitting there, so the work builds up as the voltage rises. Averaging that over the whole process is where the half comes from in the energy formula. That stored energy is what a camera flash releases in a millisecond, and what makes a large charged capacitor genuinely dangerous long after the power is off.

Given
  • U = 2.25 mJ = 2.25 × 10⁻³ J
  • V = 30 V

Find: The capacitance C, in microfarads

Formula

U = ½CV², so C =2UV²

Why this one. An energy and a voltage are given and a capacitance is wanted, and this is the formula joining exactly those three. Rearranging it moves the half across as a doubling on top, and the V² down to the bottom. The other energy forms, U = ½QV and U = Q² ÷ 2C, would need the charge, which is not given.

What each symbol means
  • U = the energy stored, in joules
  • C = the capacitance, in farads
  • V = the voltage the capacitor was charged to, in volts, and it is SQUARED
  • 1 mJ = 10⁻³ J and 1 µF = 10⁻⁶ F
The working, line by line
  1. Convert the energy: 2.25 mJ = 2.25 × 10⁻³ J
  2. Rearrange: C = (2 × U) ÷ V²
  3. Top: 2 × 2.25 × 10⁻³ = 4.5 × 10⁻³
  4. Bottom: V² = 30² = 900
  5. C = (4.5 × 10⁻³) ÷ 900
  6. Front numbers: 4.5 ÷ 900 = 0.005
  7. C = 0.005 × 10⁻³ = 5 × 10⁻⁶ F
  8. C = 5 µF
Check it

Forgetting to double gives 2.5 µF and forgetting to square the 30 gives a wildly different number; both wrong routes are sitting in the option list waiting. Verify forwards in one line: ½ × 5 × 10⁻⁶ × 900 = 2.25 × 10⁻³ J, which is the energy given. Substituting your answer back into the original formula takes seconds and catches nearly every rearrangement error.

Energy stored is U = 1/2 C V2, so rearranging for the capacitance gives C = 2U / V2. Substituting: C = 2 x 2.25 x 10-3 / 302 = 4.5 x 10-3 / 900 = 5 x 10-6 F = 5 μF. The half and the SQUARE on the voltage are where marks are lost here.

4.22mediumReference sheet R.1

The capacitance of an isolated sphere is C = 4 π ε₀ R, so a larger sphere has a larger capacitance.

Answer: true

True. R is the only variable in it, so capacitance rises directly with radius. Notice what is absent: no Q and no V, because they cancelled when C = Q/V was formed. That cancellation is the clearest proof in the course that capacitance depends on geometry alone.

4.23mediumReference sheet R.1

For C = εᵣε₀ Ad, which change INCREASES the capacitance?

  • ✓Moving the plates closer togetherCorrect. Smaller d, larger C.
  • ✗Moving the plates further apartWrong, and the opposite.
  • ✗Reducing the plate areaWrong. A is on top, so less area means less capacitance.
  • ✗Replacing the dielectric with vacuumWrong. Vacuum has εᵣ = 1, the smallest possible, so this reduces it.
  • ✗Raising the applied voltageWrong, and it is the classic misconception: no V appears in the formula at all.

d is in the denominator, so a smaller gap gives a bigger capacitance. That is why real capacitors use very thin insulating layers. Area helps too, since A is on top, and so does a dielectric, since εᵣ is at least 1. Voltage does nothing at all: it is not in the formula.

4.24mediumReference sheet R.1

Capacitors in parallel add directly, C = C1 + C2, which is backwards from how resistors behave in parallel.

Answer: true

True. Capacitors add in PARALLEL and combine reciprocally in series; resistors do exactly the opposite. Physically, putting capacitors side by side is just building one larger plate, so the capacitances sum. The reliable way to remember is to learn one of the four cases properly and flip it for the other.

4.25mediumLecture slides, module 5, slide 30

Permittivity, symbol ε, describes how readily a material permits an electric field, while permeability, symbol μ, describes how readily it permits a {{1}} field.

Answer:

A magnetic field. The two names are deliberately parallel and so are their roles: ε₀ appears in every electrostatic formula, μ₀ in every magnetic one. Their product is what gives the speed of light, which is how the two subjects turned out to be one.

4.26mediumLecture slides, module 2, slide 20

Determine the value of the capacitance if the radius of the spherical shell is R = 100 cm. Take ε-0 = 8.85 x 10-12 F/m.

  • ✓1.1 x 10-10 FCorrect. 4 π times 8.85 x 10-12 × 1 metre.
  • ✗8.85 x 10-12 FWrong. That is ε-0 itself, with the 4 π R never applied.
  • ✗1.1 x 10-8 FWrong by a factor of 100, which is what leaving R as 100 cm instead of 1 m does.
  • ✗3.5 x 10-11 FWrong. This uses π rather than 4 π.
  • ✗1.1 x 10-12 FWrong by a factor of 100 the other way.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Capacitance is not only a property of two facing plates. Any isolated conductor has one, because putting charge on it raises its potential, and capacitance is simply the ratio of the two. For a sphere the answer depends on nothing but its radius: a bigger sphere spreads the same charge over more surface, so its potential rises less, so its capacitance is larger.

Given
  • R = 100 cm = 1.0 m
  • ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹
  • π = 3.142

Find: The capacitance C of the spherical shell, in farads

Formula

C = 4πε₀R

Why this one. It follows from the two things already known about a sphere: its potential is V = kQ ÷ R, and capacitance is C = Q ÷ V. Divide one by the other and the Q cancels, leaving C = R ÷ k, which is the same as 4πε₀R because k = 1 ÷ (4πε₀). There is no second plate and no gap, so the parallel plate formula cannot apply.

What each symbol means
  • C = the capacitance, in farads
  • ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹
  • R = the radius of the sphere, in METRES
  • 4π = 12.566, a pure number
The working, line by line
  1. Convert the radius: 100 cm = 1.0 m
  2. C = 4 × π × ε₀ × R
  3. 4π = 12.566
  4. C = 12.566 × 8.85 × 10⁻¹² × 1.0
  5. Front numbers: 12.566 × 8.85 = 111.2
  6. C = 111.2 × 10⁻¹² = 1.112 × 10⁻¹⁰ F
  7. C ≈ 1.1 × 10⁻¹⁰ F, which is 111 pF
Check it

A one metre sphere, which is enormous, manages only about a hundred picofarads. That is worth remembering as a feel for the size of things: capacitance is hard to come by from geometry alone, which is exactly why real capacitors use large rolled-up areas and gaps measured in microns. The option 8.85 × 10⁻¹² is what you get by forgetting the 4π entirely.

Convert first: 100 cm is 1.00 m, not 100. Then C = 4 π ε-0 R = 4 x 3.1416 x 8.85 x 10-12 x 1.00 = 1.11 x 10-10 F, which the slide gives as 1.1 x 10-10 F. A whole metre of sphere yields only about a tenth of a nF, which is why real capacitors use closely spaced plates rather than spheres.

4.27mediumLecture slides, module 2, slide 23

A parallel plate capacitor has capacitance 8.85 x 10-11 F. What is the capacitance if a dielectric material with dielectric constant of 3 is inserted between the plates?

  • ✓2.7 x 10-10 FCorrect. Three times the original, written in standard form.
  • ✗2.95 x 10-11 FWrong. This DIVIDES by 3, but a dielectric always increases capacitance, never reduces it.
  • ✗8.85 x 10-11 FWrong. That is the original capacitance, unchanged, but inserting a dielectric must change it.
  • ✗2.7 x 10-11 FWrong by a factor of ten: 3 x 8.85 is 26.55, so the exponent moves from -11 to -10.
  • ✗1.77 x 10-10 FWrong. This multiplies by 2 rather than by the dielectric constant of 3 that the question gives.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A dielectric is an insulator slipped between the plates, and it improves a capacitor without any change of shape. Its molecules turn to face the field and set up a small field of their own pointing the other way, which partly cancels the original. Less net field means less voltage for the same charge, and less voltage for the same charge means more capacitance. The dielectric constant is simply the factor by which it helps.

Given
  • C₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹¹ F, the capacitance with nothing between the plates
  • K = 3, the dielectric constant of the material inserted

Find: The new capacitance C with the dielectric in place

Formula

C = KC₀

Why this one. The geometry is untouched, since neither the plate area nor the gap changes, so the only new ingredient is the material. Its whole effect is one multiplying factor. This is quicker and safer than rebuilding the capacitance from ε₀A ÷ d, and it works whatever the shape of the capacitor.

What each symbol means
  • C = the capacitance with the dielectric, in farads
  • K = the dielectric constant, sometimes written εr, a pure number with no unit
  • C₀ = the original capacitance in vacuum or air, in farads
The working, line by line
  1. C = K × C₀
  2. C = 3 × 8.85 × 10⁻¹¹
  3. Front numbers: 3 × 8.85 = 26.55
  4. C = 26.55 × 10⁻¹¹
  5. Tidy into standard form: C = 2.655 × 10⁻¹⁰ F
  6. C ≈ 2.7 × 10⁻¹⁰ F
Check it

A dielectric can only INCREASE capacitance, because K is never less than 1 for any real material. So the answer must be bigger than 8.85 × 10⁻¹¹, which instantly kills the options offering 2.95 × 10⁻¹¹ and 2.7 × 10⁻¹¹, both of which are smaller. The remaining care is tidying 26.55 × 10⁻¹¹ into 2.655 × 10⁻¹⁰, which is the same number.

A dielectric multiplies the capacitance: C = εᵣ times C₀ = 3 x 8.85 x 10-11 = 2.655 x 10-10 F, which the slide rounds to 2.7 x 10-10 F. Note the exponent shifts from -11 to -10 because 3 x 8.85 = 26.55, which is 2.655 in standard form with the exponent raised by one.

4.28hardReference sheet R.1

A capacitor is charged to a higher voltage. What happens to its capacitance?

  • ✓Nothing, because capacitance is fixed by geometry rather than by Q or VCorrect. Geometry sets it; Q and V simply move together.
  • ✗It increases, because C = Q/V and Q has increasedWrong. Q rises, but V rises with it, so the ratio holds.
  • ✗It decreases, because V is in the denominatorWrong, for the same reason: the numerator rises too.
  • ✗It doublesWrong. Nothing about the capacitor has changed.
  • ✗It becomes zeroWrong. Capacitance is a property of the object.

C = Q/V is charge per volt, but the RATIO is set by the geometry: the plate area, the separation and the dielectric. Raising V raises Q in exact proportion, so the ratio does not move. For a parallel plate, C = ε r ε₀ A/d contains no Q and no V at all.

4.29hardReference sheet R.1

Why does the energy stored in a capacitor carry a factor of one half, W = one half CV squared?

  • ✓Because the voltage climbed from zero as the capacitor charged, so the average voltage was half the final oneCorrect, and it is why the identical half appears in the inductor formula.
  • ✗Because only half the charge is stored on each plateWrong. Both plates carry the full magnitude Q, one positive and one negative.
  • ✗Because half the energy is always lost as heatWrong. The formula is for stored energy, not for losses.
  • ✗Because the two plates share the energy equallyWrong. The energy is in the field between them, not split between the plates.
  • ✗It is an arbitrary constant with no reason behind itWrong. It follows directly from the voltage rising linearly with charge.

The first charge to arrive crosses no potential difference; the last crosses the full V. The average is V/2, so the work done is Q times V/2, that is one half QV, which becomes one half CV squared. The same reasoning gives the half in one half LI squared for an inductor.

4.30hardThe real test 1, question 2

Determine the total capacitance of the capacitors given in Figure 1

  • ✗5.1 μFWrong. No arrangement of 1, 5 and 8 gives 5.1.
  • ✗3.43 μFWrong. This is all three in series: 1/(1/1 + 1/5 + 1/8) = 0.784, or a mis-stated version of it. Series can never exceed the smallest capacitor, so it cannot be right here.
  • ✗0.8 μFWrong, and it is the near miss: 0.833 μF is C1 and C2 in series, the FIRST step only, with C3 forgotten.
  • ✓8.8 μFCorrect. C1 in series with C2 gives 5/6 = 0.833 μF, and in parallel with C3 that is 0.833 + 8 = 8.8 μF.
  • ✗14.0 μFWrong. This is all three added as though every one were in parallel: 1 + 5 + 8 = 14.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Capacitors combine the OPPOSITE way round to resistors, and the reason is physical rather than arbitrary. Put two capacitors side by side across the same two points and you have simply built one capacitor with more plate area, so the capacitance adds. String them one after the other and the charge has to cross both gaps in turn, which is like building one capacitor with a wider gap, so the capacitance falls. Wide gap, weak capacitor.

Given
  • C₁ = 1 µF and C₂ = 5 µF, drawn one above the other in the SAME branch
  • C₃ = 8 µF, drawn in its own branch beside them
  • Both branches connect the same top wire to the same bottom wire

Find: The total capacitance of the network, in µF

Formula

1/Cs = 1/C₁ + 1/C₂, then C = Cs + C₃

Why this one. Read the picture before reaching for a formula. C₁ and C₂ sit one after the other with no junction between them, which is the definition of SERIES, so their reciprocals add. That combined branch and C₃ both run from the top wire to the bottom wire, sharing the same two ends, which is the definition of PARALLEL, so those simply add. Series first, parallel last: work from the inside of the picture outwards.

What each symbol means
  • C₁, C₂ = the two capacitors in series, in microfarads
  • Cs = their combined series value, always SMALLER than either of them
  • C₃ = the capacitor in parallel with that branch
  • C = the total capacitance seen at the terminals
The working, line by line
  1. Series pair first: 1/Cs = 1/C₁ + 1/C₂
  2. 1/Cs = 1/1 + 1/5
  3. Common denominator: 1/Cs = 5/5 + 1/5 = 6/5
  4. Turn it up the other way: Cs = 5/6 = 0.833 µF
  5. Now the parallel step: C = Cs + C₃
  6. C = 0.833 + 8
  7. C = 8.83 ≈ 8.8 µF
Check it

Two guards, both instant. A series combination must come out SMALLER than the smallest capacitor in it, and 0.833 is duly under 1. And adding anything in parallel can only INCREASE the total, so the final answer must exceed 8. That single fact kills 5.1, 3.43 and 0.8 without any arithmetic at all. The remaining trap, 14.0, is what you get by adding all three as if they were parallel.

Read the picture before reaching for a formula. C1 and C2 sit one above the other in the SAME branch, so they are in series; that branch and C3 span the same two terminals, so the pair is in parallel with C3. Series first: 1/Cs = 1/1 + 1/5 = 6/5, so Cs = 5/6 = 0.833 μF. Then parallel adds: C = 0.833 + 8 = 8.83, which is 8.8 μF. The clue that parallel comes last is that the answer must exceed 8, since adding a capacitor in parallel can only increase the total.

4.31hardThe real test 1, question 6

The area of each plate of a parallel plate capacitor is 4 x 10-2 m2. If the thickness of the dielectric placed in between the plates is 10-3 m and the relative permittivity of the dielectric is 7, then the capacitance of the capacitor is?

  • ✗0.2478 x 10-9 FWrong by a factor of ten, which is what you get from mishandling the 10-2 area or the 10-3 thickness.
  • ✗2.478 x 109 FWrong sign on the exponent. 2.478 x 109 F is larger than any capacitor ever built.
  • ✗4.77 FWrong. This drops the permittivity of free space entirely.
  • ✓2.478 x 10-9 FCorrect. C = epsilon0 epsilonr A / d = 2.478 x 10-9 F.
  • ✗0.2478 x 109 FWrong on both the digits and the sign of the exponent.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A parallel plate capacitor stores more charge when the plates are big, when the gap is small, and when the space between them is filled with an insulator rather than air. The insulator is called a dielectric, and it works because its molecules line up against the field and partly cancel it, which lowers the voltage for the same charge, and less voltage for the same charge means more capacitance. Its relative permittivity is simply the factor by which it improves things.

Given
  • A = 4 × 10⁻² m² (area of each plate)
  • d = 10⁻³ m (thickness of the dielectric, which is the plate separation)
  • εr = 7 (relative permittivity of the dielectric, a pure number)
  • ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹

Find: The capacitance C, in farads

Formula

C =ε₀ εr Ad

Why this one. Plate area and plate separation are given, which is the signature of the parallel plate formula, and a dielectric is named, which supplies the εr factor. Without a dielectric the formula would be just ε₀A ÷ d; the dielectric multiplies it by 7. Note that the thickness of the dielectric IS the gap, because the dielectric fills the gap completely.

What each symbol means
  • C = the capacitance, in farads (F)
  • ε₀ = the permittivity of free space, 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹
  • εr = the relative permittivity, how many times better than vacuum, no unit
  • A = the area of ONE plate, in m²
  • d = the separation of the plates, in metres
The working, line by line
  1. Top, part one: ε₀ × εr = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² × 7 = 61.95 × 10⁻¹² = 6.195 × 10⁻¹¹
  2. Top, part two: × A = 6.195 × 10⁻¹¹ × 4 × 10⁻²
  3. Front numbers: 6.195 × 4 = 24.78
  4. Powers of ten: 10⁻¹¹ × 10⁻² = 10⁻¹³
  5. Top = 24.78 × 10⁻¹³ = 2.478 × 10⁻¹²
  6. Divide by d: C = (2.478 × 10⁻¹²) ÷ 10⁻³
  7. Powers of ten: 10⁻¹² ÷ 10⁻³ = 10⁻⁹
  8. C = 2.478 × 10⁻⁹ F (that is 2.478 nF)
Check it

The options are the same digits with the sign of the exponent flipped, so the exponent is the whole mark. A capacitance of 2.478 × 10⁺⁹ F would be larger than every capacitor ever built; real ones live in the picofarad to microfarad range, so a negative exponent is the only physically possible answer. That reasoning alone eliminates two options and the 4.77 F.

C = (epsilon0 x epsilonr x A) / d. Substituting: (8.854 x 10-12 x 7 x 4 x 10-2) / 10-3. Numerator: 8.854 x 10-12 x 7 = 6.198 x 10-11, times 4 x 10-2 = 2.479 x 10-12. Dividing by 10-3 gives 2.478 x 10-9 F, that is about 2.5 nF. A capacitance in nF is the sanity check: a positive power of ten here would be an impossibly large capacitor.

4.32hardLecture slides, module 2, slide 17

An insulated conducting sphere of shell radius R carries charge Q, and its outer surface is an equipotential. Determine the potential of the outer surface and the capacitance of the shell.

  • ✓V = Q/(4 π ε-0 R) and C = 4 π ε-0 RCorrect, and the Q cancelling is the point: what remains depends only on the geometry.
  • ✗V = Q/(4 π ε-0 R²) and C = 4 π ε-0 R²Wrong. Potential uses a plain R, not R². R² belongs to the FIELD.
  • ✗V = QR/(4 π ε-0) and C = 4 π ε-0 / RWrong. R is in the denominator of the potential, not the numerator.
  • ✗V = Q/(4 π ε-0 R) and C = Q / (4 π ε-0 R)Wrong. This forgets to divide, so it never cancels the Q, and a capacitance cannot depend on the charge stored.
  • ✗V = 4 π ε-0 QR and C = Q/RWrong on both, and it inverts the roles of the constants.

For a conducting sphere all the charge sits on the surface and behaves, from outside, like a point charge at the centre. So the potential at the surface is V = Q/(4 π ε-0 R), which is just kQ/r with r = R. Then capacitance is C = Q/V, and the Q cancels: C = Q divided by Q/(4 π ε-0 R) = 4 π ε-0 R. Notice what is left. The charge has vanished, and the capacitance depends on the RADIUS alone, which is the whole lesson: capacitance is fixed by geometry. A larger sphere has a larger capacitance.

5Current, Resistance and Networks

Speed tip. Resistivity questions hide the area: a wire of radius r has A = πr², not r. Halving the radius quarters the area and quadruples the resistance.

5.1easyReference sheet R.4

A point where two or more conductors meet is a junction, also called a node. A closed conducting path is a {{1}}.

Answer:

A loop. The three words matter because Kirchhoff's two laws are stated in terms of them: the CURRENT law applies at a junction and conserves charge, while the VOLTAGE law applies around a loop and conserves energy. A branch is a single element such as a resistor or source.

5.2easyThe real test 1, question 12

Given a circuit where R1, R2 and R3 are in series with a voltage source of 48 V, such that R1 = 7 Ω, R2 = 6 Ω and R3 = 3 Ω, calculate the current that flows through R3?

  • ✓3 ACorrect. R total = 16 ohms, so I = 48/16 = 3 A, and that same current flows through R3.
  • ✗6 AWrong. This is 48/8, which no combination of these resistors gives.
  • ✗4 AWrong. This is 48/12, that is the sum of two of the three resistors.
  • ✗12 AWrong, and it is the trap: 48/3 uses R3 alone, ignoring that current is set by the WHOLE series loop.
  • ✗5 AWrong. No route to 5 A from 48 V and 16 ohms.
Background, so this is not just a formula

In a series circuit there is only one path, so every electron that leaves the source must pass through every component in turn. That means the CURRENT is the same everywhere, while the voltage divides itself among the resistors in proportion to their size. Parallel circuits are the mirror image: same voltage everywhere, current divides. Knowing which quantity is shared is most of circuit analysis.

Given
  • R₁ = 7 Ω, R₂ = 6 Ω, R₃ = 3 Ω, all in SERIES
  • V = 48 V

Find: The current through R₃, in amperes

Formula

R = R₁ + R₂ + R₃, then I =VR

Why this one. Series resistances add directly, because the current meets them one after the other and each takes its share of the voltage. Once the total resistance is known, Ohm's law gives the single current that flows round the whole loop, and that same current is what passes through R₃.

What each symbol means
  • R = the total series resistance, in ohms
  • V = the source voltage, in volts
  • I = the current, the SAME through every component in a series loop
The working, line by line
  1. Total resistance: R = 7 + 6 + 3
  2. R = 16 Ω
  3. Ohm's law for the whole loop: I = V ÷ R
  4. I = 48 ÷ 16
  5. I = 3 A
  6. Series means one path, so the current through R₃ is that same 3 A
Check it

The question singles out R₃, which invites you to do something extra with the 3 Ω, and there is nothing extra to do. In series the answer is identical for R₁, R₂ or R₃. Verify by adding the individual voltages: 3 × 7 = 21 V, 3 × 6 = 18 V, 3 × 3 = 9 V, and 21 + 18 + 9 = 48 V, the full supply. The voltages account for themselves exactly.

In series the resistances add and the SAME current flows through every component, so the answer for R3 is the answer for the whole loop. R = 7 + 6 + 3 = 16 ohms, and I = V/R = 48/16 = 3 A. Naming R3 specifically is the trap: it invites you to divide 48 by 3.

5.3easyReference sheet R.1

Electric current is charge per unit time, I =Δ QΔ t, and one ampere is one coulomb per {{1}}.

Answer:

One second. 1 A = 1 C/s, straight from the definition. It is why the CD-player question can go from a current and a time to a charge in one multiplication.

5.4easyReference sheet R.1

Ohm's law is V = IR, and one ohm is one volt per amp.

Answer: true

True on both. Cover the quantity you want and the formula rearranges itself: cover V and you see IR, cover I and you see V over R. The unit follows from the same relation, since R = V/I gives volts per amp.

5.5easyReference sheet R.1

Resistors in series simply add, and the same current flows through every one of them.

Answer: true

True on both, and the two facts are linked: there is only one path, so the current has nowhere else to go, and each resistor adds its own opposition to that single path. Test 1 question 12 depends on it, where naming R3 invites you to divide 48 by 3 when the answer is the whole loop's current.

5.6easyReference sheet R.4

The ampere is the unit of current, and it equals one coulomb per second.

Answer: true

True, and it is simply the definition I = Δ Q over Δ t read as units. The slides note that a 1 A current is fairly strong, and that phones and music players run on small fractions of an amp.

5.7easyReference sheet R.4

In circuit language, what is a BRANCH?

  • ✓A single element such as a resistor or a sourceCorrect. A branch is one element, such as a single resistor or a single source.
  • ✗A point where two or more conductors meetWrong. That is a junction or node.
  • ✗A closed conducting pathWrong. That is a loop.
  • ✗Any path of zero resistanceWrong. That is a short circuit.
  • ✗The whole circuitWrong. A circuit contains many branches.

A branch is one element. A junction, or node, is a meeting point, and a loop is a closed path. The three words are the vocabulary Kirchhoff's laws are written in: the current law works at junctions and the voltage law around loops.

5.8easyReference sheet R.4

A material is called {{1}} when it obeys Ohm's law, with its resistance constant at a fixed temperature.

Answer:

Ohmic. Metal wires and ordinary resistors are ohmic; semiconductors are the important exception the slides name, because their resistance falls as they warm. The temperature qualification matters: even a metal stops being ohmic if you let it heat up.

5.9easyLecture slides, module 3, slide 6

Which of the following situations has the greatest electric current? (a) the flow of 6 C in 3 s, (b) the flow of 3 C in 1 s, (c) a person walking 8 m in 4 s.

  • ✓Situation (b), at 3 ACorrect. 3 C in 1 s is 3 A, beating the 2 A of situation (a).
  • ✗Situation (a), at 2 AWrong.6 C3 s is 2 A, which is real current but less than 3 A.
  • ✗Situation (c), because the person is moving fastestWrong, and it is the trap. Speed is not current. A person is electrically neutral, so their net charge is essentially zero and the current is zero however fast they walk.
  • ✗All three carry the same currentWrong. The three come to 2 A, 3 A and zero.
  • ✗Situation (a), at 6 AWrong. 6 C is the charge, not the current; it must be divided by the 3 seconds.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Current is the rate at which charge flows past a point, which is charge divided by time. The word RATE is what matters: a large charge moving slowly can carry less current than a small charge moving quickly. And current is about CHARGE, so anything that is not charge, however fast it moves, carries no current at all.

Given
  • Situation (a): 6 C in 3 s
  • Situation (b): 3 C in 1 s
  • Situation (c): a person walking 8 m in 4 s

Find: Which situation has the greatest electric current

Formula

I =Qt

Why this one. Comparison questions are answered by computing each candidate on the same footing rather than by guessing from the raw numbers. Situation (a) has the larger charge, which invites the wrong answer, so the division must actually be done.

What each symbol means
  • I = the current, in amperes, where 1 A = 1 C s⁻¹
  • Q = the charge that flows, in coulombs
  • t = the time it takes, in seconds
The working, line by line
  1. Situation (a): I = 6 ÷ 3 = 2 A
  2. Situation (b): I = 3 ÷ 1 = 3 A
  3. Situation (c): a walking person moves mass, not charge, so I = 0
  4. Compare: 3 A is the largest
  5. The answer is situation (b), at 3 A
Check it

Situation (a) has twice the charge of (b) and still loses, which is the whole point of the question: current is a rate, so the time matters as much as the charge. Situation (c) is there to test whether you noticed that metres per second is a speed and not a current. It would only carry current if the walker were charged.

Current is charge per time, I = Δ Q over Δ t. (a) gives 6 C over 3 s = 2 A. (b) gives 3 C over 1 s = 3 A. (c) is zero, because a walking person carries essentially no NET charge, so moving them from place to place transports no charge at all. The greatest is therefore (b) at 3 A, and the smallest is (c) at zero.

5.10easyLecture slides, module 3, slide 23

A potential difference of 24 V is applied to a 150 ohm resistor. How much current flows through the resistor?

  • ✓0.16 ACorrect. 24 divided by 150.
  • ✗6.25 AWrong, and upside down: this is 150 over 24.
  • ✗3600 AWrong. This multiplies instead of dividing, which would be V times R.
  • ✗1.6 AWrong by a factor of ten.
  • ✗0.016 AWrong by a factor of ten the other way.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Ohm's law is the most-used relation in the whole of electricity, and it is worth seeing it as a statement about push and opposition. The voltage is the push, the resistance is the opposition, and the current is what results. Push harder and more flows; oppose more and less flows.

Given
  • V = 24 V
  • R = 150 Ω

Find: The current I through the resistor, in amperes

Formula

I =VR

Why this one. A voltage and a resistance are given and a current is wanted, which is Ohm's law arranged with I on the left. There is only one component, so no combining of resistances is needed first.

What each symbol means
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • V = the potential difference across the resistor, in volts
  • R = the resistance, in ohms
The working, line by line
  1. I = V ÷ R
  2. I = 24 ÷ 150
  3. I = 0.16 A
Check it

The resistance in ohms is much bigger than the voltage in volts, so the current must come out well BELOW one amp. That single glance rules out 6.25 A and 3600 A before any arithmetic. Among the survivors, 0.016 differs from 0.16 by a factor of ten, so count the decimal places carefully: 24 ÷ 150 is a bit more than 24 ÷ 240, which is 0.1.

Ohm's law, rearranged for the current: I = V over R = 24 over 150 = 0.16 A. Cover the quantity you want in V = IR and the formula rearranges itself.

5.11mediumReference sheet R.1

Resistance is R =ρ LA. Two copper wires carry the same current. Wire X is long and thin, wire Y is short and fat. Which has the greater resistance, and why?

  • ✓X, because resistance rises with length and falls with cross-sectional areaCorrect on both halves of the formula.
  • ✗Y, because a fatter wire has more material to push throughWrong, and backwards: more cross-section gives current more room, so less resistance.
  • ✗They are equal, since both are copperWrong. Same material means same ρ, but resistance also depends on shape.
  • ✗X, because copper resistivity depends on the shapeRight answer, wrong reason: resistivity does NOT depend on shape, only the resistance does.
  • ✗Y, because resistance rises with areaWrong. Resistance falls as area rises.

L is on top and A underneath, so long raises resistance and wide lowers it. The hook is long and thin resists, short and fat does not, and the water analogy makes it obvious: a long narrow hose fights the flow, a short wide one barely does. Note that ρ, the resistivity, is a property of the MATERIAL alone and is the same for both wires because both are copper.

5.12mediumReference sheet R.4

Resistivity, symbol ρ, is a material's intrinsic opposition to current, measured in ohm {{1}}.

Answer:

Ohm metres. Read the unit off R = ρ L over A: rearranged, ρ = RA over L, which is ohms times metres squared divided by metres, giving ohm metres. Insulators have large resistivities and conductors small ones.

5.13mediumReference sheet R.4

A short circuit is a path of zero resistance placed across part of a circuit. What does it do to the current?

  • ✓It carries all the current, and the equivalent resistance of that parallel section becomes zeroCorrect, and it follows directly from the parallel rule.
  • ✗It carries none of the currentWrong, and exactly backwards: it takes all of it.
  • ✗It splits the current equally with the other branchesWrong. Current divides in inverse proportion to resistance, so zero resistance takes everything.
  • ✗It stops the current entirelyWrong. It increases the current, which is the hazard.
  • ✗It doubles the total resistanceWrong. It reduces the resistance to zero.

Current takes the easiest path, and a parallel combination is always less than or equal to the smallest resistance in it, so a zero-resistance branch drags the whole section to zero. Everything in parallel with the short is bypassed and carries nothing. That is why a short circuit is dangerous: with almost no resistance left, the current rises until something melts.

5.14mediumReference sheet R.2

For V = IR, {{1}} the one you want and the formula rearranges itself.

Answer:

Cover it. Hiding V leaves I times R; hiding I leaves V over R; hiding R leaves V over I. It is a memory aid for rearranging under pressure, not a physical law.

5.15mediumReference sheet R.1

A wire is made longer and thinner. What happens to its resistance?

  • ✓It rises, because R =ρ LA means long and thin resistsCorrect on both counts, and both changes push the same way here.
  • ✗It falls, because a longer wire carries more currentWrong. Length is in the numerator, so more length means more resistance.
  • ✗It is unchanged, because resistivity is a property of the materialWrong. Resistivity is fixed by the material, but RESISTANCE also depends on shape.
  • ✗It falls, because a smaller area means less material to resistWrong, and reversed: a smaller cross-section squeezes the current and raises the resistance.
  • ✗It rises only if the material is a semiconductorWrong. The geometry argument holds for any ohmic material.

R = ρ L/A. Length is on top, so a longer wire resists more; area is underneath, so a thinner wire resists more too. Long and thin resists; short and fat does not. Resistivity ρ is the material property and does not change with shape.

5.16mediumReference sheet R.2

The hook for R =ρ LA is that long and {{1}} resists, while short and fat does not.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

Resistance is not a property of a material alone; it is a property of a particular PIECE of it. Resistivity ρ is the material's own contribution, the same for any piece of copper. Turn that into the resistance of an actual wire and two pieces of geometry enter: how far the current must travel, and how wide a road it has to travel on. A long thin wire is a long narrow road, and traffic crawls.

Find: The word that completes: long and ___ resists, while short and fat does not

Formula

R =ρLA

Why this one. The formula is the sentence. L is on top, so more length means more resistance. A is underneath, so more area means less resistance. Long and thin puts both factors in the unfavourable direction at once, which is why it resists most.

What each symbol means
  • R = the resistance of this particular piece, in ohms
  • ρ = the resistivity, a property of the MATERIAL, in Ω m
  • L = the length of the wire, in metres, on TOP
  • A = the cross-sectional area, in m², UNDERNEATH
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is THIN.
  2. Length on top: longer means more resistance.
  3. Area underneath: thinner means less area, so more resistance.
  4. Long and thin therefore resists the most, short and fat the least.
  5. Why not cold: cooling a metal actually LOWERS its resistance, which is the opposite direction.
  6. Why not straight: bending a wire does not change its length or its cross-section, so it changes nothing.
Check it

Guard the area, because that is where the marks actually go. For a round wire A = πr², so halving the radius does not halve the area, it quarters it, and therefore QUADRUPLES the resistance. Any question that gives you a radius or a diameter rather than an area is testing exactly that step.

Thin. Length on top and area underneath, so both being unfavourable raises the resistance.

5.17mediumReference sheet R.2

The equivalent resistance of a parallel combination is always less than the smallest resistor in it.

Answer: true

True, and it is the fastest sanity check in circuit work. Every extra parallel branch gives the current another route, so the combination always conducts better than the best single branch.

5.18mediumReference sheet R.1

What does each of Kirchhoff’s laws express?

  • ✓The current law conserves charge at junctions, and the voltage law conserves energy around loopsCorrect. Charge cannot pile up at a junction, and a charge going once round a loop must gain and lose equal energy.
  • ✗The current law conserves energy, and the voltage law conserves chargeWrong, and swapped.
  • ✗Both conserve chargeWrong. Only the current law is about charge.
  • ✗Both conserve energyWrong. Only the voltage law is about energy.
  • ✗Neither is a conservation law; both are experimental rules with no deeper basisWrong. Both follow from conservation principles.

KCL is charge, KVL is energy. Current in equals current out at a junction because charge cannot pile up there; the voltages around a closed loop sum to zero because a charge returning to its starting point must have gained and lost equal energy.

5.19mediumReference sheet R.2

Kirchhoff’s current law is about charge and his voltage law is about {{1}}.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

Kirchhoff's two laws look like circuit rules but they are conservation laws in disguise, which is why they are always true and never approximations. Charge cannot be created or destroyed, so it cannot pile up at a junction: whatever flows in must flow out. Energy cannot be created either, so a charge carried all the way round a loop and back to where it started must have gained exactly as much as it lost.

Find: The word that completes: the current law is about charge and the voltage law is about ___

The working, line by line
  1. The answer is ENERGY.
  2. The current law, at a junction: ΣI in = ΣI out, which is conservation of CHARGE.
  3. The voltage law, round a loop: Σ emf = Σ IR, which is conservation of ENERGY.
  4. Why not charge: that is already the current law, and the pair would then say the same thing twice.
  5. Why not power or resistance: neither is a conserved quantity, and conservation is the whole basis of both laws.
Check it

Hold it as one sentence: junctions conserve charge, loops conserve energy. Then use the shape of the circuit to pick the law. A junction, meaning a place where wires meet, calls for the current law. A closed loop you can trace with a finger calls for the voltage law. Most problems need one of each.

Energy. Junctions conserve charge, loops conserve energy: two words that keep the pair straight.

5.20mediumThe real test 2, question 13

A 10 Ω resistor is connected to a battery of internal resistance 5 Ω. The p.d. across the terminals of the battery is 2 V. Estimate the current flowing the resistor after a time of 2 seconds.

  • ✓0.2 ACorrect. I = V/R = 2/10 = 0.2 A, using the terminal p.d. across the external resistor.
  • ✗0.1 AWrong. This is 2/(10 + 5 + 5) or similar double counting of the internal resistance.
  • ✗0.3 AWrong. No route to 0.3 A from 2 V and 10 ohms.
  • ✗0.4 AWrong. This would need 4 V across the resistor.
  • ✗0.5 AWrong. This uses the internal resistance as though it were the external one: 2/5 wrongly halved, or 2 V across 4 ohms.
Background, so this is not just a formula

The terminal p.d. of a cell is not measured inside the cell, it is what appears at the terminals, and that is the same voltage that sits across whatever is connected to them. So the moment a question gives you the terminal p.d. and the external resistor, Ohm's law on that resistor alone gives the current. The internal resistance has already had its effect: it is the reason the terminal p.d. is only 2 V instead of the full emf.

Given
  • R = 10 Ω (the external resistor)
  • r = 5 Ω (internal resistance of the battery)
  • V = 2 V (the p.d. ACROSS THE TERMINALS)
  • t = 2 s (the time given in the question)

Find: The current I flowing through the 10 Ω resistor, in amperes

Formula

I =VR

Why this one. The voltage given is the terminal p.d., and the terminal p.d. is precisely what is across the external resistor, so the current follows from Ohm's law on that resistor by itself. The 5 Ω is a distractor: dividing by 15 would answer a different question, the one where the EMF was 2 V rather than the terminal p.d. Reading which voltage you have been handed is the whole difficulty.

What each symbol means
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • V = the terminal p.d., in volts, equal to the p.d. across R
  • R = the external resistance, in ohms
The working, line by line
  1. I = V ÷ R
  2. I = 2 ÷ 10
  3. I = 0.2 A
Check it

The 2 seconds never enters the calculation. In a steady d.c. circuit the current is the same at every instant, so a time can only matter if the question asks for charge (Q = It) or energy. It asks for current, so the time is there to be ignored. Notice too that dividing by 15 would give 0.133 A, which is not among the options, further confirming the reading.

The terminal p.d. is the voltage that appears ACROSS the external resistor, so Ohm's law needs nothing else: I = V/R = 2/10 = 0.2 A. Two pieces of the stem are deliberate distractions: the internal resistance is already accounted for by quoting the terminal p.d. rather than the emf, and the time of 2 seconds does not enter a steady-current calculation at all.

5.21mediumReference sheet R.1

Resistance from resistivity is R =ρ LA, so a wire that is long and thin has a {{1}} resistance than one that is short and fat.

Answer:

Higher. L is on top so length raises it, and A is underneath so width lowers it. The water-hose picture makes it stick: a long narrow hose fights the flow, a short wide one hardly does.

5.22mediumReference sheet R.1

Resistors in parallel combine reciprocally, and the answer is always {{1}} than the smallest resistor in the group.

Answer:

Less. Adding another path can only make it easier for current to flow, never harder. It is a free check on every parallel answer: if your total is not below the smallest, you forgot to flip the reciprocal back at the end.

5.23mediumReference sheet R.1

For exactly two resistors in parallel, R equivalent = R1 R2 over R1 + R2, product over sum.

Answer: true

True, and it is worth memorising because two-resistor parallel groups appear constantly. It is only a shortcut for TWO: with three or more you must go back to the reciprocal sum. Checking it on 100 and 200 ohms gives 20000 over 300 = 66.7 ohms, duly below the smaller.

5.24mediumReference sheet R.1

In the voltage divider V1 = R1 over R total, times V, the resistor on top is the {{1}} one.

Answer:

The WANTED one, the resistor whose voltage you are after. That is the opposite of the current divider, where the OTHER resistor goes on top. Getting them the wrong way round is the single commonest circuit error, and both wrong answers usually appear as options.

5.25mediumReference sheet R.1

Kirchhoff's current law says the currents into a junction equal the currents out of it, which is conservation of {{1}}.

Answer:

Charge. Nothing accumulates at a junction, so whatever arrives must leave. The partner law, the voltage law around a loop, is conservation of ENERGY. KCL is charge, KVL is energy, and keeping the pair straight is what makes the two-loop problems tractable.

5.26mediumReference sheet R.1

Kirchhoff's voltage law says the sum of the potential differences around any closed loop is zero, and it expresses conservation of energy.

Answer: true

True. Go all the way round a loop and you arrive back where you started, so the total change in potential must be nothing, exactly as walking a closed path on a hillside returns you to your starting height. The rises across sources balance the drops across resistors.

5.27mediumReference sheet R.4

A semiconductor sits between a conductor and an insulator, and unlike a metal its resistance {{1}} as it heats.

Answer:

Falls. Heating a metal makes its atoms jiggle and collide with electrons, raising resistance; heating a semiconductor frees more charge carriers, which wins. That backwards behaviour is why semiconductors are the standing exception to Ohm's law in this course.

5.28mediumReference sheet R.4

A short circuit has zero resistance, so it carries all the current and bypasses everything in parallel with it.

Answer: true

True. A parallel combination can never exceed its smallest member, so a zero-resistance branch pulls the whole section to zero and everything beside it is bypassed. With almost no resistance left the current climbs until something fails, which is why a short circuit is a hazard rather than a curiosity.

5.29mediumLecture slides, module 3, slide 8

The disk drive in a portable CD player is connected to a battery that supplies it with a current of 0.22 A. How many electrons pass through the disk drive in 4.5 s? Recall that the charge of an electron has magnitude e = 1.60 x 10-19 C.

  • ✓6.2 x 1018 electronsCorrect. 0.99 C divided by 1.60 x 10-19 C per electron.
  • ✗0.99 x 1018 electronsWrong. 0.99 is the CHARGE in coulombs, the intermediate answer, not the number of electrons.
  • ✗1.6 x 1019 electronsWrong. This inverts the electronic charge instead of dividing by it.
  • ✗3.1 x 1018 electronsWrong, and about half: this comes from using 3.2 x 10-19 for the electronic charge.
  • ✗9.9 x 1019 electronsWrong by a factor of ten, a slip in the exponent when dividing.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Current is a flow of electrons, but it is measured in coulombs per second rather than electrons per second, because an electron carries an absurdly small charge. Converting between the two is always a two-step job: first find how many coulombs flowed, then ask how many electrons that many coulombs is. Trying to do it in one step is where the error creeps in.

Given
  • I = 0.22 A
  • t = 4.5 s
  • e = 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ C, the charge on one electron

Find: The number of electrons n that pass through the drive

Formula

Q = It, then n = Q / e

Why this one. Two formulas because there are two conversions. The first turns a current and a time into a charge, which is just the definition of current rearranged. The second turns that charge into a count, using the fact that charge comes in fixed lumps of size e. Neither can be skipped.

What each symbol means
  • Q = the total charge that flowed, in coulombs
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • t = the time, in seconds
  • n = the number of electrons, a pure count
  • e = the elementary charge, 1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹ C
The working, line by line
  1. Step one, the charge: Q = I × t
  2. Q = 0.22 × 4.5 = 0.99 C
  3. Step two, the count: n = Q ÷ e
  4. n = 0.99 ÷ (1.60 × 10⁻¹⁹)
  5. Front numbers: 0.99 ÷ 1.60 = 0.619
  6. Dividing by 10⁻¹⁹ multiplies by 10¹⁹: n = 0.619 × 10¹⁹
  7. n = 6.19 × 10¹⁸ ≈ 6.2 × 10¹⁸ electrons
Check it

The option 0.99 × 10¹⁸ is a trap for stopping after step one and then decorating the charge with a power of ten. Notice the number 6.2 × 10¹⁸: it is the same figure that appears in the charge-quantisation question, because 6.24 × 10¹⁸ electrons is what makes one coulomb. Since 0.99 C is very nearly one coulomb, the count had to come out near that value.

Two steps. First the charge: Δ Q = I Δ t = 0.22 x 4.5 = 0.99 C. Then the count: N = Δ Q over e = 0.99 / 1.60 x 10-19 = 6.2 x 1018 electrons. Worth memorising, because Test 1 question 10 hands you 6.2 x 1018 electrons and asks for the charge, which is this same calculation run backwards to 0.99 C.

5.30mediumLecture slides, module 3, slide 18

A battery with an emf of 1.5 V delivers a current of 0.44 A to a flashlight bulb for 64 s. Find the charge that passes through the circuit and the work done by the battery.

  • ✓28 C and 42 JCorrect. 28 C from I times t, then 42 J from Q times emf.
  • ✗28 C and 19 JWrong on the work: this divides by the emf instead of multiplying.
  • ✗0.44 C and 0.66 JWrong. This forgets to multiply by the 64 seconds.
  • ✗96 C and 144 JWrong. This uses a current or a time larger than the ones given.
  • ✗42 C and 28 JWrong, and it swaps the two answers over.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A battery does work on every coulomb it pushes round the circuit, and the emf says how much: 1.5 V means 1.5 joules given to each coulomb. So finding the total work is again a two-step job. First find how many coulombs went round, then multiply by the joules each one received. The energy did not come from nowhere; it came out of the chemicals inside the cell.

Given
  • emf = 1.5 V
  • I = 0.44 A
  • t = 64 s

Find: The charge that passes, in coulombs, and the work done, in joules

Formula

Q = It, then W = QV

Why this one. The current and the time give the charge, because current is charge per second. The charge and the voltage then give the energy, because voltage is energy per coulomb. Each formula is a definition read the other way round, which is why they chain together so neatly.

What each symbol means
  • Q = the charge that circulates, in coulombs
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • t = the time, in seconds
  • W = the work done by the battery, in joules
  • V = the emf, the energy given per coulomb, in volts
The working, line by line
  1. Charge first: Q = I × t
  2. Q = 0.44 × 64 = 28.16 ≈ 28 C
  3. Work next: W = Q × V
  4. W = 28.16 × 1.5
  5. W = 42.24 ≈ 42 J
Check it

One option offers the same two numbers the other way round, 42 C and 28 J, so read your own labels before choosing. A quick guard: the emf is 1.5, which is more than 1, so the work in joules must be LARGER than the charge in coulombs. 42 is larger than 28, so the pairing is the right way round.

Charge first: Δ Q = I Δ t = 0.44 x 64 = 28 C. Then work: W = Δ Q times emf = 28 x 1.5 = 42 J. The insight the slide draws is that the more charge a battery moves, and the greater its emf, the more work it does, which is why a 12 V car battery delivering several amps does far more work than this 1.5 V flashlight cell.

5.31mediumLecture slides, module 3, slide 55

A 20 V source is applied across R1 = 15 kilohms and R2 = 10 kilohms in series. What is the voltage across R2?

  • ✓8.0 VCorrect. Ten twenty-fifths of 20 V.
  • ✗12.0 VWrong, and it is the voltage across R1: this puts the OTHER resistor on top, which is the current divider's rule, not the voltage divider's.
  • ✗10.0 VWrong. 10 is the resistance in kilohms, not the voltage.
  • ✗20.0 VWrong. That is the whole source voltage, which is shared between the two resistors.
  • ✗1.33 VWrong. This divides rather than taking the ratio of the resistances.
Background, so this is not just a formula

When resistors sit in series they share the supply voltage between them, and they share it in proportion to their resistance. The bigger resistor takes the bigger share, because the same current passes through both and V = IR. This is called a voltage divider, and it is how a circuit produces a smaller voltage from a larger one without a transformer.

Given
  • V = 20 V
  • R₁ = 15 kΩ
  • R₂ = 10 kΩ, in series with R₁

Find: The voltage across R₂, in volts

Formula

V₂ =V R₂(R₁ + R₂)

Why this one. The divider rule is the same physics as finding the current and multiplying by R₂, but written as one line so there is only one chance to slip. Read it as a fraction: R₂ over the total is the SHARE that R₂ takes, and that share of the supply voltage is what appears across it.

What each symbol means
  • V₂ = the voltage across R₂, in volts
  • V = the total supply voltage, in volts
  • R₂ = the resistance you want the voltage across
  • R₁ + R₂ = the total series resistance
  • 1 kΩ = 1000 Ω, but the k cancels here so it can stay
The working, line by line
  1. Total resistance: R₁ + R₂ = 15 + 10 = 25 kΩ
  2. The share taken by R₂: 10 ÷ 25 = 0.4
  3. V₂ = 20 × 0.4
  4. V₂ = 8.0 V
Check it

R₂ is the smaller resistor, so it must take the SMALLER share, meaning under half of 20 V. 8.0 V is under 10, so it leans the right way; the option 12.0 V is the share belonging to R₁, offered for anyone who put the wrong resistance on top. Check the pair adds up: 8 + 12 = 20 V, the full supply, with nothing unaccounted for. Note the kilohms never had to be converted, because the same unit appears on top and bottom and cancels.

The voltage divider puts the WANTED resistor on top: V2 = R2 over R total, times V = 10 over 25, times 20 = 8.0 V. Note the total resistance is 25 kilohms, and that the kilohms cancel in the ratio so there is no need to convert them.

5.32mediumLecture slides, module 3, slide 66

A 20 mA source feeds R1 = 100 ohms in parallel with R2 = 200 ohms. What is the value of the current in R1?

  • ✓13.3 mACorrect. Two thirds of 20 mA.
  • ✗6.7 mAWrong. 6.7 mA is the current in R2, the larger resistor's smaller share.
  • ✗20 mAWrong. 20 mA is the total from the source, before dividing.
  • ✗26.7 mAWrong, and impossible: a branch cannot carry more than the total entering the junction.
  • ✗10 mAWrong. The split would only be equal if the two resistors were equal.

Current divider, other resistor on top: I1 = R2 over R1 + R2, times I total = 200 over 300, times 20 = 13.3 mA. R1 is the smaller resistor so it takes the larger share, and the remaining 6.7 mA flows through R2.

5.33hardReference sheet R.4

What happens to the resistance of a semiconductor as it heats, and what is doping?

  • ✓Its resistance FALLS as it heats, and doping is adding impurities to make it conductCorrect on both, and the falling resistance is what makes it an exception to Ohm's law.
  • ✗Its resistance RISES as it heats, and doping is adding impurities to make it conductWrong on the temperature. That is how a METAL behaves.
  • ✗Its resistance falls as it heats, and doping is removing impurities to purify itWrong on doping. It ADDS impurities on purpose.
  • ✗Its resistance is unaffected by temperatureWrong. Temperature is precisely what changes it.
  • ✗Its resistance rises as it heats, and doping is a heat treatmentWrong on both halves: the resistance falls rather than rises, and doping is the deliberate addition of impurities, not a heat treatment.

A semiconductor sits between conductor and insulator, and it behaves BACKWARDS from a metal: heating a metal wire raises its resistance, because the atoms jiggle more and collide with electrons, but heating a semiconductor FREES more charge carriers and its resistance falls. Doping means deliberately adding impurities, such as one arsenic atom per million silicon atoms, which frees electrons and makes the silicon conduct. That is an n-type semiconductor, n for the negative electrons carrying the current.

5.34hardReference sheet R.1
