You combine three resistors in parallel and get a value LARGER than the smallest of them. What has happened?

  • ✓A mistake: parallel resistance is always less than the smallest, so the reciprocal was not flipped backCorrect, and this is a free check worth running on every parallel answer.
  • ✗Nothing: parallel resistance can be larger than the smallest resistorWrong. It is impossible.
  • ✗Nothing, provided the resistors are not equalWrong. The rule holds whether or not the resistors are equal.
  • ✗The resistors must have been in series insteadWrong. Series is where resistances add; the question specifies parallel.
  • ✗The supply voltage was too lowWrong. The combination does not depend on the supply.

Adding a parallel path always gives the current somewhere else to go, so the combination must conduct better than any single branch, which means a resistance below the smallest. Getting a larger value almost always means stopping at 1/R without inverting to get R.

5.35hardReference sheet R.1

In the current divider for two resistors, the resistor that appears on top of the fraction is the OTHER one, not the one whose current you want.

Answer: true

True, and it is the mirror image of the voltage divider. I1 =R2R1 + R2, times I total. The physical sense is that current prefers the easier path, so the SMALLER resistor takes the LARGER share, which only works out if the other resistance sits on top.

5.36hardLecture slides, module 3, slide 37

A circuit consists of three resistors connected in series to a 24.0 V battery. The current in the circuit is 0.0320 A. Given that R1 = 250.0 ohms and R2 = 150.0 ohms, find the resistance of R3.

  • ✓350 ohmsCorrect. 750 total − the 400 already accounted for.
  • ✗750 ohmsWrong. 750 ohms is the total of all three, not R3 alone.
  • ✗400 ohmsWrong. 400 ohms is R1 + R2, the part you subtract.
  • ✗250 ohmsWrong. That is R1 repeated.
  • ✗1150 ohmsWrong. This adds 750 to R2 rather than subtracting.
Background, so this is not just a formula

In a series circuit the same current passes through every component, because there is only one path for it to take. That makes the whole string behave like one big resistor whose value is the simple total. So if you know the supply voltage and the current, Ohm's law hands you that total, and anything missing from the string can then be found by subtraction.

Given
  • V = 24.0 V
  • I = 0.0320 A
  • R₁ = 250.0 Ω
  • R₂ = 150.0 Ω
  • All three resistors are in SERIES

Find: The resistance of R₃, in ohms

Formula

R =VI, then R₃ = R − R₁ − R₂

Why this one. Working backwards is the trick here. Ohm's law applied to the whole circuit gives the total resistance, because in series the current through the whole loop is the current the battery supplies. Once the total is known, the unknown resistor is whatever is left after removing the two you were told.

What each symbol means
  • R = the total resistance of the series string, in ohms
  • V = the battery voltage, in volts
  • I = the current, the SAME everywhere in a series loop
  • R₁, R₂, R₃ = the three individual resistances
The working, line by line
  1. Total resistance from Ohm's law: R = V ÷ I
  2. R = 24.0 ÷ 0.0320
  3. R = 750 Ω
  4. Subtract the known ones: R₃ = 750 − 250 − 150
  5. 750 − 250 = 500
  6. R₃ = 500 − 150 = 350 Ω
Check it

The 750 is deliberately offered as an option, so do not stop at the total. Verify forwards in one line: 250 + 150 + 350 = 750, and 24 ÷ 750 = 0.032 A, which is the current given. That closes the loop and proves the answer.

First the whole circuit: R equivalent = emf over I = 24.0 over 0.0320 = 750 ohms. In series the resistances simply add, so R3 = 750 − 250 − 150 = 350 ohms. Checking with the potential differences: V1 = 0.0320 x 250 = 8.00 V, V2 = 0.0320 x 150 = 4.80 V, V3 = 0.0320 x 350 = 11.2 V, and 8.00 + 4.80 + 11.2 = 24.0 V, the battery voltage exactly.

5.37hardLecture slides, module 3, slide 46

Consider a circuit with three resistors, R1 = 250.0 ohms, R2 = 150.0 ohms and R3 = 350.0 ohms, connected in parallel with a 24.0 V battery. Find the total current supplied by the battery.

  • ✓0.325 ACorrect, and it equals the three branch currents added together.
  • ✗0.0320 AWrong. 0.0320 A is the current when these same resistors are in SERIES, which is the neighbouring example.
  • ✗0.0960 AWrong. That is the branch current through R1 alone,24250.
  • ✗0.160 AWrong. That is the branch current through R2 alone,24150.
  • ✗0.0686 AWrong. That is the branch current through R3 alone,24350.
Background, so this is not just a formula

In parallel, every resistor is connected straight across the battery, so every one of them gets the FULL supply voltage. They do not share it. What they share is the current, each taking as much as its own resistance allows, and the battery has to supply the total. That is why adding more parallel branches makes the circuit draw more current, not less.

Given
  • R₁ = 250.0 Ω, R₂ = 150.0 Ω, R₃ = 350.0 Ω, all in PARALLEL
  • V = 24.0 V across all of them

Find: The total current supplied by the battery, in amperes

Formula

I = I₁ + I₂ + I₃, where each I = V / R

Why this one. There are two valid routes and this is the safer one. You could combine the resistances with reciprocals first and then divide once, but that means three reciprocals and a final flip, which is four chances to slip. Finding each branch current separately and adding uses only division and addition, and it also shows which branch carries most, which is often the next part of the question.

What each symbol means
  • I = the total current drawn from the battery, in amperes
  • I₁, I₂, I₃ = the currents in the three branches
  • V = 24.0 V, the same across EVERY branch in parallel
  • R₁, R₂, R₃ = the branch resistances, in ohms
The working, line by line
  1. Branch one: I₁ = 24.0 ÷ 250.0 = 0.0960 A
  2. Branch two: I₂ = 24.0 ÷ 150.0 = 0.1600 A
  3. Branch three: I₃ = 24.0 ÷ 350.0 = 0.0686 A
  4. Add them: 0.0960 + 0.1600 = 0.2560
  5. 0.2560 + 0.0686 = 0.3246
  6. I ≈ 0.325 A
Check it

Every individual branch current is offered as a wrong option, so make sure you added rather than stopping at one of them. Two guards on the result: the total must be LARGER than the biggest single branch current, and here 0.325 comfortably exceeds 0.160. And compare with the series version of the same three resistors, which drew only 0.032 A, ten times less. Parallel always draws more.

1 over R equivalent = 1/250 + 1/150 + 1/350 = 0.01352, so R equivalent = 73.96 ohms, and I = 24.0 over 73.96 = 0.325 A. Two free checks: 73.96 ohms is LESS than the smallest resistor, 150, as a parallel combination must be. And the branch currents 0.0960 + 0.160 + 0.0686 add back to 0.325 A, as they must.

5.38hardLecture slides, module 3, slide 51

The emf of the battery is 12 V and each resistor has a resistance of 450 ohms. Two of the resistors are in series with one another, and that pair is in parallel with the third. Find the current supplied by the battery.

  • ✓0.040 ACorrect. 12 V across an equivalent 300 ohms.
  • ✗0.0267 AWrong. This uses 450 ohms as the whole circuit.
  • ✗0.0089 AWrong. This treats all three as being in series, giving 1350 ohms.
  • ✗0.080 AWrong. This halves the resistance once too often.
  • ✗0.027 AWrong, and a near miss on the 450 ohm error.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Most real circuits are neither purely series nor purely parallel, but a mixture, and the way through is always the same: find a group that is clearly one or the other, replace it with a single equivalent resistor, and repeat until only one resistor is left. Work from the inside out, and never try to see the whole circuit in one go.

Given
  • emf = 12 V
  • Each resistor is 450 Ω
  • Two of them are in series with each other
  • That pair is in parallel with the third

Find: The current supplied by the battery, in amperes

Formula

R =(R₁R₂)(R₁ + R₂)

Why this one. The series pair goes first, because it is the innermost group: two resistors one after the other simply add, giving 900 Ω. That 900 Ω branch and the remaining 450 Ω branch span the same two points, so they are in parallel, and the product-over-sum form is the quickest way to combine exactly two resistors. Ohm's law then finishes it.

What each symbol means
  • R₁, R₂ = the two BRANCHES being combined in parallel, here 900 Ω and 450 Ω
  • R = their combined resistance, always SMALLER than either branch
  • I = the current from the battery, in amperes
The working, line by line
  1. Series pair first: 450 + 450 = 900 Ω
  2. Now parallel with the third: R = (900 × 450) ÷ (900 + 450)
  3. Top: 900 × 450 = 405000
  4. Bottom: 900 + 450 = 1350
  5. R = 405000 ÷ 1350 = 300 Ω
  6. Ohm's law: I = V ÷ R
  7. I = 12 ÷ 300 = 0.040 A
Check it

The parallel result must be smaller than both branches, and 300 is duly under 450 and under 900. Two wrong routes appear in the options: treating all three as one series string gives 1350 Ω and a current of 0.0089 A, while treating all three as parallel gives 150 Ω and 0.080 A. The correct answer sits between them, which is exactly where a mixed circuit should land.

Work outwards from the innermost group. The two in series give R + R = 2R = 900 ohms. That 900 in parallel with the remaining 450 gives 1 over R equivalent = 1/450 + 1/900 = 3/900, so R equivalent = 300 ohms, which is two thirds of 450. Then I = 12 over 300 = 0.040 A. The insight is that three 450 ohm resistors together come to only 300 ohms, less than one of them, because the parallel step dominates.

5.39hardLecture slides, module 3, slide 57

A 20 V source is applied across R1 = 470 ohms and R2 = 330 ohms in series. Using the voltage divider rule to find V1 and V2, find the total power dissipated.

  • ✓0.5 WCorrect. 0.29 + 0.21.
  • ✗0.29 WWrong. 0.29 W is the power in R1 alone.
  • ✗0.21 WWrong. 0.21 W is the power in R2 alone.
  • ✗1.0 WWrong. This is double the answer, from adding each power twice.
  • ✗0.08 WWrong. This uses the wrong voltage across each resistor.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Power is the rate at which a resistor turns electrical energy into heat, and it has three forms, P = VI, P = I²R and P = V² ÷ R. They are the same equation with Ohm's law substituted in, so all three always agree. Choose whichever one matches what you have been given, since that avoids an intermediate step and an intermediate chance to slip.

Given
  • V = 20 V
  • R₁ = 470 Ω
  • R₂ = 330 Ω, in series

Find: The total power dissipated, in watts

Formula

P =V²R

Why this one. The supply voltage and the total resistance are both known, so this form gets there in one step. There is a shortcut worth seeing: the TOTAL power can be found from the total resistance directly, without ever computing V₁ and V₂ separately, because the two resistors share the same current and the powers simply add.

What each symbol means
  • P = the total power dissipated as heat, in watts
  • V = the supply voltage, in volts, and it is SQUARED
  • R = the total series resistance, in ohms
The working, line by line
  1. Total resistance: R = 470 + 330 = 800 Ω
  2. P = V² ÷ R
  3. V² = 20² = 400
  4. P = 400 ÷ 800
  5. P = 0.5 W
Check it

Verify the long way in two lines to prove the shortcut. The current is I = 20 ÷ 800 = 0.025 A, so V₁ = 0.025 × 470 = 11.75 V and V₂ = 0.025 × 330 = 8.25 V, which correctly add to 20 V. Then P₁ = 11.75 × 0.025 = 0.294 W and P₂ = 8.25 × 0.025 = 0.206 W, and those add to 0.5 W. Both of those partial powers are sitting in the option list, so make sure you gave the TOTAL.

The total resistance is 800 ohms. V1 = 470 over 800, times 20 = 11.75 V, and V2 = 330 over 800, times 20 = 8.25 V, and those two add back to 20 V as a check. Then P = V² over R for each: P1 = 11.75 squared over 470 = 0.29 W, P2 = 8.25 squared over 330 = 0.21 W. Total 0.5 W.

5.40hardLecture slides, module 3, slide 62

R1 is a 2.2 kilohm resistor in parallel with R2, which is 4.7 kilohms. If the total current into the resistors is 8.0 mA, what is the current in R1?

  • ✓5.45 mACorrect, and it is the larger share because R1 is the smaller resistor.
  • ✗2.55 mAWrong. 2.55 mA is the current in R2, found by putting R1 on top instead.
  • ✗4.0 mAWrong. The current only splits equally when the resistors are equal.
  • ✗8.0 mAWrong. 8.0 mA is the total entering the pair, before it divides.
  • ✗1.70 mAWrong. No route to this from these numbers.

The current divider puts the OTHER resistor on top, which is the opposite of the voltage divider: I1 = R2 over R1 + R2, times I total = 4.7 over 6.9, times 8.0 = 5.45 mA. The remaining 2.55 mA goes through R2, and the two add back to 8.0 mA. Notice the LARGER resistor carries the SMALLER current, which is the sanity check for every current divider.

5.41hardLecture slides, module 3, slide 67

A 20 mA source feeds R1 = 100 ohms in parallel with R2 = 200 ohms. Find the voltage across R2.

  • ✓1.33 VCorrect, whichever of the two routes you take.
  • ✗4.0 VWrong. This puts the whole 20 mA through the 200 ohm resistor, but the current divides.
  • ✗0.67 VWrong. This uses the 100 ohm resistor with the wrong branch current.
  • ✗0 VWrong. Only a short circuit of zero resistance would have no voltage across it.
  • ✗2.0 VWrong. This uses 100 ohms with the whole source current.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A current source is the mirror image of a voltage source: it insists on pushing a fixed current out, and lets the voltage settle wherever the circuit requires. So the reasoning runs the other way round from usual. Instead of starting with a voltage and finding the current, you start with the current, find the resistance it faces, and multiply.

Given
  • I = 20 mA = 0.020 A, from a current source
  • R₁ = 100 Ω and R₂ = 200 Ω, in PARALLEL

Find: The voltage across R₂, in volts

Formula

R =(R₁ R₂)(R₁ + R₂)

Why this one. The whole 20 mA has to pass through the parallel combination, so the voltage that appears across it is the source current times the combined resistance. And because the two resistors are in parallel, that same voltage sits across BOTH of them, so once you have it for the pair you have it for R₂ without any further work.

What each symbol means
  • R = the combined parallel resistance, in ohms
  • R₁, R₂ = the two resistors, in ohms
  • I = the source current, in amperes
  • V = the voltage across the pair, which is the voltage across each
The working, line by line
  1. Convert the current: 20 mA = 0.020 A
  2. Combine in parallel: R = (100 × 200) ÷ (100 + 200)
  3. Top: 100 × 200 = 20000
  4. Bottom: 100 + 200 = 300
  5. R = 20000 ÷ 300 = 66.67 Ω
  6. V = I × R = 0.020 × 66.67
  7. V = 1.33 V
Check it

In parallel the voltage is common, so the answer for R₂ is identical to the answer for R₁; if you found yourself splitting the current first, you took a longer road to the same place. Guard the parallel step: 66.67 Ω is smaller than both 100 and 200, as any parallel combination must be. Using 300 Ω instead, by adding as if in series, would give 6 V, and using only R₂ would give 4 V, which is duly offered as a trap.

Two routes, and both give the same answer. Either combine first: R equivalent = 100 x 200 over 300 = 66.7 ohms, so V = 20 mA x 66.7 = 1.33 V. Or use the branch current from the previous question: the 6.7 mA through R2 gives V = 6.7 mA x 200 = 1.33 V. Parallel components share the same voltage, so this is the voltage across R1 as well.

5.42hardLecture slides, module 3, slide 92

A 10 A source feeds an 8 ohm resistor carrying I1 in parallel with a 4 ohm resistor carrying I2. Using Kirchhoff's laws, find I1 and the voltage across the pair.

  • ✓I1 = 3.33 A and V = 26.67 VCorrect, and both branches agree on the voltage.
  • ✗I1 = 6.67 A and V = 26.67 VWrong. 6.67 A is I2, the current in the 4 ohm resistor.
  • ✗I1 = 5.00 A and V = 40 VWrong. The current only splits equally when the resistances are equal.
  • ✗I1 = 3.33 A and V = 13.3 VWrong on the voltage: 13.3 V would need a 4 ohm resistor carrying I1.
  • ✗I1 = 10 A and V = 80 VWrong. The whole 10 A cannot flow through one branch when there are two paths.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Kirchhoff's two laws are just conservation restated for circuits. The junction law says charge cannot pile up at a junction, so whatever flows in must flow out. The loop law says energy cannot be created going round a loop, so the voltage rises and drops must cancel. For two resistors fed by a current source, both laws are visible at once: the currents split at the junction, and both resistors share one voltage.

Given
  • Source current: 10 A total
  • R₁ = 8 Ω carrying I₁
  • R₂ = 4 Ω carrying I₂
  • The two resistors are in parallel

Find: The current I₁ in the 8 Ω resistor, and the voltage V across the pair

Formula

R =(R₁ R₂)(R₁ + R₂)

Why this one. Find the voltage first and the currents come free. The 10 A must cross the parallel combination, so V = IR gives the shared voltage in one step, and then each branch current is that voltage divided by its own resistance. Trying to split the current first means using the current divider rule, which is easy to get upside down.

What each symbol means
  • R = the combined parallel resistance, in ohms
  • V = the voltage across the pair, common to both branches
  • I₁ = the current in the 8 Ω branch, in amperes
  • I₂ = the current in the 4 Ω branch, in amperes
The working, line by line
  1. Combine: R = (8 × 4) ÷ (8 + 4) = 32 ÷ 12 = 2.667 Ω
  2. Shared voltage: V = 10 × 2.667
  3. V = 26.67 V
  4. Branch one: I₁ = V ÷ 8 = 26.67 ÷ 8
  5. I₁ = 3.33 A
  6. Branch two, for the check: I₂ = 26.67 ÷ 4 = 6.67 A
Check it

Kirchhoff's junction law is the check, and it costs one line: I₁ + I₂ = 3.33 + 6.67 = 10 A, which is exactly what the source supplies, so nothing has been lost or invented. A second guard by eye: the SMALLER resistor must take the BIGGER current, so the 4 Ω branch should carry more than the 8 Ω branch, and 6.67 duly exceeds 3.33. The option offering 6.67 A for I₁ has the two branches swapped.

Both resistors sit across the same two nodes, so by the voltage law I1 x 8 = I2 x 4, which gives I2 = 2 I1: the smaller resistor takes twice the current. By the current law 10 = I1 + I2 = I1 + 2 I1 = 3 I1, so I1 = 3.33 A and I2 = 6.67 A. The voltage is then 3.33 x 8 = 26.67 V, and checking on the other branch, 6.67 x 4 = 26.67 V too.

5.43hardLecture slides, module 3, slide 93

A 50 V source drives a circuit with a 10 ohm resistor carrying I1, an 8 ohm resistor carrying I2, and a 6 ohm and 4 ohm resistor in series carrying I3. Traversing anti-clockwise, find I3.

  • ✓1.538 ACorrect. 50 volts divided by 32.5 ohms.
  • ✗1.25 AWrong. 1.25 is the RATIO of I2 to I3, not a current in amps.
  • ✗2.5 AWrong. This drops one of the two loop equations.
  • ✗5.0 AWrong. This puts 50 V across the 10 ohm resistor alone.
  • ✗0.65 AWrong. This inverts the final division.
Background, so this is not just a formula

When a circuit has more than one loop, Kirchhoff's laws are the general tool, but a circuit that is only series and parallel underneath can be unpicked more quickly by reduction. Collapse the parallel part into one resistor, find the total current, then expand back out to recover the individual branch currents. Reduce, solve, expand.

Given
  • V = 50 V
  • R₁ = 10 Ω, carrying I₁, in series with the rest
  • R₂ = 8 Ω, carrying I₂
  • 6 Ω and 4 Ω in series carrying I₃, forming a branch parallel to R₂

Find: The current I₃, in amperes

Formula

Rp =(R₂R₃)(R₂ + R₃)

Why this one. The 6 Ω and 4 Ω sit one after the other in the same branch, so they add to a single 10 Ω before anything else happens. That branch and the 8 Ω branch span the same two nodes, so they combine in parallel. What is left is the 10 Ω of R₁ in series with that combination, which is a single loop Ohm's law can finish.

What each symbol means
  • R₃ = 6 + 4 = 10 Ω, the series branch treated as one resistor
  • Rp = the parallel combination of R₂ and R₃, in ohms
  • I₁ = the total current from the source, in amperes
  • I₃ = the current in the 6 Ω and 4 Ω branch, which is what we want
The working, line by line
  1. Collapse the series branch: R₃ = 6 + 4 = 10 Ω
  2. Parallel it with R₂: Rp = (8 × 10) ÷ (8 + 10) = 80 ÷ 18 = 4.444 Ω
  3. Total round the loop: R = R₁ + Rp = 10 + 4.444 = 14.444 Ω
  4. Total current: I₁ = 50 ÷ 14.444 = 3.462 A
  5. Voltage across the parallel part: Vp = I₁ × Rp = 3.462 × 4.444
  6. Vp = 15.385 V
  7. Expand back out: I₃ = Vp ÷ R₃ = 15.385 ÷ 10
  8. I₃ = 1.538 A
Check it

Recover the other branch and test the junction: I₂ = 15.385 ÷ 8 = 1.923 A, and I₂ + I₃ = 1.923 + 1.538 = 3.461 A, which matches the total current I₁ to three decimals. Sense check as well: the 10 Ω branch has more resistance than the 8 Ω branch, so it must carry LESS current, and 1.538 is duly below 1.923.

Three equations, three unknowns. First loop, voltage law: minus 50 + 10 I1 + 8 I2 = 0. Second loop: minus 8 I2 + 6 I3 + 4 I3 = 0, which gives 8 I2 = 10 I3, so I2 = 1.25 I3. Current law at the junction: I1 = I2 + I3. Substituting into the first loop gives 18 I2 + 10 I3 = 50, then replacing I2 gives 1.25 I3 x 18 + 10 I3 = 50, that is 22.5 I3 + 10 I3 = 32.5 I3 = 50, so I3 = 50 over 32.5 = 1.538 A.

6EMF and Internal Resistance

Speed tip. Terminal voltage is always LESS than the emf, by exactly Ir. If your answer exceeds the emf you added where you should have subtracted.

6.1easyThe real test 2, question 10

A cell has an e.m.f. of 9 V and an internal resistance of 1 Ω. If the current flowing in the circuit is 2 A, what is the terminal voltage of the cell?

  • ✓7 VCorrect. V = E - Ir = 9 - 2 = 7 V.
  • ✗5 VWrong. This subtracts twice the internal drop, as if r were 2 ohms.
  • ✗9 VWrong. 9 V is the emf, which is only the terminal voltage when no current flows.
  • ✗11 VWrong, and it ADDS the internal drop. Terminal voltage can never exceed the emf.
  • ✗4 VWrong. No route to 4 V from a 9 V cell dropping 2 V internally.
Background, so this is not just a formula

The terminal voltage is what a voltmeter across the battery would actually read while current is flowing. It is always less than the emf, because part of the push is spent driving current through the cell's own internal resistance. Open the circuit so no current flows and the loss vanishes, which is why a flat battery can still read its full voltage until you put it under load.

Given
  • E = 9 V (the emf)
  • r = 1 Ω (the internal resistance)
  • I = 2 A (the current in the circuit)

Find: The terminal voltage V of the cell, in volts

Formula

V = E − Ir

Why this one. The emf is given and the terminal voltage is wanted, so this is the subtraction form, not E = I(R + r). The lost volts are Ir, and they are taken away because they are consumed inside the cell before anything reaches the terminals. Watching the sign is the whole question.

What each symbol means
  • V = the terminal voltage, what the external circuit gets, in volts
  • E = the emf, the total push, in volts
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • r = the internal resistance, in ohms
  • Ir = the volts lost inside the cell
The working, line by line
  1. Lost volts: I × r = 2 × 1 = 2 V
  2. V = E − Ir
  3. V = 9 − 2
  4. V = 7 V
Check it

The answer must be below 9 V, always. One of the options is 11 V, which is the trap for anyone who added instead of subtracting, and 9 V is the trap for anyone who forgot the internal resistance entirely. Both are impossible: a cell cannot deliver more than it generates.

Terminal voltage is the emf − what is lost inside the cell: V = E - Ir = 9 - (2 x 1) = 7 V. This is the mirror image of the previous question: there the terminal p.d. was given and the emf wanted, here the emf is given and the terminal p.d. wanted. The terminal voltage is always the SMALLER of the two.

6.2mediumReference sheet R.1

Why do a car’s headlights dim at the moment the engine is started?

  • ✓The starter draws a large current, so the internal drop Ir grows and the terminal voltage V = E − Ir fallsCorrect. It is I that changes, and Ir with it.
  • ✗The emf of the battery fallsWrong. The emf is the chemical property of the cell and does not drop like that.
  • ✗The internal resistance of the battery rises sharplyWrong. The internal resistance is roughly constant; the current is what surges.
  • ✗The headlights are switched off automaticallyWrong. Nothing switches them off.
  • ✗The alternator reverses polarityWrong. No polarity reversal is involved.

V = E − Ir. The emf E and the internal resistance r stay much the same, but the starter motor pulls a very large current I, so the internal drop Ir becomes large and the terminal voltage available to the lamps falls. It is the everyday illustration of internal resistance.

6.3mediumReference sheet R.2

The terminal voltage is the emf − the internal drop, V = E minus {{1}}.

Answer:

Ir, with the lower case r for the INTERNAL resistance. Capital R is the external resistance, and IR is the useful voltage delivered to the circuit.

6.4mediumThe real test 2, question 3

In a circuit, the external resistor has a value of 5 Ω. The internal resistance of the cell is 0.5 Ω. If the current flowing is 1 A. Calculate the e.m.f of the cell.

  • ✓5.5 VCorrect. E = I(R + r) = 1 x 5.5 = 5.5 V.
  • ✗4.5 VWrong, and it SUBTRACTS the internal resistance instead of adding it, which would make the emf smaller than the terminal p.d.
  • ✗3.5 VWrong. No route to 3.5 V from these numbers.
  • ✗6.5 VWrong. This would need an internal resistance of 1.5 ohms.
  • ✗7.5 VWrong. This would need an internal resistance of 2.5 ohms.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A cell is not a perfect source. The chemicals inside it have resistance of their own, so some of the push the cell generates is used up driving current through the cell itself, before any of it reaches the circuit outside. The full push is the emf; what is left over at the terminals is the terminal p.d. This is why a torch battery that works fine dims when you add a second bulb: more current means more of the emf is lost inside.

Given
  • R = 5 Ω (the external resistor)
  • r = 0.5 Ω (the internal resistance of the cell)
  • I = 1 A (the current flowing)

Find: The emf E of the cell, in volts

Formula

E = I(R + r)

Why this one. The emf has to push the current through EVERYTHING in the loop, and the current meets the outside resistor and the inside of the cell one after the other. Resistances met one after the other simply add, so the total the emf works against is R + r. This is just Ohm's law applied to the whole circuit rather than to one component.

What each symbol means
  • E = the electromotive force, the full push of the cell, in volts
  • I = the current in the circuit, in amperes
  • R = the external or load resistance, in ohms
  • r = the internal resistance of the cell, in ohms
The working, line by line
  1. Add the two resistances first: R + r = 5 + 0.5 = 5.5 Ω
  2. E = I × (R + r)
  3. E = 1 × 5.5
  4. E = 5.5 V
Check it

The emf must be LARGER than the terminal p.d., which here is IR = 1 × 5 = 5 V. It is, by exactly the 0.5 V lost inside the cell. If your emf ever comes out below IR, you subtracted where you should have added.

The emf drives the current through BOTH the external resistor and the cell's own internal resistance, so E = I(R + r) = 1 x (5 + 0.5) = 5.5 V. Equivalently E = IR + Ir, that is 5 V delivered to the circuit + 0.5 V lost inside the cell. The emf is always LARGER than the terminal p.d., which is the check.

6.5mediumReference sheet R.1

For a source with internal resistance, V = E − Ir, so the terminal voltage is always {{1}} than the emf whenever current flows.

Answer:

Less. The internal resistance eats Ir volts before anything reaches the terminals. It is why headlights dip when the starter motor draws a huge current, and it is a free check: any answer where the emf comes out below the terminal p.d. is wrong.

6.6mediumReference sheet R.4

emf is the energy per unit charge supplied by a source, and despite the name electromotive FORCE it is measured in volts.

Answer: true

True, and the name is a historical accident worth knowing about: emf is not a force at all, and it carries the unit of potential difference. Energy per unit charge is exactly what a volt measures, which is why E and V can be added and subtracted in V = E − Ir.

6.7mediumReference sheet R.4

Internal resistance is the resistance of the source {{1}}, and it is what causes the terminal voltage to fall below the emf.

Answer:

Of the source itself. A battery is not a perfect supply: its chemistry and construction resist the current passing through it, and that resistance sits in series with everything outside. Capital R is the external resistance and small r the internal one, and swapping them fails every emf question.

6.8mediumReference sheet R.4

The terminal voltage of a source is:

  • ✓The voltage across it while current flows, always less than the emfCorrect. What you would measure at the terminals with current flowing, always below the emf by exactly Ir.
  • ✗The same as the emf under all conditionsWrong while current flows, though it becomes true at zero current.
  • ✗The voltage across the internal resistance aloneWrong. That is the internal DROP, the part you lose.
  • ✗Always greater than the emfWrong, and impossible: that would mean the internal resistance added energy.
  • ✗The voltage measured with the circuit disconnectedWrong as a definition of terminal voltage, though disconnecting is indeed how you measure the emf.

What you would actually measure at the terminals with current flowing, and it is always below the emf by exactly Ir. There is an edge case worth knowing: with NO current flowing, Ir is zero and the terminal voltage does equal the emf, which is how an emf is measured in practice.

6.9mediumReference sheet R.4

While a battery is delivering current, the voltage measured at its terminals is lower than its emf.

Answer: true

True. The current has to pass through the source's own internal resistance first, and that costs Ir volts, so what reaches the terminals is E − Ir. Draw a bigger current and you lose more, which is why a torch dims as its cells age and their internal resistance climbs.

6.10mediumLecture slides, module 4, slide 84

In a circuit, the external resistor has value 5 ohms. The internal resistance of the cell is 0.5 ohms. If the current flowing is 1 A, calculate the emf of the cell.

  • ✓5.5 VCorrect. 1 A driven through 5.5 ohms in total.
  • ✗4.5 VWrong. This SUBTRACTS the internal resistance instead of adding it, but internal resistance costs voltage, it does not supply it.
  • ✗5.0 VWrong. This uses only the external 5 ohms and forgets the cell has resistance of its own.
  • ✗0.5 VWrong. That is the internal resistance alone, in ohms rather than volts.
  • ✗6.5 VWrong. This adds a whole ohm too many.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A real cell is a perfect source of emf with a small resistance built in, and that internal resistance is not a defect to be ignored but the reason a battery's voltage sags under load. The emf must drive the current through the outside circuit AND through the cell itself, so the two resistances are met one after the other and simply add.

Given
  • R = 5 Ω, the external resistor
  • r = 0.5 Ω, the internal resistance
  • I = 1 A

Find: The emf E of the cell, in volts

Formula

E = I(R + r)

Why this one. This is Ohm's law applied to the complete loop rather than to one component. The current is the same everywhere in a single loop, so the total opposition it meets is R + r, and the emf is what pushes it through both.

What each symbol means
  • E = the emf, the full push generated by the cell, in volts
  • I = the current in the loop, in amperes
  • R = the external or load resistance, in ohms
  • r = the internal resistance of the cell, in ohms
The working, line by line
  1. Add the resistances: R + r = 5 + 0.5 = 5.5 Ω
  2. E = I × (R + r)
  3. E = 1 × 5.5
  4. E = 5.5 V
Check it

The emf must exceed the terminal p.d., which here is IR = 1 × 5 = 5 V. It does, by exactly the 0.5 V lost inside. The option 5.0 V is that terminal p.d. offered as a trap for anyone who forgot the cell's own resistance. This same question was set again in test 2, which tells you the examiner likes it.

The emf has to drive the current through BOTH resistances, the external one and the cell's own. E = I(R + r) = 1 x (5 + 0.5) = 5.5 V. This slide is worth knowing by name: Test 2 question 3 is this question, word for word and number for number, so the examiner set the test straight from the deck.

6.11mediumLecture slides, module 4, slide 89

A wire of 4 ohms is connected to a battery of emf 6 V and internal resistance r. The current flowing is 1 A. Calculate r.

  • ✓2 ohmsCorrect. The total resistance must be 6 ohms, and 4 of them are external.
  • ✗6 ohmsWrong. 6 is the TOTAL resistance, or the emf in volts, not the internal part.
  • ✗1.5 ohmsWrong. This divides 6 by 4 instead of subtracting.
  • ✗10 ohmsWrong. This ADDS 6 and 4 rather than subtracting.
  • ✗0.5 ohmsWrong. No route to 0.5 from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Every question in this family is the same equation, E = I(R + r), read with a different unknown. Here the emf and the current are known and the internal resistance is the mystery, so the algebra runs backwards, but the physics has not changed at all. Recognising the family saves you from learning four separate formulas.

Given
  • R = 4 Ω, the wire
  • E = 6 V
  • I = 1 A

Find: The internal resistance r of the battery, in ohms

Formula

E = I(R + r)

Why this one. One equation with one unknown, so it can simply be rearranged. Divide by the current to get the total resistance, then subtract the part you already know. That two-move pattern, total then subtract, is the same one used to find a missing resistor in a series string.

What each symbol means
  • E = the emf, in volts
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • R = the external resistance, in ohms
  • r = the internal resistance, the unknown
The working, line by line
  1. Start from: E = I(R + r)
  2. Divide both sides by I: R + r = E ÷ I
  3. R + r = 6 ÷ 1 = 6 Ω
  4. Subtract the known part: r = 6 − 4
  5. r = 2 Ω
Check it

Substitute back: E = 1 × (4 + 2) = 6 V, which is the emf given, so the answer stands. The option 6 Ω is the TOTAL resistance offered as a trap for stopping one line early, and 10 Ω is what adding gives instead of subtracting.

Start from E = I(R + r) and rearrange in symbols before substituting: R + r = E/I, so r = E/I − R. Then r = 6/1 − 4 = 6 − 4 = 2 ohms. Check it forwards: 1 A through 4 + 2 = 6 ohms needs exactly 6 V, which is the emf given.

6.12hardThe real test 2, question 4

A cell of unknown e.m.f E, and internal resistance 2 Ω is connected to a 5 Ω resistor. If the terminal p.d. is 1 volt, calculate the value of "E" if a current of "I" flows through the cell.

  • ✓1.4 VCorrect. I = 1/5 = 0.2 A, then E = I(R + r) = 0.2 x 7 = 1.4 V.
  • ✗1.2 VWrong. This adds only half the internal drop, as if r were 1 ohm.
  • ✗1.3 VWrong. No consistent route gives 1.3 V here.
  • ✗1 VWrong, and it is the trap: 1 V is the TERMINAL p.d. given in the stem, not the emf. The emf must exceed it by the internal drop.
  • ✗1.5 VWrong. This would need a current of 0.25 A, that is a 4 ohm external resistor.
Background, so this is not just a formula

This is the same cell idea as before, read from the other end. You are told what the outside world actually receives, the terminal p.d., and asked to work back to the full push the cell was generating. The lost volts, the part burned inside the cell, are exactly Ir. So emf = what got out + what was lost inside.

Given
  • r = 2 Ω (internal resistance)
  • R = 5 Ω (external resistor)
  • V = 1 V (terminal p.d., the voltage actually across the 5 Ω)

Find: The emf E of the cell, in volts

Formula

E = V + Ir

Why this one. The terminal p.d. is given, not the current, so the circuit has to be entered through Ohm's law on the external resistor first: the terminal p.d. is what sits across R, so I = V ÷ R. Once the current is known, the volts lost inside are Ir, and adding them back to V rebuilds the emf.

What each symbol means
  • E = the emf, the total push generated, in volts
  • V = the terminal p.d., what the outside circuit receives, in volts
  • I = the current, in amperes, found from V and R
  • r = the internal resistance, in ohms
  • Ir = the lost volts, the part used up inside the cell
The working, line by line
  1. The terminal p.d. sits across R, so find I from Ohm's law:
  2. I = V ÷ R = 1 ÷ 5 = 0.2 A
  3. Lost volts inside the cell: Ir = 0.2 × 2 = 0.4 V
  4. E = V + Ir = 1 + 0.4
  5. E = 1.4 V
Check it

Cross-check with the other form: E = I(R + r) = 0.2 × (5 + 2) = 0.2 × 7 = 1.4 V. Two independent routes to the same number is the strongest check there is, and it costs one line.

Two steps. First the current, from the terminal p.d. across the external resistor: I = V/R = 1/5 = 0.2 A. Then the emf, which must also push that current through the internal resistance: E = I(R + r) = 0.2 x (5 + 2) = 0.2 x 7 = 1.4 V. Equivalently E = V + Ir = 1 + 0.2 x 2 = 1.4 V.

6.13hardLecture slides, module 4, slide 86

A cell of unknown emf E and internal resistance 2 ohms is connected to a 5 ohm resistance. If the terminal p.d. V is 1 V, calculate E.

  • ✓1.4 VCorrect. 1 V at the terminals + 0.4 V lost inside the cell.
  • ✗1.0 VWrong, and it is the trap: 1 V is the terminal p.d. copied straight out of the question. The emf must always be LARGER.
  • ✗1.2 VWrong. This uses an internal drop of 0.2 V rather than 0.4 V.
  • ✗1.5 VWrong. This would need an internal resistance of 2.5 ohms.
  • ✗1.3 VWrong. A near miss on the internal drop.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Read from the other end, the same cell idea asks you to rebuild the emf from what actually reached the outside world. The terminal p.d. is what got out; the volts lost inside are Ir; add them and you have the full push. What makes it a step harder is that the current is not given, so it has to be found first.

Given
  • r = 2 Ω
  • R = 5 Ω
  • V = 1 V, the terminal p.d.

Find: The emf E of the cell, in volts

Formula

E = V + Ir

Why this one. The terminal p.d. is the voltage across the external resistor, so Ohm's law on R alone gives the current. Only then can the lost volts be worked out. Jumping straight to E = I(R + r) is not wrong, but it needs the same current first, so nothing is saved.

What each symbol means
  • E = the emf, in volts
  • V = the terminal p.d., what the outside circuit receives, in volts
  • I = the current, found from V and R
  • r = the internal resistance, in ohms
  • Ir = the volts lost inside the cell
The working, line by line
  1. The terminal p.d. sits across R, so: I = V ÷ R
  2. I = 1 ÷ 5 = 0.2 A
  3. Volts lost inside: Ir = 0.2 × 2 = 0.4 V
  4. E = V + Ir = 1 + 0.4
  5. E = 1.4 V
Check it

Cross-check by the other route: E = I(R + r) = 0.2 × 7 = 1.4 V. Agreement from two independent formulas is worth the one line it costs. Sanity: the emf must be bigger than the 1 V that got out, and 1.4 is. This question was also set in test 2, word for word.

Two steps, and the first is the one people miss. The terminal p.d. is the voltage across the EXTERNAL resistor, so it gives you the current: I = V/R = 1/5 = 0.2 A. The drop inside the cell is then Ir = 0.2 x 2 = 0.4 V, and the emf is the terminal voltage + that internal loss: E = 1 + 0.4 = 1.4 V. Equivalently E = I(R + r) = 0.2 x 7 = 1.4 V. This is Test 2 question 4, verbatim from this slide.

6.14hardLecture slides, module 4, slide 90

The terminal p.d. of a battery is 12 V when an external resistance of 20 ohms is connected, and 13.5 V when an external resistance of 45 ohms is connected. Calculate the emf of the battery.

  • ✓15 VCorrect. r works out at 5 ohms and E at 15 V.
  • ✗13.5 VWrong. 13.5 V is the higher terminal reading, still below the emf.
  • ✗25.5 VWrong. This adds the two terminal voltages, which have no reason to be added.
  • ✗12 VWrong. 12 V is the lower terminal reading.
  • ✗14 VWrong. A near miss that does not satisfy both readings at once.
Background, so this is not just a formula

This is the standard experiment for measuring what is inside a cell without opening it. Take two readings with different loads and you get two equations; the two unknowns, the emf and the internal resistance, then fall out together. It works because the emf is a fixed property of the cell while the terminal p.d. changes with the load, and the difference between them is always Ir.

Given
  • First reading: V = 12 V with R = 20 Ω
  • Second reading: V = 13.5 V with R = 45 Ω

Find: The emf E of the battery, in volts

Formula

E = V + Ir, where I = V / R

Why this one. One reading is not enough, because it gives one equation with two unknowns. A second reading gives a second equation, and since E and r are the same cell in both, setting the two expressions for E equal eliminates E and leaves r alone. This is simultaneous equations doing exactly what they are for.

What each symbol means
  • E = the emf, the same in both readings
  • r = the internal resistance, also the same in both
  • V = the terminal p.d. for that reading, in volts
  • R = the external resistance for that reading, in ohms
  • I = the current for that reading, found as V ÷ R
The working, line by line
  1. First reading, current: I₁ = 12 ÷ 20 = 0.6 A
  2. First equation: E = 12 + 0.6r
  3. Second reading, current: I₂ = 13.5 ÷ 45 = 0.3 A
  4. Second equation: E = 13.5 + 0.3r
  5. The cell is the same, so set them equal: 12 + 0.6r = 13.5 + 0.3r
  6. Collect the r terms: 0.6r − 0.3r = 13.5 − 12
  7. 0.3r = 1.5, so r = 5 Ω
  8. Put r back into either equation: E = 12 + 0.6 × 5
  9. E = 12 + 3 = 15 V
Check it

Test r = 5 in the OTHER equation, the one you did not use to finish: E = 13.5 + 0.3 × 5 = 13.5 + 1.5 = 15 V. Both agree, so the pair is consistent. Sense check: the emf must exceed both terminal readings, and 15 is above 13.5 and above 12. Notice also the physical story, that the larger load resistance drew less current, so less was lost inside and the terminal reading climbed closer to the true emf.

Two readings give two equations, and two unknowns, E and r, need exactly that. First currents: I1 = 12/20 = 0.6 A and I2 = 13.5/45 = 0.3 A. Then V = E − Ir twice over: 12 = E − 0.6r and 13.5 = E − 0.3r. Subtracting the first from the second gives 1.5 = 0.3r, so r = 5 ohms. Substituting back: E = 12 + 0.6 x 5 = 15 V. Notice the pattern the question is teaching: a LARGER external resistance draws LESS current, so less is lost inside the cell and the terminal voltage climbs closer to the emf.

7Magnetic Force on Charges and Wires

Speed tip. The magnetic force never changes the speed, only the direction. Kinetic energy is unchanged and the path is a circle of radius mv/qB.

7.1easyReference sheet R.4

Magnetic flux density, the quantity everyone simply calls the magnetic field, is measured in {{1}}.

Answer:

The tesla, symbol T. Keep it apart from the weber, which is the unit of magnetic FLUX rather than flux density, and one weber is one tesla square metre. A tesla is a large unit: the fields in this course run from 0.03 T up to 0.6 T, and a fridge magnet is only a few thousandths.

7.2easyReference sheet R.1

What is the mass of an electron?

  • ✓9.11 x 10-31 kgCorrect. 9.11 x 10-31 kg.
  • ✗1.67 x 10-27 kgWrong. That is the PROTON mass, which is far larger.
  • ✗1.602 x 10-19 kgWrong. That is the elementary charge, in coulombs not kilograms.
  • ✗9.11 x 1031 kgWrong sign on the exponent, which would make an electron heavier than a star.
  • ✗8.85 x 10-12 kgWrong. That is ε₀.

The electron mass is 9.11 x 10-31 kg, about 1836 × smaller than the proton’s 1.67 x 10-27 kg. It appears in the radius of a circular path, r = mv/(qB), and in the cyclotron frequency.

7.3easyReference sheet R.1

What is the mass of a proton?

  • ✓1.67 x 10-27 kgCorrect. 1.67 x 10-27 kg.
  • ✗9.11 x 10-31 kgWrong. That is the electron mass.
  • ✗1.602 x 10-19 kgWrong. That is a charge, not a mass.
  • ✗1.67 x 1027 kgWrong sign on the exponent.
  • ✗4 π x 10-7 kgWrong. That is μ₀.

The proton mass is 1.67 x 10-27 kg. Keep the three small constants apart by their exponents: charge at 10 to the − 19 C, proton at 10 to the − 27 kg, electron at 10 to the − 31 kg.

7.4easyReference sheet R.4

Which pairing is correct?

  • ✓The tesla measures magnetic flux DENSITY, and the weber measures magnetic FLUXCorrect. Tesla goes with B, the density; weber goes with Φ, the flux.
  • ✗The tesla measures magnetic flux, and the weber measures flux densityWrong, and exactly reversed.
  • ✗Both measure magnetic fluxWrong. They measure related but different quantities.
  • ✗The tesla measures current and the weber measures fieldWrong. Current is in amperes.
  • ✗The weber measures inductanceWrong. Inductance is in henries.

B, the flux density, is in tesla. Φ, the flux, is in webers, and Φ = BA cos θ ties them: one weber is one tesla square metre. The distinction matters on Test 2 question 11, whose answer is that a changing FLUX induces an emf, not a changing field.

7.5mediumReference sheet R.1

When BOTH an electric and a magnetic field are present, the force on a moving charge is:

  • ✓F = q(E + v cross B)Correct, and this is the full Lorentz force.
  • ✗F = qE only, since magnetic fields do no workWrong. Doing no work is not the same as exerting no force; a magnetic force bends the path.
  • ✗F = qvB sin θ onlyWrong. That drops the electric contribution entirely.
  • ✗F = q(E cross B)Wrong. E and B are not crossed with each other here. That combination is the Poynting vector, a different quantity.
  • ✗F = qE times qvBWrong. Forces add; they do not multiply.

The two contributions simply add: the electric part qE, which acts whether or not the charge is moving, plus the magnetic part qv cross B, which needs motion. Note that the magnetic part doing no WORK does not mean it exerts no FORCE: it steers without changing the speed.

7.6mediumReference sheet R.1

The period of a charge circling in a magnetic field is T =2 π mqB, which like the frequency is independent of the {{1}}.

Answer:

The speed. Mass, charge and field all appear in the formula and all change the period; speed does not appear and does not. It matters for the helical path, where the pitch is v cos θ times T: the period is fixed by the field and the particle, and only the along-field velocity decides how far it advances in that time.

7.7mediumReference sheet R.1

A straight wire of length L carries current I in a magnetic field B. The force on it is F = BIL sin θ. At what angle is this force greatest?

  • ✓90°, with the wire across the fieldCorrect. Perpendicular gives sin = 1.
  • ✗0°, with the wire along the fieldWrong, and it is the ZERO-force case.
  • ✗45°Wrong. sin 45 is about 0.71, so substantial but not maximal.
  • ✗180°Wrong. sin 180 = 0, so no force, same as along the field.
  • ✗The angle makes no differenceWrong. The sine factor is precisely the angle's effect.

sin 90 = 1, the largest the sine can be, so the force is maximum with the wire perpendicular to the field. Along the field, sin 0 = 0 and the wire feels nothing at all, which is the same rule as for a single moving charge: only the component of motion ACROSS the field is deflected.

7.8mediumReference sheet R.1

The magnetic dipole moment of a current loop is m = NIA, where N is the number of turns, I the current and A the {{1}} the loop encloses.

Answer:

The area, in square metres, giving m the unit A m². Test 2 question 7 works it backwards, giving a torque and asking for m. Note that the m here is a MAGNETIC MOMENT and not the mass that appears in r =mvqB, which is the most confusable pair of symbols in the topic.

7.9mediumReference sheet R.1

The torque on a current loop is τ = mB sin θ = NIAB sin θ. When is it zero?

  • ✓When the magnetic moment m lies along BCorrect, and it is the stable resting position.
  • ✗When m is perpendicular to BWrong. Perpendicular is where the torque is at its MAXIMUM.
  • ✗When the current is at its maximumWrong. More current gives more torque, not less.
  • ✗Only when the current is zeroWrong. Zero current is one way, but alignment gives zero torque with the current still flowing.
  • ✗When the loop has more than one turnWrong. More turns increase the torque.

sin 0 = 0, so aligned means no torque. It is the exact counterpart of the ELECTRIC dipole, whose torque pE sin θ also vanishes on alignment. Both are the position the field is trying to turn the object into, which is why a compass needle settles pointing along the field and then stops.

7.10mediumReference sheet R.4

Asked twice. Also set as: Reference sheet R.4.

The cyclotron frequency is the number of revolutions per second of a charge in a magnetic field, and it is independent of the particle's speed.

Answer: true

True on both counts. f = qB over 2 π m contains the charge, the field and the mass, and nothing about how fast the particle is going. A faster particle circles on a wider radius and arrives back at the same moment.

7.11mediumReference sheet R.1

The electron mass is 9.11 × 10 to the power minus {{1}} kg.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

The electron and the proton carry the same size of charge but wildly different masses, and that single asymmetry runs through the whole of this course. It is why the electron does almost all the moving in a current, why it curves far more tightly in a magnetic field, and why the nucleus can be treated as sitting still while electrons orbit it.

Given
  • Electron mass: mₑ = 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ kg
  • Proton mass: mₚ = 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ kg

Find: The exponent in the electron mass, 9.11 × 10 to the power minus ___

The working, line by line
  1. The answer is 31.
  2. Compare the two: 10⁻²⁷ ÷ 10⁻³¹ = 10⁴, so the proton is about 10000 times heavier by the powers alone.
  3. Include the front numbers: 1.67 ÷ 9.11 is about 0.18, so the true ratio is about 1836.
  4. Why not 27: that is the PROTON, and the two are deliberately offered together.
  5. Why not 19: that is 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹, the elementary CHARGE, not a mass.
  6. Why not 12: that is 8.85 × 10⁻¹², the permittivity ε₀.
Check it

Sort the four constants by what they measure rather than by their digits, since the digits are what the options play on. 10⁻³¹ and 10⁻²⁷ are masses in kilograms, 10⁻¹⁹ is a charge in coulombs, and 10⁻¹² is ε₀ in farads per metre. Ask what UNIT the answer needs and only one candidate survives.

Minus 31. The proton, at 10 to the − 27, is about two thousand times heavier.

7.12mediumReference sheet R.1

The proton mass is 1.67 × 10 to the power minus {{1}} kg.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

The proton is the heavy partner. About 1836 times the mass of an electron, which is why in a wire it is the electrons that drift while the positive ions stay put, vibrating in the lattice. In a magnetic field the same mass difference means a proton curves on a far wider arc than an electron at the same speed, since r = mv ÷ qB puts mass on the top.

Given
  • Proton mass: mₚ = 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ kg
  • Electron mass, for contrast: mₑ = 9.11 × 10⁻³¹ kg

Find: The exponent in the proton mass, 1.67 × 10 to the power minus ___

The working, line by line
  1. The answer is 27.
  2. A less negative exponent means a BIGGER number, and the proton is the heavier one.
  3. So the proton's −27 must sit above the electron's −31, which it does.
  4. Why not 31: that is the electron, the light one.
  5. Why not 19 or 12: those are the elementary charge and ε₀, neither of which is a mass.
Check it

Use the sign as the check rather than memory alone. Between 10⁻²⁷ and 10⁻³¹, the one closer to zero is larger, so it must belong to the heavier particle. If you ever attach −31 to the proton, the mass ratio comes out upside down and a proton becomes lighter than an electron, which contradicts everything the course says about which particle moves.

Minus 27, against the electron’s − 31.

7.13mediumReference sheet R.2

A charge moving ALONG a magnetic field feels no force, because sin 0 equals {{1}}.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

A magnetic field only acts on the part of a charge's motion that CUTS ACROSS the field lines. Move exactly along them and you cut nothing, so you feel nothing. This is the deep difference between the magnetic force and the electric force: an electric field pushes a charge whether it moves or not, and always along the field, while a magnetic field pushes only moving charges and always at right angles to their motion.

Find: The value of sin 0 that makes the force vanish

Formula

F = qvB sin θ

Why this one. The angle θ is measured between the VELOCITY and the FIELD. Moving along the field makes that angle zero, and sin 0 = 0, so the whole force collapses to nothing however fast the charge is going and however strong the field is.

What each symbol means
  • F = the magnetic force, in newtons
  • q = the charge, in coulombs
  • v = the speed, in m s⁻¹
  • B = the flux density, in teslas
  • θ = the angle between v and B, taking SINE
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is 0.
  2. Moving ALONG the field means θ = 0°.
  3. sin 0° = 0.
  4. F = qvB × 0 = 0, so there is no force at all.
  5. Why not 1: that is sin 90°, motion straight across the field, which gives the MAXIMUM force.
  6. Why not 0.5: that is sin 30°, and why not 90: that is an angle, not a sine.
Check it

Keep the two trigonometric functions apart by their zero cases. FORCE uses sine, so it is zero when the motion is along the field. FLUX uses cosine, so it is zero when the surface is edge-on to the field. Ask yourself which quantity is being asked for and the function follows, which stops the single commonest error in this whole module.

Zero. The angle is measured between the velocity and the field, so moving along the field kills the force entirely.

7.14mediumReference sheet R.2

A magnetic field can increase the speed of a charged particle.

Answer: false

It cannot. The magnetic force is always perpendicular to the velocity, so it does no work and cannot change the speed. It changes direction only.

7.15mediumReference sheet R.2

The cyclotron frequency is independent of the particle’s {{1}}.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

A charged particle circling in a magnetic field completes each lap in the same time no matter how fast it is going. Speed it up and it swings out onto a wider circle, but the extra distance exactly cancels the extra speed, so the time per lap does not change. That surprising fact is what makes the cyclotron possible: a fixed alternating voltage at one fixed frequency can keep accelerating the particle lap after lap.

Find: The quantity the cyclotron frequency does NOT depend on

Formula

f =qB(2πm)

Why this one. Read the formula and notice what is absent. Charge, field and mass all appear; speed and radius do not. That is the whole answer, and it is more reliable than trying to remember a sentence about it.

What each symbol means
  • f = the cyclotron frequency, in hertz, the laps per second
  • q = the charge on the particle, in coulombs
  • B = the flux density, in teslas
  • m = the mass of the particle, in kilograms
  • 2π = the pure number that turns radians per second into laps per second
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is SPEED.
  2. Start from the radius: r = mv ÷ (qB), so a faster particle takes a wider circle.
  3. One lap is a distance 2πr, covered at speed v, so the time is T = 2πr ÷ v.
  4. Substitute r: T = 2π(mv ÷ qB) ÷ v, and the v cancels top and bottom.
  5. T = 2πm ÷ (qB), and f = 1 ÷ T = qB ÷ (2πm), with no v anywhere.
  6. Why not charge, mass or field: all three are visibly present in the formula.
Check it

The cancellation is the proof, and it is worth writing out rather than accepting: the v that arrives with the wider radius is the same v that carries the particle round faster, so the two cancel exactly. This is also why a cyclotron eventually fails at very high energies, since relativity makes the mass grow and the frequency then does drift.

Speed. The charge, the mass and the field all appear in f = qB/(2 π m); the speed does not.

7.16mediumReference sheet R.1

The magnetic force is F = qvB sin θ, so it is zero either when v = 0 or when the charge travels {{1}} the field.

Answer:

Along the field, where θ = 0 and sin 0 = 0. Those are the two escapes: stand still, or travel straight down the field lines. A stationary charge in the strongest magnet on earth feels nothing at all, which is the sharpest difference between a magnetic force and an electric one.

7.17mediumReference sheet R.1

In r =mvqB, a faster particle travels a wider circle but takes exactly the same time to go round.

Answer: true

True, and it is the cyclotron principle. The radius is proportional to v, so doubling the speed doubles the circle, but the period T = 2 π m over qB contains no v at all, so the longer path is covered in the same time. That is what lets a fixed-frequency accelerator keep pushing particles as they speed up.

7.18mediumReference sheet R.1

The force on a current-carrying conductor is F = BIL sin θ, which is at a maximum when the wire is at {{1}} degrees to the field.

Answer:

90°, where sin is 1. Along the field, sin 0 = 0 and the wire feels nothing, exactly as a single charge moving along the field feels nothing. Only the component ACROSS the field is deflected, whether it is one charge or a wire full of them.

7.19mediumReference sheet R.1

A coil of 50 turns, each enclosing 4 x 10-4 m², carries 2 A. What is its magnetic dipole moment?

  • ✓0.04 A m2Correct. 50 × 2 × 4 x 10-4.
  • ✗0.0008 A m2Wrong. This omits the 50 turns.
  • ✗400 A m2Wrong. This drops the 10-4 entirely.
  • ✗0.02 A m2Wrong. This omits the current.
  • ✗100 A m2Wrong. That is N times I, with the area forgotten.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A current loop behaves like a tiny bar magnet, and its magnetic moment measures how strongly. Three things make it stronger: more current, more area enclosed, and more turns, because each turn adds its own contribution on top of the others. That is why a compass needle can be replaced by a coil, and why motors use many turns rather than one.

Given
  • N = 50 turns
  • A = 4 × 10⁻⁴ m² per turn
  • I = 2 A

Find: The magnetic dipole moment m, in A m²

Formula

m = NIA

Why this one. The unit gives the formula away: A m² is amperes times metres squared, so it must be current times area, and the turns multiply because each one contributes equally. No field appears in the question, and none is needed, because the moment is a property of the loop itself and exists before any field is applied.

What each symbol means
  • m = the magnetic dipole moment, in A m²
  • N = the number of turns
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • A = the area enclosed by ONE turn, in m²
The working, line by line
  1. m = N × I × A
  2. m = 50 × 2 × 4 × 10⁻⁴
  3. 50 × 2 = 100
  4. 100 × 4 = 400
  5. m = 400 × 10⁻⁴
  6. m = 0.04 A m²
Check it

Reconstruct the unit as the check: amperes × metres squared = A m², which matches what was asked for, so the multiplication was the right operation. The option 0.0008 is what you get by forgetting the 50 turns, and the turns are exactly why a coil is used instead of a single loop.

m = NIA = 50 x 2 x 4 x 10-4 = 100 x 4 x 10-4 = 0.04 A m². All three multiply, so more turns, more current or more area each raise it. Note this m is a MAGNETIC MOMENT, not the mass that appears in r =mvqB.

7.20mediumReference sheet R.1

The torque on a current loop is τ = NIAB sin θ, and it falls to zero when the magnetic moment lies {{1}} the field.

Answer:

Along the field, where sin 0 = 0. That is the resting position the field is turning the loop towards, and it is exactly the same behaviour as an electric dipole in an electric field. A compass needle settling on north and then stopping is the everyday case.

7.21mediumReference sheet R.4

Asked twice. Also set as: Reference sheet R.4.

The cyclotron frequency is the number of revolutions per second of a charge in a magnetic field, and it is independent of the particle's {{1}}.

Answer:

Its speed. f = qB over 2 π m contains the charge, the field and the mass, and nothing about how fast the particle moves. Charge, mass and field all DO change it, which is worth noting because three of the four candidate answers are genuinely in the formula.

7.22hardReference sheet R.1

The cyclotron frequency is f =qB2 π m. A proton is injected twice into the same field, the second time at double the speed. What happens to its frequency of revolution?

  • ✓Unchanged, because there is no v in the formulaCorrect, and the absence of v from the formula is the whole point.
  • ✗It doublesWrong. Doubling the speed doubles the RADIUS, not the frequency.
  • ✗It halvesWrong. Nothing halves here.
  • ✗It quadruplesWrong. No quantity here scales as a square.
  • ✗It falls by the square root of twoWrong. No square root appears in this relationship.

There is no v in f = qB over 2 π m, so the frequency is independent of speed, and so is the period T = 2 π m over qB. A faster proton simply travels a wider circle, since r = mv over qB, and covers that longer path in exactly the same time. That is the cyclotron principle: it is what allows a fixed-frequency accelerating voltage to keep pushing particles that are getting faster.

7.23hardReference sheet R.1

In F = qvB sin θ, name TWO situations in which a charged particle in a magnetic field feels no force at all.

  • ✓When it is stationary, and when it moves along the field directionCorrect. Either no motion, or motion parallel to the field.
  • ✗When it is stationary, and when it moves at 90° to the fieldWrong on the second: 90° is where the force is at its MAXIMUM.
  • ✗When it moves fast, and when the field is strongWrong. Both of those increase the force.
  • ✗When the charge is negative, and when the field is uniformWrong. A negative charge feels a force in the opposite direction, not no force.
  • ✗There is no situation in which the force vanishesWrong. Both cases above give exactly zero.

Two ways to make the product zero. If v = 0 the whole expression is zero, so a stationary charge feels nothing. If θ = 0 the particle moves ALONG the field and sin 0 = 0, so again nothing. The force is greatest at 90°, where the sine is 1.

7.24hardReference sheet R.1

What happens to the kinetic energy of a charged particle moving through a magnetic field alone?

  • ✓It stays constant, because a magnetic field steers but never speeds upCorrect. Magnetic fields steer, they do not accelerate in the sense of changing speed.
  • ✗It increases steadilyWrong. That would require work to be done, which a perpendicular force cannot do.
  • ✗It decreases steadilyWrong, for the same reason.
  • ✗It oscillates between zero and a maximumWrong. The speed is constant throughout.
  • ✗It depends on the sign of the chargeWrong. The sign changes which way it curves, not the speed.

The magnetic force is always perpendicular to the velocity, and a force at right angles to the motion does no work. So the speed, and with it the kinetic energy, is unchanged: the field only changes the DIRECTION. That is why the path is a circle at constant speed.

7.25hardReference sheet R.1

What is notable about the cyclotron frequency f = qB / (2 π m)?

  • ✓It contains no v, so the frequency does not depend on the particle’s speedCorrect, and the cancellation with the radius is why.
  • ✗It rises in proportion to the speedWrong. Speed cancels out entirely.
  • ✗It falls in proportion to the speedWrong, for the same reason.
  • ✗It depends on the radius but not on the fieldWrong. B is in the formula and r is not.
  • ✗It applies only to electronsWrong. It holds for any charged particle.

No v appears in the formula. A faster particle travels a larger circle, since r = mv/(qB), and the two effects cancel exactly, so it goes round in the same time. That is the cyclotron principle, and it is what makes the machine work with a fixed driving frequency.

7.26hardThe real test 2, question 2

A proton experiences a magnetic force of 3.2 × 10-14 N while moving through a magnetic field of 0.2 T. If the angle between the velocity vector and the magnetic field is 60°, calculate the speed of the proton. Take: Charge of proton: (q = 1.6 × 10-19 C).

  • ✓1.155 × 106 m/sCorrect. v = F/(qB sin 60) = 3.2 x 10-14 / 2.771 x 10-20 = 1.155 x 106 m/s.
  • ✗6.155 × 106 m/sWrong. No route to this value; the leading digit cannot be 6 with this force and field.
  • ✗4.155 × 106 m/sWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✗3.155 × 106 m/sWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✗9.155 × 106 m/sWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A magnetic field only pushes on a charge that is MOVING, and only on the part of that motion which cuts across the field. Move exactly along the field lines and there is no force at all. Move exactly across them and the force is at its largest. Every angle in between is handled by sin θ, which is 0 when you move along the field and 1 when you move across it. That is the opposite of the flux formula, which uses cos θ, and mixing them up is the commonest way to lose this mark.

Given
  • F = 3.2 × 10⁻¹⁴ N (the magnetic force felt by the proton)
  • B = 0.2 T (the field strength, in teslas)
  • θ = 60° (the angle between the velocity and the field)
  • q = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ C (the charge on a proton, given in the question)

Find: The speed v of the proton, in m s⁻¹

Formula

F = qvB sin θ, so v =F(qB sin θ)

Why this one. The question names a moving charge, a field, an angle and a force, which is exactly the four quantities this formula ties together. It is not the wire formula F = BIL, because nothing here carries a current along a length; it is a single particle. Rearranging for v means everything except F moves to the bottom.

What each symbol means
  • F = the magnetic force, in newtons
  • q = the charge on the particle, in coulombs
  • v = the speed of the particle, in m s⁻¹, which is what we want
  • B = the magnetic flux density, in teslas
  • θ = the angle between v and B, taking SINE not cosine
The working, line by line
  1. Rearrange: v = F ÷ (q B sin θ)
  2. sin 60° = 0.866
  3. Bottom, step one: q × B = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 0.2 = 3.2 × 10⁻²⁰
  4. Bottom, step two: 3.2 × 10⁻²⁰ × 0.866 = 2.771 × 10⁻²⁰
  5. v = (3.2 × 10⁻¹⁴) ÷ (2.771 × 10⁻²⁰)
  6. Front numbers: 3.2 ÷ 2.771 = 1.155
  7. Powers of ten: 10⁻¹⁴ ÷ 10⁻²⁰ = 10⁶
  8. v = 1.155 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹
Check it

sin 60° is less than 1, so dividing by it makes the answer BIGGER than it would be at 90°. At 90° you would get 1.0 × 10⁶, and 1.155 × 10⁶ is duly larger. An answer smaller than the perpendicular case means you multiplied by the sine instead of dividing.

The magnetic force is F = qvB sin(θ), so v = F / (qB sin θ). The sine is what makes this more than a plug-in: sin 60 = 0.866, not 1. v = 3.2 x 10-14 / (1.6 x 10-19 x 0.2 x 0.866) = 3.2 x 10-14 / 2.771 x 10-20 = 1.155 x 106 m/s. Forgetting the sine would give 1.0 x 106, which is not offered, so the sine is exactly what is being tested.

7.27hardThe real test 2, question 7

A rectangular current loop placed in a uniform magnetic field experiences a torque of 4.8 × 10-3 Nm. If the magnetic field strength is 0.6 T and the angle between the loop's magnetic moment and the field is 30°, calculate the magnetic dipole moment of the loop.

  • ✓1.6 × 10-2 Am2Correct. m = τ/(B sin 30) = 4.8 x 10-3 / 0.3 = 1.6 x 10-2 Am2.
  • ✗13 × 10-2 Am2Wrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✗20 × 10-2 Am2Wrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✗3 × 10-2 Am2Wrong. This uses sin 30 as 0.25, or divides by 1.6 rather than 0.3.
  • ✗5 × 10-2 Am2Wrong. This drops the sine altogether and then mishandles the division.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A current loop sitting in a magnetic field behaves exactly like a small compass needle. It has a magnetic moment, an arrow saying which way it is facing and how strongly, and the field tries to twist that arrow into line with itself. That twist is the torque. When the loop already lies along the field there is nothing left to twist, so the torque is zero; when it sits across the field the twist is greatest. This is precisely how an electric motor turns.

Given
  • τ = 4.8 × 10⁻³ N m (the torque on the loop)
  • B = 0.6 T (the field strength)
  • θ = 30° (the angle between the magnetic moment and the field)

Find: The magnetic dipole moment m of the loop, in A m²

Formula

τ = mB sin θ, so m =τ(B sin θ)

Why this one. A torque, a field and an angle are given and a moment is wanted, and this formula is the only one linking those four. It takes SINE because the twist is greatest when the moment lies across the field and vanishes when it lies along it, which is exactly what sine does: sin 90° = 1 and sin 0° = 0.

What each symbol means
  • τ = the torque, in newton metres (N m)
  • m = the magnetic dipole moment, in ampere metres squared (A m²)
  • B = the magnetic flux density, in teslas
  • θ = the angle between the moment and the field
The working, line by line
  1. Rearrange: m = τ ÷ (B sin θ)
  2. sin 30° = 0.5 (worth knowing by heart, no calculator needed)
  3. Bottom: B × sin θ = 0.6 × 0.5 = 0.3
  4. m = (4.8 × 10⁻³) ÷ 0.3
  5. Front numbers: 4.8 ÷ 0.3 = 16
  6. m = 16 × 10⁻³ = 1.6 × 10⁻² A m²
Check it

16 × 10⁻³ and 1.6 × 10⁻² are the same number written two ways, so recognise your answer even when the options move the decimal point. If the angle had been 90° the moment needed would be only half as large, since a square-on loop feels the full twist from a smaller moment.

Torque on a magnetic dipole is τ = mB sin(θ), so m = τ / (B sin θ). With sin 30 = 0.5: m = 4.8 x 10-3 / (0.6 x 0.5) = 4.8 x 10-3 / 0.3 = 1.6 x 10-2 Am2. Note the contrast with the proton question: there the angle was 60 and the sine 0.866, here it is 30 and the sine exactly 0.5, so read the angle before reaching for a value.

7.28hardThe real test 2, question 8

An electron of mass 9.1 × 10-31 kg and charge 1.6 × 10-19 enters a magnetic field of 0.03 T perpendicularly with velocity 4 × 106 m/s. Find the radius of its circular path.

  • ✓7.58 × 10-4 mCorrect. r = mv/(qB) = 3.64 x 10-24 / 4.8 x 10-21 = 7.58 x 10-4 m.
  • ✗7.58 × 10-6 mWrong exponent, off by 100. Check the subtraction: -24 minus -21 is -3, then the leading 3.64/4.8 being less than one shifts it to -4.
  • ✗7.58 × 10-2 mWrong exponent, off by 100 the other way.
  • ✗7.58 × 10-8 mWrong exponent, off by 10000.
  • ✗7.58 × 10-1 mWrong exponent, and a radius of 0.758 m would be larger than the apparatus.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A magnetic force always acts at right angles to the direction of travel. A force that is permanently sideways can never speed a particle up or slow it down, it can only bend it, so the particle keeps its speed and curls into a circle forever. That is the principle behind the cyclotron and behind the way a mass spectrometer separates isotopes: heavier particles have more momentum, so they curve more gently and land further along.

Given
  • m = 9.1 × 10⁻³¹ kg (the mass of the electron)
  • q = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ C (the charge on the electron)
  • B = 0.03 T
  • v = 4 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹, entering PERPENDICULAR to the field

Find: The radius r of the circular path, in metres

Formula

r =mv(qB)

Why this one. It comes from setting the magnetic force equal to the centripetal force the circle requires: qvB = mv² ÷ r. Cancel one v from both sides and rearrange, and r = mv ÷ qB drops out. Because the particle enters perpendicular, sin θ = 1 and the angle disappears from the problem entirely. The top line, mv, is just momentum, which is the useful way to remember it: more momentum means a wider curve.

What each symbol means
  • r = the radius of the circular path, in metres
  • m = the mass of the particle, in kilograms
  • v = its speed, in m s⁻¹
  • q = its charge, in coulombs
  • B = the flux density, in teslas
The working, line by line
  1. Top: m × v = 9.1 × 10⁻³¹ × 4 × 10⁶
  2. Front numbers: 9.1 × 4 = 36.4
  3. Powers of ten: 10⁻³¹ × 10⁶ = 10⁻²⁵
  4. Top = 36.4 × 10⁻²⁵ = 3.64 × 10⁻²⁴
  5. Bottom: q × B = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 0.03 = 4.8 × 10⁻²¹
  6. r = (3.64 × 10⁻²⁴) ÷ (4.8 × 10⁻²¹)
  7. Front numbers: 3.64 ÷ 4.8 = 0.758
  8. Powers of ten: 10⁻²⁴ ÷ 10⁻²¹ = 10⁻³
  9. r = 0.758 × 10⁻³ = 7.58 × 10⁻⁴ m
Check it

Every option in this question is 7.58 with a different power of ten, so the whole mark rides on the exponent arithmetic and not on the multiplication. Do the powers of ten on their own line, as above, and the trap disappears. Sanity: 7.58 × 10⁻⁴ m is under a millimetre, which is believable for a light electron in a real laboratory field.

A charge moving perpendicular to a field goes in a circle, with the magnetic force supplying the centripetal force: qvB = mv2/r, so r = mv/(qB). Substituting: r = (9.1 x 10-31 x 4 x 106) / (1.6 x 10-19 x 0.03) = 3.64 x 10-24 / 4.8 x 10-21 = 7.58 x 10-4 m. Every option shares the digits 7.58, so the ONLY thing being tested is the power of ten.

7.29hardLecture slides, module 4, slide 35

Tutorial question 1. A proton enters a magnetic field of 0.5 T perpendicular to the field at 2 x 106 m/s. Calculate the magnetic force and the radius of its path. Take the proton mass as 1.67 x 10-27 kg and its charge as 1.6 x 10-19 C.

  • ✓F = 1.6 x 10-13 N and r = 4.2 x 10-2 mCorrect on both.
  • ✗F = 1.6 x 10-13 N and r = 4.2 x 10-4 mWrong on the radius by a factor of 100, a slip when dividing the exponents.
  • ✗F = 8.0 x 10-14 N and r = 4.2 x 10-2 mWrong on the force, from halving it as though the sine were 0.5 rather than 1.
  • ✗F = 1.6 x 10-19 N and r = 2.1 x 10-2 mWrong. 1.6 x 10-19 is the proton's CHARGE copied out as though it were a force.
  • ✗F = 3.2 x 10-13 N and r = 8.4 x 10-2 mWrong on both, from doubling the force and the radius together.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A charged particle fired across a magnetic field is pushed sideways, and because the push stays sideways as the particle turns, it never speeds up or slows down. It just curls into a circle. Two questions follow naturally: how hard is the push, and how tight is the circle. The first is the force formula, the second comes from setting that force equal to what a circle requires.

Given
  • B = 0.5 T
  • v = 2 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹, entering PERPENDICULAR to the field
  • m = 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ kg, the proton mass
  • q = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ C

Find: The magnetic force F, in newtons, and the radius r of the path, in metres

Formula

F = qvB, then r = mv / (qB)

Why this one. Perpendicular entry means θ = 90° and sin 90° = 1, so the angle drops out of the force formula entirely. The radius formula comes from qvB = mv² ÷ r: the magnetic force IS the centripetal force, since it is the only force acting. Cancel one v and rearrange.

What each symbol means
  • F = the magnetic force, in newtons
  • q = the charge on the proton, in coulombs
  • v = its speed, in m s⁻¹
  • B = the flux density, in teslas
  • m = the proton mass, in kilograms
  • r = the radius of the circular path, in metres
The working, line by line
  1. Force first: F = q × v × B
  2. F = 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 2 × 10⁶ × 0.5
  3. Front numbers: 1.6 × 2 × 0.5 = 1.6
  4. Powers of ten: 10⁻¹⁹ × 10⁶ = 10⁻¹³
  5. F = 1.6 × 10⁻¹³ N
  6. Radius next: r = (m × v) ÷ (q × B)
  7. Top: 1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ × 2 × 10⁶ = 3.34 × 10⁻²¹
  8. Bottom: 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ × 0.5 = 8.0 × 10⁻²⁰
  9. r = (3.34 × 10⁻²¹) ÷ (8.0 × 10⁻²⁰)
  10. Front numbers: 3.34 ÷ 8.0 = 0.4175
  11. Powers of ten: 10⁻²¹ ÷ 10⁻²⁰ = 10⁻¹
  12. r = 0.4175 × 10⁻¹ = 4.2 × 10⁻² m, about 4 cm
Check it

Cross-check the two answers against each other using the third relation, F = mv² ÷ r: (1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ × (2 × 10⁶)²) ÷ 0.0418 = (1.67 × 10⁻²⁷ × 4 × 10¹²) ÷ 0.0418 = 6.68 × 10⁻¹⁵ ÷ 0.0418 = 1.6 × 10⁻¹³ N, matching the force found first. A 4 cm circle is also a believable laboratory size, whereas the option offering 4.2 × 10⁻⁴ m would be a quarter of a millimetre.

Perpendicular means θ is 90° and sin 90 = 1, so the sine quietly disappears. Force: F = qvB = 1.6 x 10-19 x 2 x 106 x 0.5 = 1.6 x 10-13 N. Radius: r = mv over qB = (1.67 x 10-27 x 2 x 106) / (1.6 x 10-19 x 0.5) = 3.34 x 10-21 / 8.0 x 10-20 = 4.2 x 10-2 m, which is about 4 cm. A believable size for a proton curving in half a tesla, which is the step 7 check.

7.30hardLecture slides, module 4, slide 36

A proton moves with a velocity of 3.5 x 106 m/s in a magnetic field of 0.25 T at an angle of 60°. Which component of the velocity sets the RADIUS of the helix, and which sets its PITCH?

  • ✓v sin 60 sets the radius, and v cos 60 sets the pitchCorrect. Sine for the radius, cosine for the pitch.
  • ✗v cos 60 sets the radius, and v sin 60 sets the pitchWrong, and it is the trap, since these two are the easiest pair in the course to swap.
  • ✗The full v sets bothWrong. Using the whole speed for both ignores that only the perpendicular part is deflected.
  • ✗v sin 60 sets bothWrong. If the pitch used sine too, the path would never advance along the field.
  • ✗v tan 60 sets the radius, and v sin 60 sets the pitchWrong. A tangent does not appear anywhere in this topic.
Background, so this is not just a formula

When a particle enters a field at an angle rather than square on, split its velocity into two parts and treat them separately. The part ACROSS the field feels the magnetic force and curls into a circle. The part ALONG the field feels no force at all, since a charge moving parallel to the field is not cut by it, so it just carries on at constant speed. A circle plus a steady drift along the axis is a helix, like a stretched spring.

Given
  • v = 3.5 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹
  • B = 0.25 T
  • θ = 60° between the velocity and the field

Find: Which velocity component sets the RADIUS, and which sets the PITCH

Formula

r =m v sin θ(qB)

Why this one. The formula itself answers the question. Only the perpendicular component appears in it, so only v sin θ can affect the radius. The pitch, which is how far the helix advances along the field in one full turn, is set by the parallel component v cos θ multiplied by the time for one revolution, because that is the part doing the advancing.

What each symbol means
  • v sin θ = the component ACROSS the field, which is bent into the circle
  • v cos θ = the component ALONG the field, which is never bent
  • r = the radius of the helix, in metres
  • the pitch = the advance along the field per complete turn, in metres
The working, line by line
  1. Split the velocity at the angle to the FIELD:
  2. Across the field: v sin 60° = 3.5 × 10⁶ × 0.866 = 3.03 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹
  3. Along the field: v cos 60° = 3.5 × 10⁶ × 0.5 = 1.75 × 10⁶ m s⁻¹
  4. The across part feels the force qvB and curls, so it sets the RADIUS
  5. The along part feels no force and drifts, so it sets the PITCH
  6. Answer: v sin 60 sets the radius, v cos 60 sets the pitch
Check it

Test the two extremes, which settles it without memorising. At θ = 90°, straight across the field, sin 90 = 1 and cos 90 = 0, so the radius is at its largest and the pitch is zero: a flat circle, which is right. At θ = 0°, straight along the field, sin 0 = 0 so there is no circle at all and the particle flies straight: infinite pitch, zero radius, also right. Only the sine-for-radius pairing survives both tests.

Split the velocity into the part ACROSS the field and the part ALONG it. The perpendicular part, v sin θ, is what the magnetic force acts on, so it drives the circular motion and fixes the radius: r = mv sin θ over qB.
