The parallel part, v cos θ, feels no force at all, because sin 0 is zero along the field, so the proton simply drifts steadily in that direction, and that drift over one period is the pitch: p = v cos θ times T. Circle across the field, drift along it, and the two together trace a helix.

8Sources of a Magnetic Field

Speed tip. The loop centre formula has NO π in the denominator. The straight wire and the toroid both do. That difference alone identifies the formula.

8.1easyReference sheet R.4

An Amperian loop is an {{1}} closed path, chosen to make Ampere's law easy.

Answer:

Imaginary, exactly like the Gaussian surface in electrostatics. It is a path you draw on the diagram, not a wire, and you choose its shape to match the symmetry: a circle around a straight wire, a rectangle threading a solenoid.

8.2easyReference sheet R.4

What is a solenoid, and what is a toroid?

  • ✓A solenoid is a long coil producing a uniform field inside; a toroid is a solenoid bent into a ringCorrect on both.
  • ✗A solenoid is a single loop; a toroid is a straight wireWrong. A solenoid is many turns, not one.
  • ✗A solenoid is a ring; a toroid is a long coilWrong, and reversed.
  • ✗Both are single loops of different radiusWrong. Both are multi-turn coils.
  • ✗A solenoid carries AC and a toroid carries DCWrong. Neither is defined by the kind of current.

A long coil, and the same coil closed into a doughnut. The uniform interior field is what makes a solenoid useful, and bending it into a ring confines the field entirely within the loop, which is why toroids are used where stray fields would be a nuisance. Note their formulas differ in exactly the way the previous question describes.

8.3easyReference sheet R.1

What is the value of the permeability of free space, μ₀?

  • ✓4 π x 10-7 H m-1Correct. 4 π x 10-7 H m-1.
  • ✗8.85 x 10-12 H m-1Wrong. That is ε₀, the electric counterpart.
  • ✗8.99 x 109 H m-1Wrong. That is the Coulomb constant.
  • ✗4 π x 107 H m-1Wrong sign on the exponent.
  • ✗1.602 x 10-19 H m-1Wrong. That is the elementary charge.

μ₀ = 4 π x 10-7 H m-1. Leaving it as 4 π rather than 1.257 x 10-6 is usually the faster route, because the π very often cancels against the 2 π r in the straight wire formula.

8.4easyReference sheet R.4

A solenoid is a long coil producing a uniform field inside it, and a toroid is a solenoid bent into a {{1}}.

Answer:

A ring. Closing the coil into a doughnut confines the field entirely within the loop, so a toroid produces almost no stray field outside it, which is why it is chosen where interference would be a problem.

8.5mediumReference sheet R.1

Both the Biot-Savart law and Ampere's law give the magnetic field of a current. When is Ampere's law the one to reach for?

  • ✓When the arrangement has symmetry, because it is the shortcutCorrect, and symmetry is the condition that makes it usable.
  • ✗Always, since Biot-Savart is wrongWrong. Both are correct; they differ in convenience.
  • ✗Only for a single current elementWrong. That is Biot-Savart's domain.
  • ✗When the current is changing with timeWrong. Changing currents belong to induction, a different topic.
  • ✗When there is no current enclosedWrong. With no enclosed current the law still holds but tells you little.

Biot-Savart is general but laborious: it gives the field of one tiny current element and you must sum over the whole conductor. Ampere's law is the shortcut, but it is only SOLVABLE when you can choose a loop on which B is constant and along the path, which needs symmetry. That is exactly what Test 2 question 5 tests with its one-word answer.

8.6mediumReference sheet R.1

The Biot-Savart law gives the field of a current element, and like Coulomb's law it falls as 1 over r to the power {{1}}.

Answer:

Squared, exactly like Coulomb's law. That is the point Test 2 question 12 makes with its gap. Do not confuse it with the whole straight WIRE, whose field falls only as 1 over r, because summing the contributions of every element along an infinite line changes the power.

8.7mediumReference sheet R.1

A circular loop of N turns and radius R carries current I. The field at its centre is:

  • ✓B =μ₀ N I2RCorrect. B =μ₀ N I2R, with no π in the denominator, which is what separates the flat loop from the straight wire and the toroid.
  • ✗B =μ₀ N I2 π RWrong. That π belongs to the toroid, not the flat loop.
  • ✗B = μ₀ n I, with n the turns per metreWrong. That is the solenoid.
  • ✗B =μ₀ I2 π RWrong. That is the long straight wire.
  • ✗B =μ₀ N I R2Wrong. R divides here; it does not multiply.

B =μ₀ N I2R, with no π in the denominator, which is what distinguishes it from the straight wire and the toroid. Note the R here is the radius of the LOOP itself, not a distance you choose, because this formula gives the field at one specific place: the centre.

8.8mediumReference sheet R.1

Ampere's law states that the closed line integral of B dot dl equals μ₀ times the {{1}} current.

Answer:

The ENCLOSED current, meaning the current threading through the loop you have drawn. Currents outside the loop contribute nothing to the integral, exactly as external charges contribute nothing to the flux in Gauss's law. The two laws are the same idea in their two subjects.

8.9mediumReference sheet R.1

The permeability of free space is written μ₀ and equals 4 π times 10 to the power minus {{1}} H per m.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

μ₀ is written as 4π × 10⁻⁷ rather than as 1.257 × 10⁻⁶, and the odd form is deliberate. Almost every magnetic formula for a wire or a toroid carries a 2π or a 4π underneath, so keeping the π visible on top lets it cancel by eye instead of by calculator. That single habit removes the commonest arithmetic error in the whole module.

Given
  • μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ H m⁻¹, the permeability of free space

Find: The exponent in 4π × 10 to the power minus ___

Formula

B =μ₀I(2πr)

Why this one. Substituting μ₀ into the straight wire law shows why the form is chosen. The 4π on top and the 2π underneath cancel down to 2, leaving B = 2 × 10⁻⁷ × I ÷ r, which needs no π at all.

What each symbol means
  • μ₀ = the permeability of free space, in henries per metre
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • r = the distance from the wire, in metres
  • 2 × 10⁻⁷ = what μ₀ ÷ 2π simplifies to exactly
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is 7.
  2. μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ H m⁻¹.
  3. In the wire formula: 4π ÷ 2π = 2, so B = (2 × 10⁻⁷ × I) ÷ r.
  4. Why not 12: that is 8.85 × 10⁻¹², which is ε₀, the ELECTRIC partner.
  5. Why not 19: that is the elementary charge, 1.6 × 10⁻¹⁹ C.
  6. Why not 9: that is 9 × 10⁹, the Coulomb constant, and it is POSITIVE.
Check it

Pair the two constants of free space so neither can be reached for by mistake: ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² is electric, μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ is magnetic. Their product is 1 ÷ c², which is the relation Maxwell used to identify light. If you can state that link you will never confuse the two.

Minus 7. Keeping μ₀ as 4 π x 10-7 lets the π cancel in B = μ₀ I / (2 π r).

8.10mediumThe real test 2, question 5

Ampère's law is most useful for conductors with high {{1}}.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

Ampère's law says that if you walk right around any closed loop and add up the magnetic field along your path, the total equals μ₀ times the current threaded through that loop. It is ALWAYS true. But being true and being useful are different things. To actually get B out of it you need the field to have the same size everywhere on your loop and to point along it, so it can be pulled outside the sum. That only happens when the arrangement has a shape that repeats itself: a long straight wire, a solenoid, a toroid.

Find: The word that completes: conductors with high ___

The working, line by line
  1. The answer is SYMMETRY.
  2. Why not resistance: Ampère's law contains no resistance at all, only current and field.
  3. Why not permeability: μ₀ appears as a constant of the medium, but a high value does not make the law easier to solve.
  4. Why not current: a big current gives a big field, but it does nothing to make the field uniform along a loop, which is the actual difficulty.
Check it

Test it on a real case. For a long straight wire you can draw a circle around it on which B is constant and tangential, so the law collapses to B × 2πr = μ₀I and B falls straight out. Try the same on a bent, lumpy conductor and there is no such circle, so the law still holds but gives you nothing. Symmetry is what made the difference.

Symmetry. Ampere's law is always true, but it is only USEFUL when you can choose a loop on which the field has the same magnitude everywhere and points along the loop, so that the integral collapses to B times the length. That needs symmetry: a long straight wire, a solenoid, a toroid. Without it the law still holds but cannot be solved for B, which is exactly the same reason Gauss's law is only useful for spherical, cylindrical or planar charge distributions.

8.11mediumThe real test 2, question 12

According to the Biot-Savart law, the magnetic field around a current element decreases with the square of the {{1}}.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

The Biot-Savart law is the magnetic twin of Coulomb's law. Coulomb tells you the electric field produced by one small piece of charge; Biot-Savart tells you the magnetic field produced by one small piece of current. Both spread their influence over the surface of a sphere as they travel outwards, and a sphere's area grows as r², so both are diluted by the same factor.

Find: The word that completes: decreases with the square of the ___

Formula

dB =μ₀I dl sin θ(4πr²)

Why this one. The r² sitting on the bottom is the whole answer, and it is worth writing the law out to see it rather than trying to recall a sentence about it. The same r² appears in Coulomb's law for the same geometric reason, so the two laws can be remembered as one idea.

What each symbol means
  • dB = the tiny field contributed by one small piece of the wire
  • I = the current in the wire, in amperes
  • dl = the length of that small piece, in metres
  • r = the distance from that piece to the point of interest, and it is SQUARED
  • θ = the angle between the piece of wire and the line joining it to the point
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is DISTANCE.
  2. Why not current: the field is directly proportional to current, not inversely, and there is no square on it.
  3. Why not permeability: μ₀ is a constant and does not vary from point to point.
  4. Why not angle: the angle enters through sin θ, which is not a square at all.
Check it

Guard against a real trap here. One small ELEMENT of current gives a field falling as 1/r², but a whole infinite straight WIRE gives a field falling as 1/r, because it is the sum of countless elements stretching away in both directions. The question says current element, so the square is right; had it said long straight wire, it would not be.

Distance. The Biot-Savart law carries a 1/r2 factor, so the contribution of a current element falls off with the square of the distance from it, exactly as Coulomb's law does for charge. Do not confuse this with the field of a whole long straight wire, which falls off as 1/r rather than 1/r2, because that is the sum of every element along an infinite line.

8.12mediumThe real test 2, question 14

At a distance of 5 cm from a long straight wire, the magnetic field measured is 4.0 × 10-5 T. Calculate the current I flowing through the wire. (Use μ0 = 4π × 10-7 T/A)

  • ✓10 ACorrect. I = 2 π r B / mu0 = 10 A once π cancels.
  • ✗20 AWrong, and exactly double: this drops the 2 in the denominator of the field formula.
  • ✗3 AWrong. No route to 3 A from these numbers.
  • ✗15 AWrong. No route to 15 A from these numbers.
  • ✗12 AWrong. No route to 12 A from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Current in a long straight wire wraps a magnetic field around itself in circles, like ripples round a stick in water. Grip the wire with your right hand, thumb along the current, and your fingers curl the way the field points. The field weakens as you move away, but only as 1/r, not 1/r², because the wire is a line rather than a point and there is always more of it stretching away to help.

Given
  • r = 5 cm = 0.05 m (distance from the wire; convert first)
  • B = 4.0 × 10⁻⁵ T (the measured field)
  • μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T m A⁻¹

Find: The current I in the wire, in amperes

Formula

B =μ₀I(2πr)

Why this one. Long straight wire is the signature phrase for this formula. Its distinguishing mark against the solenoid is the 2πr underneath, which is the circumference of the circular field line you are standing on: the field spreads itself evenly around that circle, so the longer the circle, the weaker the field.

What each symbol means
  • B = the field at distance r, in teslas
  • μ₀ = the permeability of free space, 4π × 10⁻⁷
  • I = the current in the wire, in amperes
  • r = the perpendicular distance from the wire, in metres, NOT squared
  • 2πr = the circumference of the field circle at that distance
The working, line by line
  1. Convert: 5 cm = 0.05 m
  2. Rearrange: I = (2πrB) ÷ μ₀
  3. I = (2π × 0.05 × 4.0 × 10⁻⁵) ÷ (4π × 10⁻⁷)
  4. π cancels top and bottom, which is the tidy way to do it:
  5. I = (2 × 0.05 × 4.0 × 10⁻⁵) ÷ (4 × 10⁻⁷)
  6. Top: 2 × 0.05 = 0.1, then 0.1 × 4.0 × 10⁻⁵ = 4.0 × 10⁻⁶
  7. I = (4.0 × 10⁻⁶) ÷ (4 × 10⁻⁷)
  8. Front numbers: 4 ÷ 4 = 1
  9. Powers of ten: 10⁻⁶ ÷ 10⁻⁷ = 10¹
  10. I = 1 × 10 = 10 A
Check it

Cancelling π before touching the calculator removes the commonest slip in this question, which is keying 4π as 4 and then multiplying by π again. Sanity: 10 A is a large but ordinary current, and a field of tens of microteslas at 5 cm is the right order, being comparable to the Earth's own field.

For a long straight wire B = mu0 I / (2 π r), so I = 2 π r B / mu0. Substituting with r = 0.05 m: I = (2 π x 0.05 x 4 x 10-5) / (4 π x 10-7). The π cancels top and bottom, leaving I = (2 x 0.05 x 4 x 10-5) / (4 x 10-7) = 4 x 10-6 / 4 x 10-7 = 10 A. Cancelling π before substituting saves the arithmetic.

8.13mediumReference sheet R.1

At the centre of a loop of N turns and radius R the field is B =μ₀ N I2R, a formula with no {{1}} in its denominator.

Answer:

No π. That is the quickest way to tell the flat loop apart from the straight wire, B = μ₀ I over 2 π r, and the toroid, B = μ₀ N I over 2 π r. The R here is the radius of the loop itself, since the formula gives the field at one particular place: its centre.

8.14mediumReference sheet R.1

The toroid formula B = μ₀ N I over 2 π r uses the TOTAL number of turns, not the turns per metre.

Answer: true

True, and the inconsistency with the solenoid is deliberate on the examiner's part. Solenoid uses small n, turns per metre. Toroid uses capital N, the total count. Reading which symbol the formula carries, before substituting, is the whole defence.

8.15mediumLecture slides, module 4, slide 50

A long straight conductor carries a current of 12 A. Calculate the magnetic field at a point 5 cm from the conductor.

  • ✓4.8 x 10-5 TCorrect. 2 x 10-7 × 12 divided by 0.05.
  • ✗4.8 x 10-7 TWrong by a factor of 100, which is leaving r as 5 rather than 0.05.
  • ✗2.4 x 10-5 TWrong by a factor of two, from using μ₀ over 4 π instead of 2 π.
  • ✗4.8 x 10-3 TWrong by a factor of 100 the other way.
  • ✗1.5 x 10-4 TWrong. No route to this from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Current in a straight wire wraps a magnetic field around itself in circles. Grip the wire with your right hand, thumb pointing along the current, and your fingers curl the way the field points. The strength falls off as 1 over r, not as the inverse square, because a wire is a line rather than a point: move away and there is always more wire stretching off in both directions to make up for the distance.

Given
  • I = 12 A
  • r = 5 cm = 0.05 m
  • μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T m A⁻¹

Find: The magnetic field B at that point, in teslas

Formula

B =μ₀I(2πr)

Why this one. Long straight conductor is the phrase that selects this formula. The 2πr underneath is the circumference of the field circle you are standing on: the field spreads itself around that circle, so a bigger circle means a weaker field. The solenoid formula has no π underneath at all, which is the quickest way to tell the two apart.

What each symbol means
  • B = the field at that distance, in teslas
  • μ₀ = the permeability of free space, 4π × 10⁻⁷
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • r = the perpendicular distance from the wire, in metres, NOT squared
  • 2πr = the circumference of the circular field line
The working, line by line
  1. Convert: 5 cm = 0.05 m
  2. B = (4π × 10⁻⁷ × 12) ÷ (2π × 0.05)
  3. Cancel π top and bottom before anything else:
  4. B = (4 × 10⁻⁷ × 12) ÷ (2 × 0.05)
  5. Simplify 4 ÷ 2 = 2, so B = (2 × 10⁻⁷ × 12) ÷ 0.05
  6. Top: 2 × 12 = 24, so top = 24 × 10⁻⁷ = 2.4 × 10⁻⁶
  7. B = (2.4 × 10⁻⁶) ÷ 0.05
  8. B = 4.8 × 10⁻⁵ T
Check it

Learn the shortcut this cancelling reveals: μ₀ ÷ 2π is exactly 2 × 10⁻⁷, so B = 2 × 10⁻⁷ × I ÷ r every time for a straight wire. That one substitution removes the commonest slip, which is keying 4π as 4 and then multiplying by π again. Sanity: 4.8 × 10⁻⁵ T is roughly the Earth's own field, which is the right order for a domestic current a few centimetres away.

Convert first: 5 cm = 0.05 m. Then B = μ₀ I over 2 π r. Keep μ₀ as 4 π x 10-7 and the π cancels: B = (4 π x 10-7 x 12) / (2 π x 0.05) = (2 x 10-7 x 12) / 0.05 = 2.4 x 10-6 / 0.05 = 4.8 x 10-5 T. The shortcut worth memorising is that μ₀ over 2 π is exactly 2 x 10-7, so B = 2 x 10-7 x I over r for every straight wire.

8.16mediumLecture slides, module 4, slide 63

A straight conductor carries 15 A. Calculate the magnetic field 4 cm away.

  • ✓7.5 x 10-5 TCorrect. 3.0 x 10-6 divided by 0.04.
  • ✗3.75 x 10-5 TWrong by a factor of two, from halving μ₀ somewhere.
  • ✗7.5 x 10-7 TWrong by a factor of 100, from leaving the distance in cm.
  • ✗6.0 x 10-5 TWrong. This uses 5 cm rather than the 4 cm given.
  • ✗1.9 x 10-4 TWrong. No route to this from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

The same straight wire law, with different numbers, which is the point of setting it twice: the routine should become automatic. Convert the centimetres, use μ₀ ÷ 2π = 2 × 10⁻⁷ so no π ever reaches the calculator, then divide by the distance to the first power.

Given
  • I = 15 A
  • r = 4 cm = 0.04 m
  • μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T m A⁻¹

Find: The magnetic field B at 4 cm from the wire, in teslas

Formula

B =μ₀I(2πr)

Why this one. A straight conductor and a perpendicular distance, so the same formula as before. Nothing about a bigger current or a shorter distance changes which law applies; they only change the numbers that go into it.

What each symbol means
  • B = the field, in teslas
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • r = the distance from the wire, in metres
  • μ₀ ÷ 2π = 2 × 10⁻⁷ exactly, worth using as a single constant
The working, line by line
  1. Convert: 4 cm = 0.04 m
  2. Use the shortcut form: B = (2 × 10⁻⁷ × I) ÷ r
  3. B = (2 × 10⁻⁷ × 15) ÷ 0.04
  4. Top: 2 × 15 = 30, so top = 30 × 10⁻⁷ = 3.0 × 10⁻⁶
  5. B = (3.0 × 10⁻⁶) ÷ 0.04
  6. B = 7.5 × 10⁻⁵ T
Check it

Compare with the previous question as a cross-check on the physics. There, 12 A at 5 cm gave 4.8 × 10⁻⁵ T. Here the current is 1.25 times larger and the distance 0.8 times as far, so the field should be 1.25 ÷ 0.8 = 1.5625 times stronger. And 4.8 × 1.5625 = 7.5, which is exactly what came out. Proportional reasoning like that catches an arithmetic slip in seconds.

Same shortcut, new numbers. B = 2 x 10-7 x I over r = 2 x 10-7 x 15 / 0.04 = 3.0 x 10-6 / 0.04 = 7.5 x 10-5 T. Compare it with the 12 A at 5 cm case: more current and a shorter distance both push the field up, and it duly rises from 4.8 to 7.5 x 10-5 T. A sanity check that costs nothing.

8.17hardReference sheet R.1

A solenoid uses B = μ₀ n I and a toroid uses B =μ₀ N I2 π r. What is the difference between the n and the N?

  • ✓n is turns PER METRE, while N is the TOTAL number of turnsCorrect, and it is the trap the examiner sets.
  • ✗They mean the same thing, written differentlyWrong. One is a density and the other a count, and they differ here by a factor of 2.5.
  • ✗n is the total turns and N is turns per metreWrong, and exactly reversed.
  • ✗n counts layers and N counts turnsWrong. Neither counts layers.
  • ✗n is for a single loop and N for manyWrong. Both formulas describe many turns.

Small n is a DENSITY, turns per metre, found as N divided by the length. Capital N is a plain count. Test 2 question 9 punishes the confusion directly: 800 turns over 0.4 m gives n = 2000 per metre, and putting 800 into the solenoid formula produces an answer that is on the option list. The solenoid also gives a UNIFORM field inside, which is why it is the standard way to make one.

8.18hardThe real test 2, question 9

A solenoid of length 0.4 m has 800 turns and produces a magnetic field of 2.5 × 10-4 T inside it. Calculate the current flowing through the solenoid. (Use μ0 = 4π × 10-7 T/A)

  • ✓0.1 ACorrect. n = 800/0.4 = 2000 per metre, so I = B/(mu0 n) = 0.1 A.
  • ✗2.6 AWrong. This is roughly what you get using N = 800 directly instead of n = 2000 per metre.
  • ✗5.9 AWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
  • ✗0.6 AWrong. No route to this value; it is six times the answer.
  • ✗3.1 AWrong. No route to this value from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A solenoid is a wire wound into a long tube. Each turn makes its own small circular field, and stacked side by side they reinforce each other inside the tube and largely cancel outside it. The result is a nearly uniform field down the middle, like a bar magnet made of wire. What matters is not how many turns you have but how tightly they are packed, which is why the formula uses turns PER METRE.

Given
  • L = 0.4 m (the length of the solenoid)
  • N = 800 turns
  • B = 2.5 × 10⁻⁴ T (the field inside)
  • μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T m A⁻¹

Find: The current I flowing through the solenoid, in amperes

Formula

B = μ₀nI, so I =B(μ₀n)

Why this one. The word solenoid picks this formula out from its neighbours by a single feature: there is no π on the bottom. The long straight wire has 2πr underneath and the toroid has 2πr too, but the solenoid does not, because its field does not spread out radially. Spot the word solenoid and you have already halved the work.

What each symbol means
  • B = the field inside the solenoid, in teslas
  • μ₀ = the permeability of free space, 4π × 10⁻⁷
  • n = the number of turns PER METRE, found as N ÷ L
  • I = the current, in amperes
  • N = the total number of turns, which is NOT the same as n
The working, line by line
  1. First turn N into n, turns per metre:
  2. n = N ÷ L = 800 ÷ 0.4 = 2000 turns per metre
  3. Bottom: μ₀ × n = 4π × 10⁻⁷ × 2000
  4. 4π = 12.566, so 12.566 × 10⁻⁷ × 2000 = 2.513 × 10⁻³
  5. I = B ÷ (μ₀n) = (2.5 × 10⁻⁴) ÷ (2.513 × 10⁻³)
  6. Front numbers: 2.5 ÷ 2.513 = 0.995
  7. Powers of ten: 10⁻⁴ ÷ 10⁻³ = 10⁻¹
  8. I = 0.995 × 10⁻¹ = 0.0995 ≈ 0.1 A
Check it

The commonest error is feeding in 800 instead of 2000, which would make the current 2.5 times larger, about 0.25 A, and none of the options match that. Whenever a solenoid question gives a LENGTH, that length exists to convert N into n. If you never used it, you have made this mistake.

Inside a solenoid B = mu0 n I, where n is turns PER METRE, not the total number of turns. First n = N/L = 800/0.4 = 2000 turns per metre. Then I = B/(mu0 n) = 2.5 x 10-4 / (4pi x 10-7 x 2000) = 2.5 x 10-4 / 2.513 x 10-3 = 0.0995, that is 0.1 A. Using 800 instead of 2000 is the mistake this question is built to catch.

8.19hardReference sheet R.1

A solenoid is 0.5 m long and has 1500 turns. What value of n goes into B = μ₀ n I?

  • ✓3000 per metreCorrect. 1500 divided by 0.5.
  • ✗1500Wrong, and it is the trap: 1500 is N, the total count, not the density.
  • ✗750 per metreWrong. This MULTIPLIES by 0.5 instead of dividing.
  • ✗0.5Wrong. That is the length in metres.
  • ✗3000 turns in totalWrong. The number is right but the meaning is not: there are 1500 turns in total, and 3000 is how many there would be per metre.
Background, so this is not just a formula

In B = μ₀nI the letter n does not mean the number of turns. It means turns PER METRE, the packing density. This matters because the field inside a solenoid depends on how tightly the wire is wound, not on how much wire there is: stretch the same 1500 turns over twice the length and the field halves.

Given
  • length L = 0.5 m
  • N = 1500 turns in total

Find: The value of n to put into B = μ₀nI, with its unit

Formula

n =NL

Why this one. Whenever a solenoid question gives you a LENGTH, that length is there to convert the total turns into turns per metre. If you finish a solenoid problem without having used the length, you have almost certainly fed in N by mistake.

What each symbol means
  • n = turns per metre, with the unit m⁻¹
  • N = the total number of turns, a pure count with no unit
  • L = the length of the solenoid, in metres
The working, line by line
  1. n = N ÷ L
  2. n = 1500 ÷ 0.5
  3. Dividing by 0.5 is the same as multiplying by 2
  4. n = 3000 per metre
Check it

The unit is the mark here. Two of the options give the number 3000, but one of them calls it 3000 turns in total, which is wrong: there are only 1500 turns, packed at 3000 per metre because the solenoid is half a metre long. Read the unit on the option, not just the digits. Note too that n came out larger than N, which always happens when the solenoid is shorter than a metre.

n is turns PER METRE, so n = N over L = 1500 over 0.5 = 3000 per metre. Dividing by a number less than one makes it larger, which catches people out. Test 2 question 9 sets the same trap with 800 turns over 0.4 m, and putting the raw turn count in produces an answer that is on the option list.

9Electromagnetic Induction

Speed tip. Nothing changing means no emf, whatever the field strength. Faraday needs a RATE, so look for a time in the question.

9.1easyReference sheet R.4

Magnetic flux is measured in webers, and one weber is one tesla {{1}}.

Answer:

One tesla square metre, which you can read straight off Φ = BA cos θ: a flux density in tesla times an area in square metres. Keep the two units apart: the TESLA measures flux density B, the WEBER measures flux Φ, and Test 2 question 11 depends on knowing that it is the FLUX that must change to induce an emf.

9.2easyThe real test 2, question 1

A 2 H inductor carries 5 A of current. What is the stored energy?

  • ✓25 JCorrect. U = 1/2 L I2 = 0.5 x 2 x 25 = 25 J.
  • ✗15 JWrong. No route to 15 J from L = 2 H and I = 5 A.
  • ✗20 JWrong. This squares the inductance instead of the current: 0.5 x 4 x 10 or similar.
  • ✗10 JWrong, and it is the common slip: L x I = 2 x 5 = 10, forgetting both the half and the square.
  • ✗30 JWrong. No route to 30 J from these numbers.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A coil does not store energy in the wire, it stores it in the magnetic field filling the space around and inside the coil. Building that field takes work, because while the current is rising the coil fights back with an induced emf that opposes the change. Once the current is steady the fighting stops and the energy sits in the field, ready to be given back the instant you open the switch. That is why a switch sparks: the field collapses and dumps its energy across the gap.

Given
  • L = 2 H (the inductance, in henries)
  • I = 5 A (the steady current in the coil)

Find: The energy U stored in the inductor, in joules (J)

Formula

U = ½LI²

Why this one. An inductance and a current are given and an energy is asked for, and this is the only formula joining those three. Notice its shape: a half, a property of the device, and the square of what flows through it. The capacitor version U = ½CV² has exactly the same shape, which is worth memorising as one pattern rather than two formulas.

What each symbol means
  • U = the stored magnetic energy, in joules
  • L = the inductance, in henries (H)
  • I = the current, in amperes, and it is SQUARED
The working, line by line
  1. U = ½ × L × I²
  2. I² = 5² = 25
  3. U = ½ × 2 × 25
  4. ½ × 2 = 1
  5. U = 1 × 25 = 25 J
Check it

The current is squared, so doubling the current to 10 A would give 100 J, four times as much, not twice. If halving or doubling a current changes your energy by the same factor, you dropped the square.

Energy stored in an inductor is U = 1/2 L I2, the magnetic twin of the capacitor's U = 1/2 C V2. Substituting: U = 1/2 x 2 x 52 = 1 x 25 = 25 J. The current is SQUARED and the inductance is not, which is the only place this can go wrong.

9.3easyThe real test 2, question 11

A changing magnetic {{1}} induces an emf.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

Faraday found that it is not the magnetic field that makes an emf, but the CHANGE in how much of it passes through a loop. Hold a magnet still inside a coil, however strong, and nothing happens; move it and a voltage appears. The quantity that has to change is the flux, Φ = BA cos θ, which bundles three things at once: the strength of the field, the area of the loop, and the angle between them.

Find: The word that completes: a changing magnetic ___ induces an emf

Formula

emf = −N ΔΦΔt, where Φ = BA cos θ

Why this one. Writing the law out shows exactly why the missing word is flux and not field. Because flux contains B, A and cos θ, an emf can be produced in three different ways: change the field strength, change the area of the loop, or simply rotate the loop in a steady field. A generator uses the third, spinning a coil between fixed magnets, and no answer built on the word field can explain that.

What each symbol means
  • emf = the induced voltage, in volts
  • N = the number of turns in the coil
  • Φ = the magnetic flux, in webers (Wb)
  • ΔΦ ÷ Δt = the RATE of change of flux, which is what actually matters
  • the minus sign = Lenz's law, saying the induced effect opposes the change that caused it
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is FLUX.
  2. Why not force: a force is a push on a charge or a wire, and it has nothing to change through a loop.
  3. Why not moment: a magnetic moment describes a current loop as a source of field, not what passes through one.
  4. Why not permeability: that is a property of the material and it is not what varies with time here.
Check it

Two words earn the marks in Faraday questions: RATE and FLUX. Say rate of change of flux, and you have stated the law. Say a strong magnetic field induces an emf and you have said something false, because a strong field held still induces nothing at all.

Flux. Faraday's law says the induced emf equals the RATE OF CHANGE of magnetic flux, not of the field itself: emf = -dΦ/dt. The distinction earns marks, because flux is B times A times cos(θ), so an emf can be induced by changing the field, by changing the area, or merely by rotating the loop, which is exactly how a generator works with a constant magnet.

9.4easyReference sheet R.4

One weber is equal to:

  • ✓One tesla square metreCorrect. A tesla times a square metre, exactly as Φ = BA says.
  • ✗One tesla per square metreWrong, and inverted: the area multiplies, it does not divide.
  • ✗One tesla per metreWrong. The area is squared metres and it multiplies.
  • ✗One tesla per secondWrong. That would be a rate of change of flux density.
  • ✗One ampere metre squaredWrong. That is the unit of MAGNETIC MOMENT, m = NIA.

A tesla square metre, straight from Φ = BA cos θ: a flux density in tesla times an area in square metres. Keep the pair apart, because the tesla measures flux DENSITY while the weber measures FLUX, and Faraday's law is about the flux changing.

9.5mediumReference sheet R.1

A conductor of length L moves at speed v through a magnetic field B, cutting the field lines. The emf induced is:

  • ✓BLvCorrect. All three multiply.
  • ✗BLvWrong. A faster rod induces MORE emf, so v cannot be in the denominator.
  • ✗BLvWrong on both placements.
  • ✗BLv squaredWrong. The speed enters to the first power only.
  • ✗LBvWrong. This inverts almost everything.

emf = BLv, the three quantities simply multiplied. It is Faraday's law in disguise: as the rod moves it sweeps out area at a rate Lv, so the flux through the circuit changes at BLv, and that rate of change IS the emf. Stop the rod and the emf stops instantly, because change is the whole word.

9.6mediumReference sheet R.1

The energy stored in an inductor is W = one half L I², in which the quantity that is SQUARED is the {{1}}.

Answer:

The current. W = one half L I² mirrors one half C V² for a capacitor, and the half is there for the same reason in both: the quantity climbed from zero rather than sitting at its final value throughout. Test 2 question 1 uses it, and its trap answer of 10 J is what you get by writing L times I and forgetting both the half and the square.

9.7mediumReference sheet R.4

An eddy current is:

  • ✓A loop of current induced inside a solid conductor by changing fluxCorrect, and the name comes from the swirling pattern.
  • ✗The current in the primary coil of a transformerWrong. That is simply the primary current.
  • ✗A current that flows only in superconductorsWrong. They appear in ordinary conductors.
  • ✗The leakage current through an insulatorWrong. That is leakage, a different thing.
  • ✗A current that alternates at very high frequencyWrong. Frequency is not what defines them.

Changing flux induces an emf in ANY conducting loop, and a solid block of metal is full of possible loops, so swirling currents appear inside it. They are Faraday's law applied where nobody wired a circuit. They waste energy as heat, which is why transformer cores are built from thin laminated sheets rather than solid metal, breaking up the paths the eddies would take.

9.8mediumReference sheet R.1

A loop sits in a strong but perfectly STEADY magnetic field. What emf is induced in it?

  • ✓None, because it is the rate of CHANGE of flux that induces an emfCorrect. A steady flux induces nothing.
  • ✗A large one, because the field is strongWrong. Strength is irrelevant; only change matters.
  • ✗A small but non-zero oneWrong. It is exactly zero.
  • ✗One proportional to the area of the loopWrong. Area enters the flux, but a constant flux still has zero rate of change.
  • ✗One proportional to the field strengthWrong, for the same reason.

Faraday’s law is emf = minus N dΦ/dt. If the flux is not changing, its rate of change is zero and so is the emf, no matter how strong the field is. CHANGE is the whole word: the field can be enormous and still induce nothing.

9.9mediumReference sheet R.2

In Faraday’s law the key word is {{1}}: a steady flux induces nothing.

Answer:

Background, so this is not just a formula

Faraday's law is about CHANGE, and that is genuinely counter-intuitive. A coil sitting in the strongest magnetic field ever built produces no voltage at all, provided nothing is moving. Push a magnet towards a coil and a voltage appears; stop with the magnet inside and it disappears; pull it out and it appears again with the opposite sign. What the coil responds to is not the flux but the rate at which the flux changes.

Find: The key word in Faraday's law

Formula

emf = −N ΔΦΔt

Why this one. The Δ symbols are the answer written in algebra. It is ΔΦ, the CHANGE in flux, divided by Δt, the time it took. Both are differences; neither is an amount. A steady flux gives ΔΦ = 0 and therefore no emf.

What each symbol means
  • emf = the induced voltage, in volts
  • N = the number of turns, which multiplies the result
  • ΔΦ = the CHANGE in flux, in webers
  • Δt = the time over which it changed, in seconds
  • the minus sign = Lenz's law, the induced effect opposing the change
The working, line by line
  1. The answer is CHANGE.
  2. A steady flux, however large, gives ΔΦ = 0 and therefore no emf.
  3. Why not strength: a strong but constant field induces nothing whatsoever.
  4. Why not area: changing the area is one WAY to change the flux, but it is not the key idea, and rotating the loop works just as well.
  5. Why not direction: the same objection, since it is one route to a change rather than the principle.
Check it

Test yourself with the generator, which settles it. A generator spins a coil between magnets of FIXED strength and produces a steady supply of electricity. Nothing about the field is changing, only the angle of the coil, so the flux BA cos θ changes because of the cosine. Only the word change survives that test; strength, area and direction all fail it.

Change. The law is about dΦ/dt, the rate of change of flux, not about the flux itself.

9.10mediumReference sheet R.2

The − sign in Faraday’s law expresses Lenz’s law, and it is required by conservation of energy.

Answer: true

True. Without the opposition, an induced current would strengthen the very change that created it, and the system would generate energy from nothing.

9.11mediumReference sheet R.1

Faraday's law is emf = minus N dΦ/dt, so what induces an emf is not the flux itself but its rate of {{1}}.

Answer:

Change. A coil parked in the strongest steady magnet you can find generates nothing at all. Move it, turn it, or switch the field, and it does. That single word is why a generator must spin and why a transformer cannot run on DC.

9.12mediumReference sheet R.1

A rod 0.25 m long moves at 4 m/s through a 0.2 T field, cutting the lines squarely. What emf is induced?

  • ✓0.2 VCorrect. 0.2 × 0.25 × 4.
  • ✗5 VWrong. This divides somewhere rather than multiplying throughout.
  • ✗0.05 VWrong. This omits the speed.
  • ✗1.25 VWrong. No route to this from these numbers.
  • ✗0.8 VWrong. This drops the field strength.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Push a rod across a magnetic field and the free electrons inside it are dragged sideways by the magnetic force, piling up at one end. That separation of charge is a voltage, and it lasts as long as the motion does. This is motional emf, and it is Faraday's law in disguise: as the rod moves, the area of the circuit it sweeps out grows, so the flux through that circuit changes.

Given
  • L = 0.25 m, the length of the rod
  • v = 4 m s⁻¹
  • B = 0.2 T, and the rod cuts the lines SQUARELY

Find: The induced emf, in volts

Formula

emf = BLv

Why this one. Cutting the lines squarely means all three are mutually perpendicular, so there is no sine or cosine to apply and the three simply multiply. This is the shortcut form of Faraday's law for a straight conductor; the general ΔΦ ÷ Δt version would need the swept area and the time, which the question does not give directly.

What each symbol means
  • emf = the induced voltage, in volts
  • B = the flux density, in teslas
  • L = the length of rod inside the field, in metres
  • v = the speed at which it moves, in m s⁻¹
The working, line by line
  1. emf = B × L × v
  2. emf = 0.2 × 0.25 × 4
  3. 0.2 × 0.25 = 0.05
  4. emf = 0.05 × 4
  5. emf = 0.2 V
Check it

Check by units, which is the reliable guard here: tesla × metre × (metre per second) works out to volts, so the three quantities were meant to be multiplied. Sense check the size too: a fifth of a volt from a quarter-metre rod at walking pace is a reminder of why real generators use many turns and spin fast, since one rod moving slowly produces very little.

emf = BLv = 0.2 x 0.25 x 4 = 0.2 V. All three quantities simply multiply. It is Faraday's law wearing a different hat: the rod sweeps area at a rate Lv, so the flux changes at BLv, and that rate IS the emf.

9.13mediumReference sheet R.1

The − sign in emf = minus L dI/dt belongs to {{1}} law, which says the induced effect opposes the change that caused it.

Answer:

Lenz's law, and it is conservation of energy in disguise. If the induced effect reinforced the change instead of opposing it, the smallest nudge would grow without limit and make energy from nothing. Nature always fights back.

9.14mediumReference sheet R.1

A 4 H inductor carries 3 A. What energy is stored in it?

  • ✓18 JCorrect. Half of 4 × 9.
  • ✗12 JWrong. That is L times I, forgetting both the half and the square.
  • ✗36 JWrong. That is L I² with the half forgotten.
  • ✗6 JWrong. This halves L times I rather than squaring the current.
  • ✗1.5 JWrong. This squares nothing and halves twice.
Background, so this is not just a formula

An inductor stores energy in the magnetic field it creates, and the field cannot appear instantly: while the current is climbing, the coil induces a back-emf opposing the climb, and the work done against that back-emf is what ends up stored. Open the switch and the field collapses, returning the energy, which is why switching off an inductive load makes a spark.

Given
  • L = 4 H
  • I = 3 A

Find: The energy U stored in the inductor, in joules

Formula

U = ½LI²

Why this one. An inductance and a current given, an energy wanted, and this is the only relation between the three. Notice the family resemblance to the capacitor's U = ½CV²: a half, the device property, and the square of what flows through or sits across it. Remembering one pattern gives you both.

What each symbol means
  • U = the stored energy, in joules
  • L = the inductance, in henries
  • I = the current, in amperes, and it is SQUARED
The working, line by line
  1. U = ½ × L × I²
  2. I² = 3² = 9
  3. U = ½ × 4 × 9
  4. ½ × 4 = 2
  5. U = 2 × 9 = 18 J
Check it

The option 12 J is what you get by forgetting the square and computing ½ × 4 × 3 × 2, and 36 J is what you get by forgetting the half. Both wrong routes are in the list, so make sure both the half and the square survived. Guard by proportion: triple the current and the energy would rise ninefold, not threefold.

W = one half L I² = 0.5 x 4 x 9 = 18 J. The CURRENT is squared and the inductance is not. It mirrors one half C V² for a capacitor, and the half is there for the same reason: the current climbed from zero rather than sitting at 3 A throughout.

9.15mediumReference sheet R.4

Eddy currents are loops of current induced inside a solid conductor by changing flux, and laminating a transformer core reduces them.

Answer: true

True on both. A solid block of metal is full of possible loops, so changing flux sets swirling currents going inside it, wasting energy as heat. Building the core from thin insulated sheets breaks up the paths those eddies would take, which is why transformer cores are laminated rather than solid.

9.16mediumReference sheet R.1

For a conductor moving through a magnetic field the induced emf is BLv, where B is the flux density, L is the length of the conductor and v is its {{1}}.

Answer:

Speed. Three quantities, all multiplied, no squares and no constants: park the rod and v is zero, so the emf is zero, which is Faraday's law again. Standing still through the strongest field on earth induces nothing.

9.17mediumLecture slides, module 4, slide 104

A coil of 200 turns experiences a change in magnetic flux from 0.08 Wb to 0.02 Wb in 0.10 s. Calculate the induced emf.

  • ✓120 VCorrect. 200 turns times a rate of 0.6 Wb per second.
  • ✗12 VWrong by a factor of ten in the division by 0.10 s.
  • ✗0.6 VWrong. 0.6 V is the rate of change of flux, before multiplying by the 200 turns.
  • ✗200 VWrong. 200 is the number of turns, not a voltage.
  • ✗20 VWrong. This uses a flux change of 0.01 Wb rather than 0.06 Wb.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Faraday's law is about the RATE at which flux changes, not about how much flux there is. A coil sitting in the strongest field in the world produces nothing if nothing is changing. What is more, every turn of the coil experiences the same change, and their emfs add in series, so a coil of 200 turns produces 200 times the emf of a single loop. That is the whole reason coils are wound rather than left as single rings.

Given
  • N = 200 turns
  • Flux falls from Φ₁ = 0.08 Wb to Φ₂ = 0.02 Wb
  • Δt = 0.10 s

Find: The magnitude of the induced emf, in volts

Formula

emf =N ΔΦΔt

Why this one. A flux change, a time and a number of turns are exactly the three quantities Faraday's law needs. The minus sign belongs to Lenz's law and states the direction, meaning the induced effect opposes the change that caused it; since the question asks only for the size, work with the magnitude of the change.

What each symbol means
  • emf = the induced voltage, in volts
  • N = the number of turns, which MULTIPLIES the result
  • ΔΦ = the change in flux, in webers, taken as final minus initial
  • Δt = the time over which the change happens, in seconds
The working, line by line
  1. Change in flux: ΔΦ = 0.08 − 0.02 = 0.06 Wb
  2. Rate of change: ΔΦ ÷ Δt = 0.06 ÷ 0.10 = 0.6 Wb s⁻¹
  3. Multiply by the turns: emf = 200 × 0.6
  4. emf = 120 V
Check it

The option 0.6 V is the rate of change before multiplying by the turns, which is the commonest omission, and it is sitting there waiting. Ask two questions of every Faraday problem: did I divide by the TIME, and did I multiply by the TURNS. Note also that a modest flux change of 0.06 Wb yields 120 V purely because of the 200 turns, which is exactly how a real generator reaches useful voltages.

Faraday's law, size first. The CHANGE in flux is 0.08 − 0.02 = 0.06 Wb, and it happens in 0.10 s, so the rate of change is 0.06 / 0.10 = 0.6 Wb per second. Multiply by the 200 turns: emf = N x rate = 200 x 0.6 = 120 V. Two traps live here. The flux VALUES do not go into the formula, only their difference. And the number of turns multiplies, because each turn is its own loop cut by the same changing flux.

9.18hardReference sheet R.1

The self-induced emf in an inductor is emf = minus L dI/dt. What does the − sign express?

  • ✓Lenz's law: the induced effect opposes the change that produced itCorrect, and the same − sign appears in Faraday's law for the same reason.
  • ✗That the current always flows backwards through an inductorWrong. The current flows whichever way the circuit drives it.
  • ✗That the inductance L is a negative quantityWrong. Inductance is always positive.
  • ✗That energy is lost as heatWrong. Heat loss is resistance, not inductance.
  • ✗Nothing physical; it is a sign convention onlyWrong. It carries the physics, and dropping it would permit perpetual motion.

Lenz's law, and it is conservation of energy wearing a − sign. If the induced effect REINFORCED the change instead of opposing it, the smallest disturbance would grow without limit and make energy from nothing. Nature always fights back: try to increase the current and the inductor pushes against you, which is why a current cannot change instantly in an inductor.

9.19hardReference sheet R.1

What does Lenz’s law say, and which conservation principle is behind it?

  • ✓The induced effect opposes the change that produced it, and it follows from conservation of energyCorrect on both.
  • ✗The induced effect reinforces the change that produced it, and it follows from conservation of chargeWrong. Reinforcement would create energy from nothing.
  • ✗The induced current is always clockwise, by conventionWrong. The direction depends on the change, not on a convention.
  • ✗The induced emf equals the flux rather than its rate of changeWrong. It is the rate of change that matters.
  • ✗It applies only to transformersWrong. It applies to every induced emf.

Lenz opposes. The − sign in emf = minus N dΦ/dt is Lenz’s law, and the reason is conservation of energy: if the induced effect reinforced the change instead, the smallest disturbance would grow without limit and produce energy from nothing. Nature always fights back.

10Transformers

Speed tip. Step the voltage up and the current steps down. Power in equals power out if it is ideal.

10.1mediumReference sheet R.1

A transformer has 100 turns on the primary and 1000 on the secondary, with 240 V applied to the primary. Is it a step up or a step down, and what is the secondary voltage?

  • ✓Step up, 2400 VCorrect. Ten times the turns gives ten times the voltage.
  • ✗Step down, 24 VWrong direction. More secondary turns means MORE voltage, not less.
  • ✗Step up, 240 VWrong. That is the primary voltage unchanged.
  • ✗Step down, 2.4 VWrong on both direction and size.
  • ✗Step up, 24000 VWrong by a factor of ten, from a turns ratio of 100.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A transformer trades voltage against current using only the ratio of turns on its two coils. The alternating current in the primary makes a changing flux in the iron core, the core carries that flux to the secondary, and each secondary turn picks up an equal share. More turns therefore means more volts. Nothing is created: what the voltage gains, the current gives up.

Given
  • Np = 100 turns on the primary
  • Ns = 1000 turns on the secondary
  • Ep = 240 V applied to the primary

Find: Whether it steps up or down, and the secondary voltage Es

Formula

Es / Ep = Ns / Np

Why this one. The turns ratio and one voltage are given and the other voltage is wanted. Read the ratio before calculating: Ns is ten times Np, so this is a step UP and the answer must exceed 240 V. That reading alone eliminates most of the options.

What each symbol means
  • Es = the secondary or output voltage, in volts
  • Ep = the primary or input voltage, in volts
  • Ns = turns on the secondary coil
  • Np = turns on the primary coil
The working, line by line
  1. Turns ratio: Ns ÷ Np = 1000 ÷ 100 = 10
  2. More turns on the secondary, so it STEPS UP
  3. Es = Ep × 10
  4. Es = 240 × 10
  5. Es = 2400 V
Check it

Decide the direction first and the arithmetic becomes a formality: step up rules out both step-down options immediately, and among what remains the ratio is 10, not 1 and not 100. Remember that stepping the voltage up steps the current DOWN by the same factor, so this transformer would deliver a tenth of the primary current, since power cannot be manufactured by winding wire.

The voltage ratio equals the turns ratio: Vs over Vp = Ns over Np = 1000 over 100 = 10. So Vs = 240 x 10 = 2400 V. Decide step up or step down FIRST, from whether Ns exceeds Np, and three of the five options disappear before any arithmetic. Test 2 question 15 is exactly this.

10.2mediumThe real test 2, question 15

In one type of transformer, Ns is greater than Np. If Ns = 1000, Np = 100 and Ep = 240 V, what is the value of Es?

  • ✓2400 VCorrect. Es = Ep x Ns/Np = 240 x 10 = 2400 V.
  • ✗24 VWrong, and it steps DOWN by ten, which contradicts Ns being greater than Np.
  • ✗240 VWrong. This is the primary voltage unchanged, which would need Ns = Np.
  • ✗2.4 VWrong. This steps down by a hundred, the opposite of what the stem describes.
  • ✗24000 VWrong. This steps up by a hundred, using the turns as 1000 and 10.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A transformer trades voltage against current using nothing but the number of turns on each coil. The primary's alternating current makes a changing flux in the iron core, the core carries that flux to the secondary, and every turn on the secondary picks up the same share of it. More turns therefore means more volts. Nothing is gained: whatever the voltage goes up by, the current comes down by, because power in equals power out in the ideal case.

Given
  • Ns = 1000 turns (secondary)
  • Np = 100 turns (primary)
  • Ep = 240 V (primary voltage)

Find: The secondary voltage Es, in volts

Formula

Es / Ep = Ns / Np

Why this one. The turns ratio and one voltage are given and the other voltage is wanted, which is exactly what this relation provides. The question also tells you which way the answer must go before any arithmetic: Ns is greater than Np, so this is a STEP-UP transformer and the secondary voltage must be larger than 240 V.

What each symbol means
  • Es = the secondary or output voltage, in volts
  • Ep = the primary or input voltage, in volts
  • Ns = the number of turns on the secondary coil
  • Np = the number of turns on the primary coil
The working, line by line
  1. Rearrange: Es = Ep × (Ns ÷ Np)
  2. Turns ratio: Ns ÷ Np = 1000 ÷ 100 = 10
  3. Es = 240 × 10
  4. Es = 2400 V
Check it

Decide step-up or step-down BEFORE calculating and half the options die immediately. Ns is bigger than Np, so the answer must be bigger than 240 V, which eliminates 24 V, 240 V and 2.4 V in one stroke. That leaves only 2400 V and 24000 V, and the ratio is 10, not 100.

The turns ratio equals the voltage ratio: Es/Ep = Ns/Np. So Es = Ep x (Ns/Np) = 240 x (1000/100) = 240 x 10 = 2400 V. The stem tells you Ns is greater than Np, which means this is a STEP-UP transformer, so the answer must be larger than 240 V. That single check eliminates three of the five options before any arithmetic.

10.3mediumReference sheet R.1

In a transformer the voltage ratio equals the turns ratio, Vs over Vp = Ns over Np, and it works on {{1}} only.

Answer:

AC only, because a transformer needs a CHANGING flux to induce anything in the secondary. Deciding step up or step down before any arithmetic, simply from whether Ns exceeds Np, eliminates most of the options on a multiple-choice paper.

10.4mediumReference sheet R.4

The two coils of a transformer are connected to each other electrically.

Answer: false

They are not. The coils are coupled by MUTUAL INDUCTION through the shared core: the changing flux from the primary induces an emf in the secondary without any conducting path between them. That electrical isolation is a feature rather than an accident, and it is why transformers are used to separate circuits as well as to change voltage.

10.5hardReference sheet R.4

A transformer is two coils coupled by mutual induction. Why does it work on AC but not on DC?

  • ✓Because it needs a CHANGING flux to induce anything, and a steady direct current gives a steady fluxCorrect, and it is the same sentence that governs every induction question on the paper.
  • ✗Because DC would melt the windingsWrong. Heating is not the reason, and a transformer on DC simply does nothing.
  • ✗Because the coils are only connected electrically on ACWrong. The coils are never electrically connected; that is the point of a transformer.
  • ✗Because DC has no voltageWrong. DC certainly has a voltage.
  • ✗Because iron cores only magnetise under ACWrong. Iron magnetises perfectly well under DC.

Faraday's law again: emf = minus N dΦ/dt, so it is the RATE OF CHANGE of flux that induces, not the flux itself. An alternating primary current makes a constantly changing flux in the core, which the secondary responds to. A steady direct current makes a steady flux, its rate of change is zero, and the secondary sees nothing at all. Note the two coils are never connected electrically in either case; the core carries the flux between them.

10.6hardThe real test 2, question 6

A 240 V step-down transformer is used to light up eight 12 V, 24 W lamps and draws a current of 1 A in the primary. Calculate its efficiency.

  • ✓80 %Correct. Output 8 x 24 = 192 W over input 240 x 1 = 240 W, giving 80 per cent.
  • ✗60 %Wrong. This would need an output of 144 W, that is six lamps rather than eight.
  • ✗40 %Wrong. This would need an output of 96 W, that is four lamps.
  • ✗20 %Wrong. This uses one lamp only: 24/240 wrongly scaled.
  • ✗0 %Wrong. A transformer lighting eight lamps is plainly transferring power, so zero cannot be right.
Background, so this is not just a formula

A transformer is judged by how much of the power fed in actually comes out the other side. Nothing is perfect: some energy heats the core through eddy currents, some is lost reversing the magnetisation, some warms the windings. Efficiency is simply the fraction that survives the trip, written as a percentage. Note that a transformer changes voltage and current but can never create power.

Given
  • Primary voltage Vp = 240 V
  • Primary current Ip = 1 A
  • Eight lamps, each rated 12 V and 24 W

Find: The efficiency, as a percentage

Formula

efficiency =PoutPin

Why this one. Both powers can be built from what is given, so no transformer turns ratio is needed at all. The lamp voltage of 12 V is a distractor here: the lamps are rated 24 W each, and a wattage is already a power, so it can be used directly without touching the voltage.

What each symbol means
  • Pout = the total power the lamps actually consume, in watts
  • Pin = VpIp, what the mains delivers to the primary, in watts
  • Vp, Ip = the primary voltage and current, in volts and amperes
  • efficiency = the ratio of the two, multiplied by 100 to make a percentage
The working, line by line
  1. Output power = 8 lamps × 24 W each
  2. Output power = 192 W
  3. Input power = Vp × Ip = 240 × 1
  4. Input power = 240 W
  5. efficiency = 192 ÷ 240 = 0.8
  6. As a percentage: 0.8 × 100 = 80 %
Check it

Efficiency can never exceed 100 %, because a transformer has no source of energy of its own. If your fraction comes out above 1, you divided input by output instead of output by input. Here 192 is comfortably less than 240, so the answer is safely under 100 %.

Efficiency is output power over input power. Output: eight lamps at 24 W each is 8 x 24 = 192 W. Input: the primary side, P = VI = 240 x 1 = 240 W. So efficiency = 192/240 = 0.8, that is 80 per cent. The 12 V rating of each lamp is not needed, because the wattage already states the power each one consumes.

11Maxwell and Electromagnetic Waves

Speed tip. In a vacuum everything is c = 3 × 10⁸. E/B = c for a plane wave, and c = fλ ties frequency to wavelength.

11.1mediumLecture slides, module 5, slide 36

A radio transmitter produces an electromagnetic wave with an electric field amplitude of 0.080 V/m. What is the corresponding magnetic field amplitude?

  • ✓2.67 x 10-10 TCorrect. 0.080 divided by 3.0 x 108.
  • ✗2.4 x 107 TWrong. This MULTIPLIES by c instead of dividing, which is the commonest slip here.
  • ✗2.67 x 10-8 TWrong by a factor of 100 in the exponent.
  • ✗0.080 TWrong. That is the electric amplitude repeated, in the wrong unit.
  • ✗3.0 x 108 TWrong. That is the speed of light itself, labelled as a field.
Background, so this is not just a formula

An electromagnetic wave carries an electric field and a magnetic field locked together, at right angles to each other and to the direction of travel. They rise and fall in step, and their sizes are not independent: the ratio of the two is always the speed of light. That is why the magnetic part of light is so small a number, and why light is usually described by its electric field alone.

Given
  • E₀ = 0.080 V m⁻¹, the electric field amplitude
  • c = 3.0 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹

Find: The magnetic field amplitude B₀, in teslas

Formula

E₀ / B₀ = c, so B₀ = E₀ / c

Why this one. For any plane wave in free space the two amplitudes are tied by the speed of light, so one gives the other immediately with no wavelength, frequency or power needed. Dividing by such a large number is what makes B₀ tiny, which is the physical point rather than an arithmetic accident.

What each symbol means
  • E₀ = the electric field amplitude, in V m⁻¹
  • B₀ = the magnetic field amplitude, in teslas
  • c = the speed of light in vacuum, 3.0 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹
The working, line by line
  1. B₀ = E₀ ÷ c
  2. B₀ = 0.080 ÷ (3.0 × 10⁸)
  3. Write the top in standard form: 0.080 = 8.0 × 10⁻²
  4. Front numbers: 8.0 ÷ 3.0 = 2.67
  5. Powers of ten: 10⁻² ÷ 10⁸ = 10⁻¹⁰
  6. B₀ = 2.67 × 10⁻¹⁰ T
Check it

Multiplying instead of dividing gives 2.4 × 10⁷ T, which is offered as an option and is physically absurd: the strongest laboratory magnets manage a few tens of teslas. Whenever you divide by c the answer must come out very SMALL, and whenever you multiply by c it must come out very large. That single habit sorts this whole family of questions.

In an electromagnetic wave the two fields are locked together by E = cB, so B = E over c = 0.080 / 3.0 x 108 = 2.67 x 10-10 T. The magnetic amplitude always comes out tiny beside the electric one, by a factor of the speed of light, which is why radio work quotes E rather than B.

11.2mediumLecture slides, module 5, slide 43

A laser pointer emits 5.0 mW of power in a beam with cross-sectional area 3.0 x 10-6 m². Calculate the intensity.

  • ✓1667 W/m2Correct. 5.0 x 10-3 divided by 3.0 x 10-6.
  • ✗1.5 x 10-8 W/m2Wrong. This MULTIPLIES the power by the area instead of dividing.
  • ✗1.667 W/m2Wrong by a factor of 1000, from leaving the power in milliwatts.
  • ✗6.0 x 10-4 W/m2Wrong. No route to this from these numbers.
  • ✗1000 W/m2Wrong. 1000 W/m² is the intensity of SUNLIGHT, the figure being compared against.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Intensity is power spread over area, which is why a laser feels dangerous and a light bulb of the same wattage does not. The bulb throws its power over a whole room; the laser concentrates it into a spot a few millimetres across. Same watts, wildly different intensity, and it is the intensity that does the damage.

Given
  • P = 5.0 mW = 5.0 × 10⁻³ W
  • A = 3.0 × 10⁻⁶ m²

Find: The intensity I, in W m⁻²

Formula

I =PA

Why this one. Intensity is defined as power per unit area, so this is the definition rather than a derived result. The only real work is the conversion of milliwatts to watts, which must happen before the division.

What each symbol means
  • I = the intensity, in watts per square metre
  • P = the power, in watts
  • A = the cross-sectional area of the beam, in m²
The working, line by line
  1. Convert the power: 5.0 mW = 5.0 × 10⁻³ W
  2. I = P ÷ A
  3. I = (5.0 × 10⁻³) ÷ (3.0 × 10⁻⁶)
  4. Front numbers: 5.0 ÷ 3.0 = 1.667
  5. Powers of ten: 10⁻³ ÷ 10⁻⁶ = 10³
  6. I = 1.667 × 10³ = 1667 W m⁻²
Check it

The option 1.667 is what you get by leaving the power in milliwatts, and it is the one most people pick. Feel the size of the answer: 1667 W m⁻² is above the roughly 1000 W m⁻² of full sunlight, from a 5 mW pointer, which is precisely why you must never aim one at an eye. If your answer had come out around 1 W m⁻², the conversion did not happen.

Intensity is power per unit area: I = P over A = 5.0 x 10-3 / 3.0 x 10-6 = 1667 W/m². The interpretation the slide draws is worth keeping: despite a tiny power of only 5 mW, this beats the roughly 1000 W per square metre of full sunlight, because the energy is concentrated into a very small area. That is why a laser pointer is dangerous to the eye and a 5 mW lamp is not.

11.3mediumLecture slides, module 5, slide 32

Select the properties of electromagnetic waves the slides list.

  • ✓Transverse: E and B oscillate perpendicular to the direction of propagationCorrect, transverse.
  • ✓In vacuum they all travel at c = 3 x 108 m/sCorrect, and it is the same c for every part of the spectrum.
  • ✓They need no medium, unlike soundCorrect, and it is the property that distinguishes them from sound.
  • ✓E and B are linked at every point by E = cBCorrect, which is what makes B tiny compared with E.
  • ✓They carry energy, with the flux given by the Poynting vectorCorrect, and the Poynting vector S = one over μ₀, times E cross B.
  • ✗They travel faster through a dense medium than through vacuumWrong. Nothing exceeds c, and a medium slows light rather than speeding it.

Five properties, and the sixth option is false in a way worth knowing: nothing outruns c, and light in fact SLOWS in a medium such as glass or water. Note also that all electromagnetic waves share the same speed in vacuum, from radio at the long end of the spectrum to gamma rays at the short one, and only their frequency and wavelength differ, tied together by c = f λ.

11.4hardLecture slides, module 5, slide 40

In an electromagnetic wave the electric and magnetic contributions to the energy density are equal.

Answer: true

True, and it follows from E = cB. The energy density is u = one half ε₀ E² plus B² over 2 μ₀. Substituting B = E/c and c = 1 over the square root of μ₀ ε₀ makes the second term identical to the first, so the energy is shared exactly evenly between the two fields and u = ε₀ E² = B² over μ₀. The magnetic amplitude LOOKS tiny beside the electric one, but it carries just as much energy.

11.5hardLecture slides, module 5, slide 33

In Ey = E-nought sin(kx − omega t), the wave number is k = 2 π over {{1}} and the angular frequency is omega = 2 π f.

Answer:

λ, the wavelength. Both symbols carry the same 2 π: k packs 2 π into one wavelength of SPACE, omega packs 2 π into one period of TIME. Ey and Bz share the identical sin(kx − omega t), which is why the two fields peak together and vanish together.

11.6hardLecture slides, module 5, slide 33

In the plane wave Ey = E-nought sin(kx − omega t) and Bz = B-nought sin(kx − omega t), the electric field points along y, the magnetic field along z, and the wave travels along x.

Answer: true

True, and the three directions are mutually perpendicular by construction. The subscript on each field names its own direction, and the x inside the bracket names the direction of travel. Hold your thumb, first finger and second finger at right angles: E, B and the direction of travel, never two of them along the same line.

11.7hardLecture slides, module 5, slide 42

The intensity of an electromagnetic wave, I = one half ε₀ c E-nought squared, is measured in watts per square {{1}}.

Answer:

Watts per square metre: power spread over area. The half is there because the field oscillates, so the AVERAGE of the squared sine over a cycle is one half, not one. The same one half turns up in one half C V² and one half L I², always for the same reason.

11.8hardLecture slides, module 5, slide 42

Doubling the peak electric field of a wave doubles its intensity.

Answer: false

It QUADRUPLES it. I = one half ε₀ c E-nought squared, and the field is squared, so twice the field gives four times the intensity. That is why a lamp twice as bright does not feel twice as fierce, and why halving the field cuts the power to a quarter.

11.9hardLecture slides, module 5, slide 41

The Poynting vector S = one over μ₀, times E cross B, gives the rate at which {{1}} is transported per unit area by the wave.

Answer:

Energy. The name is a happy accident: the vector POINTS the way the energy travels, which is the direction of E cross B, at right angles to both fields. Its size is the power crossing each square metre, and averaging it over a cycle is exactly what gives the intensity I.

11.10hardLecture slides, module 5, slide 29

In free space Maxwell's equations give a wave equation whose speed is v = 1 over the square root of μ₀ ε₀. Evaluating it with μ₀ = 4 π x 10-7 and ε₀ = 8.85 x 10-12 gives:

  • ✓2.998 x 108 m/s, which is exactly the speed of lightCorrect, and the agreement with the measured speed of light is the whole point.
  • ✗3.34 x 10-9 m/sWrong. 3.34 x 10-9 is the square root of the product, the number you still have to invert.
  • ✗1.11 x 10-17 m/sWrong. 1.11 x 10-17 is the product itself, before the square root.
  • ✗8.99 x 109 m/sWrong. 8.99 x 109 is Coulomb's constant k, a different combination of the same constants.
  • ✗2.998 x 106 m/sWrong by a hundred in the exponent.
Background, so this is not just a formula

Maxwell's greatest result was not a new experiment but a piece of arithmetic. He combined his four equations, found they predicted a wave, and computed its speed from two constants measured in entirely electrical and magnetic experiments, with no light involved. The number that came out was the measured speed of light, which is how light was discovered to be an electromagnetic wave.

Given
  • μ₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ T m A⁻¹
  • ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹

Find: The wave speed v, in m s⁻¹

Formula

v = 1 / √(μ₀ε₀)

Why this one. The two constants come from measurements with magnets and with capacitors, and neither has anything to do with light, which is what makes the result so striking. Do the product first, then the square root, then the reciprocal; changing that order is the usual source of error.

What each symbol means
  • v = the speed of the electromagnetic wave in free space, in m s⁻¹
  • μ₀ = the permeability of free space, from magnetic measurements
  • ε₀ = the permittivity of free space, from electric measurements
  • c = the accepted symbol once you recognise the answer as the speed of light
The working, line by line
  1. Multiply first: μ₀ε₀ = 4π × 10⁻⁷ × 8.85 × 10⁻¹²
  2. 4π = 12.566, and 12.566 × 8.85 = 111.2
  3. Powers of ten: 10⁻⁷ × 10⁻¹² = 10⁻¹⁹
  4. μ₀ε₀ = 111.2 × 10⁻¹⁹ = 1.112 × 10⁻¹⁷
  5. Square root: √(1.112 × 10⁻¹⁷) = 3.336 × 10⁻⁹
  6. Reciprocal: v = 1 ÷ (3.336 × 10⁻⁹)
  7. v = 2.998 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹, which is the speed of light c
Check it

Taking a square root of a power of ten needs the exponent to be EVEN, so rewrite 1.112 × 10⁻¹⁷ as 11.12 × 10⁻¹⁸ before rooting, and the root is √11.12 × 10⁻⁹ = 3.336 × 10⁻⁹. The option 3.34 × 10⁻⁹ is what you get by stopping before the reciprocal, and 8.99 × 10⁹ is the Coulomb constant wandering in from another topic. You should recognise 3 × 10⁸ on sight, which makes this question answerable in five seconds.

μ₀ times ε₀ = 4 π x 10-7 x 8.85 x 10-12 = 1.113 x 10-17. Its square root is 3.336 x 10-9, and one divided by that is 2.998 x 108 m/s. This is the result the slides call the moment that shook nineteenth-century physics: two constants measured in laboratory experiments about magnets and static charge, with no light involved at all, combine to give the measured speed of light exactly. Maxwell's own conclusion was that we can scarcely avoid the inference that light consists in transverse undulations of the same medium which is the cause of electric and magnetic phenomena.

11.11hardLecture slides, module 5, slide 42

Continuing the laser problem, the intensity is 1667 W/m². Using I = one half ε₀ c E-nought squared, find the electric field amplitude.

  • ✓about 1120 V/mCorrect. The square root of 3334 divided by 2.655 x 10-3.
  • ✗about 1.12 V/mWrong by a factor of 1000, a slip in the exponent before the square root.
  • ✗about 3334 V/mWrong. 3334 is the numerator 2I, not the answer.
  • ✗about 2.655 x 10-3 V/mWrong. 2.655 x 10-3 is the denominator ε₀ c.
  • ✗about 628 V/mWrong. This forgets the factor of 2 when rearranging the one half.
Background, so this is not just a formula

The energy in an electromagnetic wave is carried by its fields, and the intensity is what the fields deliver per second per square metre. The relation involves E₀ SQUARED, so doubling the field quadruples the intensity, which is the same square law that appears in every wave in physics. Going backwards from intensity to field therefore needs a square root.

Given
  • I = 1667 W m⁻², carried over from the previous part
  • ε₀ = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² F m⁻¹
  • c = 3.0 × 10⁸ m s⁻¹

Find: The electric field amplitude E₀, in V m⁻¹

Formula

E₀ = √(2I / (ε₀c))

Why this one. It is I = ½ε₀cE₀² rearranged for E₀. The half moves across as a doubling on top, ε₀c moves to the bottom, and the square comes off as a square root. Do it as one rearranged formula rather than in pieces, because each move is a chance to lose the factor of two.

What each symbol means
  • E₀ = the electric field amplitude, in V m⁻¹
  • I = the intensity, in W m⁻²
  • ε₀ = the permittivity of free space
  • c = the speed of light, in m s⁻¹
  • the 2 = what is left of the half after rearranging
The working, line by line
  1. Bottom first: ε₀ × c = 8.85 × 10⁻¹² × 3.0 × 10⁸
  2. Front numbers: 8.85 × 3.0 = 26.55
  3. Powers of ten: 10⁻¹² × 10⁸ = 10⁻⁴
  4. Bottom = 26.55 × 10⁻⁴ = 2.655 × 10⁻³
  5. Top: 2 × I = 2 × 1667 = 3334
  6. Divide: 3334 ÷ (2.655 × 10⁻³) = 1.2558 × 10⁶
  7. Square root: E₀ = √(1.2558 × 10⁶)
  8. E₀ = 1120 V m⁻¹
Check it

The option 2.655 × 10⁻³ is the value of ε₀c, offered to catch anyone who stopped mid-calculation, and 628 V m⁻¹ is roughly what dropping the factor of 2 would give. Cross-check forwards in one line: ½ × 8.85 × 10⁻¹² × 3 × 10⁸ × 1120² = ½ × 2.655 × 10⁻³ × 1.2544 × 10⁶ = 1665 W m⁻², which is the intensity we started from. Note too that √(1.2558 × 10⁶) is manageable by hand, since 10⁶ roots to 10³ and √1.2558 is about 1.12.

Rearrange in symbols first: E-nought = square root of (2I over ε₀ c). Top: 2 x 1667 = 3334. Bottom: 8.85 x 10-12 x 3.00 x 108 = 2.655 x 10-3. Dividing gives 1.256 x 106, and its square root is about 1120 V/m. The square root at the end is the step to watch, since E-nought appears SQUARED in the intensity formula.
